PREFACE 

THE publication of this book was inspired by an article which appeared in the September, 1935, number of the American 

Ecclesiastical Review. Its title was "The Writing of Parish 

Histories"; its author, Monsignor Peter Guilday.

At that time, I had been pastor of St. Mary's in Jersey City for a little more than two years. From old residents in the district, I learned much of the past glory of St. Mary's, and this was supplemented by stories told by priests of the diocese. 

And when I traveled outside the parish, and was introduced to strangers as the new pastor of St. Mary's in Jersey City, very many of these new-found friends turned out to be former parishioners of St. Mary's, or former students of the Christian 

Brothers at the Catholic Institute. All these confirmed the impressions I had already received. 

There could be no doubt of the immense influence which St. Mary's had exerted throughout the city of Jersey City and the State of New Jersey. This influence was even felt throughout the nation; one of the students of the Catholic Institute was an important figure in the Cabinet of President Woodrow Wilson during the World War. Such influence, I felt, should not go unrecorded, and so I began a search for a man who would patiently unearth and faithfully recount the principal events in the life of this parish. 

My search happily resulted in the choice of the author of this parish history, Mr. John Francis Gough. His grandparents on both sides were early settlers in St. Mary's parish, where four generations of the family have worshiped. He himself attended St. Mary's Catholic Institute (1886), the elementary public schools of the neighborhood (1887-1895), the Jersey 

City High School on Bay Street (1895-1899). At Harvard 

College (1899-1902), he attained his A.B. cum laude. During the year 1899-1900, he studied Catholic Philosophy under the late Rev. William Gaston, S.J., once president of Boston Colxi

xii 

PREFACE 

lege. Mr. Gough has practiced law in Jersey City since 1905. 

Since 1916, he has been a communicant at St. Aloysius Church, Jersey City, but he has always maintained interest in St. Mary's, where he was born, baptized, and confirmed. 

Mr. Gough's recent avocation has been the study of French- 

American relations between 1770 and 1800. In 1934, the 

Government of France conferred upon him the diploma of Officier d'Académie. He has translated from the French Letters of Baron de Vioménil on Polish Affairs, 1771-1772, also Antoine Charles du Houx, Baron de Vioménil, Lieutenant General of the Armies of the King, Second in Command under Rocham beau, by Le Comte de Montmort. 

The author and I express our thanks and appreciation to all who have assisted with material, ideas, and good wishes. Our gratitude is particularly expressed to the Sisters of Charity at Mount St. Vincent in New York, and at Convent in New 

Jersey; to the Sisters of the Poor of St. Francis in Jersey City, Hoboken, and Cincinnati; to the Christian Brothers at Ammendale, Maryland; to librarians and their assistants in the New 

Jersey Historical Society in Newark, and the Free Public 

Libraries in Jersey City, Newark, New York City, Paterson, and Philadelphia. 

Jersey City, New Jersey 

August 16, 1938

INTRODUCTION 

FROM the very earliest times of Christianity, psalms were sung in church processions.¹ And in a sixth century book, the First 

Roman Ordo, we read that "as soon as the candles are lit and everything is ready, the singers come and stand before the altar on either side. Presently, the leader of the choir begins the antiphon for the entrance. As soon as the deacons hear his voice, they go to the Pope, who rises, and comes from the secristy to the altar in procession." This introit psalm on great feast days sounded the keynote, it set the tone of the entire religious celebration.

A fragment of this psalm and antiphon, sung by the early Christians while the celebrant and ministers entered the church and approached the altar, is still preserved in the Catholic Mass. It is called the Introit, and it is sung or read as soon as the priest ascends to the altar platform. As in the beginning, so now, it sounds the keynote of the religious celebration of the day.

An example helps to make this point evident. On December eighth each year, the Catholic Church celebrates one of her great days of joy. It is the feast of the Immaculate Conception of Mary, the Mother of God. What is the Introit of the Mass of that day? The words are taken from the great prophet 

Isaias (61:10). "I will greatly rejoice in the Lord, and my soul shall be joyful in my God: for He hath clothed me with the garments of salvation, and with the robe of justice He hath covered me, as a bride adorned with her jewels."

This high tone of spiritual gladness is maintained throughout the entire Mass. In the Epistle, the Church appropriates the words of King Solomon (Proverbs 8:30): "I was with 

Him, forming all things, and was delighted every day, playing before Him at all times, playing in the world. And my delights were to be with the children of men." In the Gospel are the words of the Angel Gabriel (Luke 1:28): Hail, full of grace,

XV

xvi 

INTRODUCTION 

the Lord is with thee: blessed art thou among women." And toward the end of the Mass, we hear at the Communion the words of King David (Psalms 86:3): Glorious things are said of thee."

Mary indeed was honored by all true Christians. And truly should be honored, this Jewish virgin chosen to be the Mother of God. For to Mary are applied the words addressed to 

Judith of old (Judith 13:23, 15:10): "Blessed art thou, O daughter, by the Lord the most high God, above all women upon the earth. Thou art the glory of Jerusalem, thou art the joy of Israel, thou art the honor of our people."

As an outward expression of this honor, true Christians from the very beginning have reared temples and shrines to Mary. 

In every city, and in most towns, St. Mary's is the outstanding 

Catholic church. Naturally then, when Catholics in Harsimus became sufficiently numerous, they planned and built a church to honor Mary. And the first permanent church they built, they dedicated to the Virgin Most Pure under her title of the Immaculate Conception. That Catholic church, and the Catholic people who form the congregation of that parish church, are the subject of this historical study: "St. Mary's in Jersey City."

CHAPTER I HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT OF LOWER JERSEY CITY 

A.D. 1636-1870 

WHEN St. Mary's parish was erected in 1859, it embraced besides its present area all of lower Jersey City (except St. Peter's parish) north of Railroad Avenue and east of the "hill," which is geologically part of the well-known Palisades. This original parish territory, an area of about four hundred acres, was first known by the name of Harsimus, once owned by the Kennedys, who were nobles of Scotland.2 

Andrew Kennedy (1685-1763) was a Collector-General of the Province of New York. Upon his advice and knowledge in public affairs Benjamin Franklin often relied. He was of great influence and importance in New York and London. Although in full sympathy with the mercantile aims of the British government, he disagreed with its methods in the pre-Revolutionary days, and boldly adopted the opinion that Americans. would not continue in friendship with Britons simply because their great-grandmothers had been acquainted. For one hundred pounds, in 1746, Kennedy acquired Bedloe's Island, now the site of the Statue of Liberty, and for one thousand pounds, in 1755, he sold it to New York, for purposes of quarantine.

He was also the owner of property listed as numbers 1 and 3 

Broadway, in New York City. On this plot, in 1760, he erected a spacious and costly dwelling which was a landmark for years. It was his son, Captain Archibald Kennedy, the eleventh Earl of Cassilis in Scotland, who in 1804, conveyed to John B. Coles the land known as Harsimus, extending from the Hudson River westward to Bergen Hill. On the north, at the present-day Hoboken line, Harsimus was bounded by a creek, and on the south, by what is now known as Newark 3

Avenue. Within Harsimus, however, there was for two centuries a small farm in the possession of the Van Vorst family. 

This farm adjoined Harsimus Cove, between what is now Third and Fifth Streets, and extended westward to the vicinity of the present Jersey Avenue and Second Street.¹

To the south of St. Mary's Parish is the land known of old as Paulus Hook, a British outpost of New York in the 

American Revolution. To the west and southwest are Bergen and Bergen Point (now Bayonne), which were colonial settlements comprising the town of Bergen, originally a Dutch foothold in the ancient province of East Jersey.5 North of the original parish lie three old settlements: Hoboken, long the plantation of the Bayards of New York; Weehawken, where in 1804, at the foot of the cliffs, Alexander 

Hamilton, a victim of the French Revolution, fell wounded by the murderous bullet of the adventurous Aaron Burr-who had been within one vote of being President of the United States; and Fort Lee, named in honor of the English-born 

American Major General Charles Lee, almost a century before his British attachments and concealed duplicity were made quite apparent. Directly to the east of the parish flows in full strength the mighty Hudson, a veritable arm of the Atlantic Ocean.

Here is a harbor of ships-and also of memories. From almost any roof in St. Mary's parish the thoughtful observer sees in upper New York Bay the Statue of Liberty, soft in its halfcentury patina, holding sternly aloft the torch of enlightenment. The statue, a gift of the French Republic, is distant but a short mile from the soaring towers of Manhattan on the east bank of the Hudson, where Henry Hudson, in 1607, beheld only clumps of woods.

At the southern edge of the massed steel and masonry of the metropolis is Battery Park, with its Aquarium. This building is the ancient Castle Garden. This was the first earthly contact with America for millions of brave souls who in the nineteenth century were to build the nation, whether they clung tenaciously to the seaboard, or went boldly to the West.8

HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT 

3 As one gazes upon the lovely waters where river and bay meet, there comes to mind a day in the spring of 1789, and George Washington, on board a gaily decorated barge manned by seamen. They had rowed him from Elizabeth, by way of 

Newark Bay, the Kill von Kull, and the Jersey side of New

York Bay, across the Hudson River and around Battery Park to a landing on the East River, at the foot of Wall Street. A thousand feet or so inland at Wall and Broad Streets, he was to take his oath as first President of the United States which he had so resolutely, in council room and on battlefield, helped establish. 

And looking down upon and beyond the freight cars and signal towers in the Harsimus Yards of the Pennsylvania Railroad, the reflective mind goes back to August, 1642, when, mutilated and serene, Father Isaac Jogues, intrepid Jesuit, ransomed providentially from the Iroquois by Dutch Protestants, sailed past the curved shore-line in the shallow waters of Harsimus Cove, and into the shelter of New Amsterdam, where he healed his body and renewed his courage for a martyrdom in 1646, at 

Auriesville, near Albany, in New York, which justified his

Faith and earned him his place among those sanctified in Christ Jesus.10 

Harsimus, Paulus Hook, Van Vorst Township, and Jersey City

In the story of lower Jersey City there are many outstanding dates. As early as 1636 Cornelson Van Vorst lived at Harsimus, near the water's edge, on what is now Henderson Street, be tween Fourth and Fifth Streets.11 In 1663 Dutch settlers built a trading hut at Paulus Hook.12 On April 5, 1664, Peter

Stuyvesant, Governor of New Netherlands, granted to Cornelson Van Vorst a plot of ground at the head of Harsimus Cove, southwest of the Wagon Road (present-day Railroad Avenue). 

This then became Van Vorst's homestead lot.18 Stuyvesant also conveyed to him a plot of ground to the south of Harsimus, extending to Jan de Locker's Hook, later known as Mill Creek 

Point, near the present Pine and Walnut Streets, in Lafayette.¹4

It was in this year, too, that the government of Holland sur-

ST MARY'S IN JERSEY CITY 

rendered its river settlements to Great Britain, and Charles II of England granted to his brother James, the Duke of York, afterwards King, a province of indefinite extent, lying between the Connecticut and Delaware Rivers.15 This was almost immediately conveyed to Lord John Berkeley and Sir George

Cartaret, who named it New Jersey, after the Isle of Jersey, which Carteret, a staunch friend of the Stuarts, had ruled.16 

For prospective settlers they prepared and published concessions, which included full guarantees of civil and religious liberty. 17 Four years later Governor Carteret granted a charter to the County of Bergen, then running north and south from Bergen Point to the New York State boundary line, which at that time had not been definitely fixed.18 On the west, too, the county was of undefined extent. In this same year Carteret confirmed to Van Vorst the grant of land which lay south of Harsimus, and included the present Lafayette.19 In 1675, the first County of Bergen was legally established.20 The next year, the province was divided into East and West Jersey.' 21 In 1682 William Penn and other Quakers who had settled 

Pennsylvania,22 after interesting themselves in West Jersey, purchased East Jersey from the trustees of Carteret, who had meanwhile died.28 In 1693 the County of Bergen was enlarged to include all territory in the present Counties of Hudson, Bergen, and Passaic.24 In 1698 the second Van Vorst purchased

Paulus Hook from Abraham Isaacsen Verplank.25 This land was practically an island, and lay to the east of what is now 

Warren Street.26

In 1702 all the powers of government, then held by several conflicting interests, were surrendered to the English Crown,27 and a commission to govern East and West Jersey was granted by King William III to Edward Hyde, a dissipated bigot,28 generally known as Lord Cornbury. He was a first cousin of 

Queen Anne. By appropriate ordinance Cornbury laid the first outlines of provisional policy for the pre-Revolutionary period (1702-1776), a plan of government marked by disabilities against Catholics. These persisted even when New Jersey

HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT 

5 adopted its first Constitution, in 1776, and they were not legally removed until the present Constitution was adopted, in 1844.29 

It was in 1724 that Andrew Kennedy secured from the English Proprietors the title to the West India Company's Farmalso known as the Duke's Farm30-which was practically Harsimus itself. It contained about four hundred acres, of which, however, two small parcels-one of twenty acres and one of six acres were owned by the Van Vorsts and others. Numerous disputes as to the boundaries and ownership between the Kennedy and Van Vorst families were taken into the courts of New Jersey between 1753 and 1776, but they were not settled judicially.31 In 1804 claimants on both sides executed deeds to John B. Coles, who for $32,000 obtained title to land in what now comprises all of lower Jersey City west of the Hudson River, south of Hoboken, east of the Hill, and north of the line running from the present Henderson Street and Railroad Avenue, westerly to the neighborhood of Railroad Avenue and Monmouth Street. The filed map of purchase, rough and not over instructive, was made by Joseph F. Mangin.

In the deeds the property is described by causeways, coves, creeks, ditches, marshes, bridges, mills, gutts, fences, posts, stakes, meadows, uplands, and north and middle-house chimneys. These traces have been long lost to memory. 32 The Van Vorsts cultivated their farm lands for generations, and until the modern city grew up, they ruled their households in Old Country style.33 About the time of the American Revolution the Duke's Farm, or Harsimus, with its five or six families, was considered a small village. Van Vorst's larger farm lay south of the present Railroad Avenue, which was later made the boundary between the Van Vorst and the Coles property.34 

General Nathaniel Greene in a report of September 5, 1776, mentions but one house at Paulus Hook and another at Hoboken. From a hill top near the site of the present Dickinson 

High School, at Newark and Palisade Avenues, on September 

22, 1776, General Greene watched the floating British batteries being brought into the Harsimus Cove, near the present Hen-

derson Street at Newark Avenue (a causeway then, and now a road leading to Newark). He saw them destroy the rebels' fortifications in Paulus Hook (in the neighborhood of presentday Grand and Washington Streets), though the Americans had rendered them useless before they withdrew to Bergen Hill a few hours earlier.3 35

There is a glimpse of old conditions in the prospectus of the Associates of Jersey City, issued in 1804, when the development of Paulus Hook (later, Jersey City) was begun in earnest. 

There were then within it, one dwelling house, a ferry house, and a tavern.36 The tide, unless obstructed, came in through a creek which ran along what is now the north side of Montgomery Street, across Warren Street, and along Newark Avenue, and emptied into Harsimus Cove, near Henderson and First 

Streets. Here was a landing place for boats of good size, to which local fishermen used to bring their catch of North River shad.37

Until well after the turn of the century (1802), farming was still the main occupation in land that is now lower Jersey City. In 1815, Isaac Edge's grist mill was erected on the present Montgomery Street, West of Greene Street. In 1818, the 

Long Dock, thirteen hundred feet long (now Pavonia Avenue), was built, extending to the deep water of the Hudson River. 

In 1824 glass factories, in 1825 pottery works, and in 1827 crucible foundries were established.38 The slow growth of the community is shown by the slight increase of the population of Harsimus between 1837 and 1840, when the number of inhabitants there increased from 923 to only 1057.39 In the late 

1830's and early 1840's the railroads built their first terminals and shops, and the opening of the Erie Canal made New York 

City the most important port on the Atlantic seaboard.40 Laborers looking for work and convenient homes, began to settle in what is now called Jersey City.41

In 1851, the descendants of John B. Coles (who died in 

1825) still owned all Harsimus except fifty acres, and about that time, the township received approximately $100,000 in return for lands along the water front. A capable observer of

HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT 

7 the period¹² described Harsimus as originally two-thirds salt meadows, through which ran a creek. In these meadows were numerous salt ponds. Some of the streets and avenues had been improved at public expense, but by and large, the property was still used as a meadow. Most of the streets indicated on Mangin's map of 1804 had not yet been opened, or even marked on the ground. Those that crossed the meadow would have to be filled up at great expense. Many lots needed filling, and others would have to be cut down considerably. The taxing authorities assessed the holdings of the Coles at a million dollars, the equivalent of the sum of the prices asked for single lots; but the agent for the owners thought the property would not bring $400,000. The actual rent received that year for all the property was $1515, of which $900 was the income from four brick houses, and the balance, $615, came from the rent of vacant lots. In that year the budget of Jersey City was $41,- 

530.98, exclusive of $4500 to be raised by a poll tax upon voters. Two years previously, in Van Vorst Township, which later corresponded roughly to St. Mary's Parish, $1400 was collected for the use of the public school, and to this amount the state added $100. At this school-probably conducted by Isaac 

Coriell on part of the site of the present St. Mary's Instituteabout one hundred pupils were taught free of charge, while others received instruction at reduced prices. In the whole city, the property of religious societies, consisting of churches and the lots on which they were built, was valued at $100,000. 

In those days the Catholics of Hoboken, in order to reach St. 

Peter's on Grand Street in Jersey City, journeyed in a semicircle halfway up the hillside, both going and returning, to pass around the cove that then separated Hoboken from Jersey 

City 48 43 Primitive conditions prevailed in the lower city. Before 1845 there were no public street lamps. As late as 1851 the only available water for family use was obtained from three pumps -one at Newark Avenue and Bay Street, another at First and 

Erie Streets, and the third at Grove and Fifteenth Streets. Gas was first used in houses on December 1, 1851, and in street

lamps three days later. The names of streets were not put on the lamps until June, 1861.

The first Hudson County Court House, dedicated on March 11, 1845, was built of trap-rock taken out of the ground on which the edifice was erected. St. Joseph's Church, in the same neighborhood (1867-1870), and St. Patrick's Church (18701877), were built of similar stone taken from near-by points.¹4 

It was on April 16, 1836, that Paulus Hook became a municipality quite independent of the old town of Bergen. In 

March, 1839, the westerly boundary of Jersey City—the new name of Paulus Hook-was extended to Grove Street on the west, and First Street on the north. The lowland territory west and north of Paulus Hook-that is to say, Harsimus, now the lower city north of Newark Avenue-remained part of Bergen 

Township until March 11, 1841. Then by an act of the Legislature it was named Van Vorst Township, a title it retained until 1851, when it became part of the enlarged city of Jersey City. The combined population numbered about 12,000. In 

Van Vorst Township the referendum vote stood, 377 in favor of consolidation, to 49 against; and in Jersey City (old Paulus 

Hook), 489 votes were in favor and 3 against the measure.

The present Jersey City was not established until 1870, when, upon referendum, Hudson City and Bergen City voted to consolidate with the whole lower city. 

The population of the city increased from about 12,000 in 1850 to 29,226, in 1860. A picture of the water front in 1866 shows a well-built city with many flourishing enterprises.15 

Among these were glass factories, foundries, locomotive works, a trunk manufactory, a fireworks factory, soap factory, jewelry factory, dry docks, a railroad depot, ferry slips, the piers of the Cunard line, and several hotels. Outstanding, though in the background, is the present St. Peter's Church, at Van Vorst and Grand Streets, which was at that time nearing completion.

JERSEY CITY AND ENVIRONS, 1859

CHAPTER II EARLY CATHOLIC HISTORY 

A.D. 1804-1860 

OLD St. Peter's Church in New York City, established in 1785, at Barclay and Church Streets, is the mother church of Catholic 

New York and northern New Jersey. Aside from the labors of its early priests, it owes its origin to a small group of pious Catholics, mainly of Irish descent, and to the encouragement of 

Spanish and French diplomats resident in New York. The most energetic spirit among them was an early French consul, Hector St. John de Crevecour, who was, strange to say, not a very strong Catholic, if one at all. Aid came from many sources: the Corporation of Trinity Church (Episcopal), New 

York, practically assured the site; the interest of friends and relatives of St. Peter's congregation in the old countries was proved by substantial contributions; the subscriptions of non- 

Catholic officials helped; and wealthy Spanish, French, and 

Portuguese residents gave generously. One noteworthy gift, in 

1792, was of a thousand dollars from the Bishop and clergy of the ancient diocese of Los Angeles, then in Spanish territory. 

In its early days, St. Peter's in New York City suffered the trials of dissension, both among priests and laity, and during the 1830's, of suspicion from without, for those were the years of the "Know Nothing" party. Its history, nevertheless, is the unfolding of the progress of the Catholic Church in New York City and adjacent New Jersey. In 1785 there were two hundred 

Catholics in the original congregation of St. Peter's; today its filial churches exceed three thousand, with congregations of more than 4,500,000, who maintain religious orders, seminaries, schools, hospitals, asylums, charitable agencies, and other activities¹-a manifestation of the realization of the principles of religious freedom maintained by the leaders in the American

ΙΟ 

Revolution, and particularly by Washington, Franklin, Jefferson, Madison, Lafayette, and Carroll.2 

St. Peter's, Jersey City

In the early days of Paulus Hook (the southeastern part of lower Jersey City), the Catholic families, who were employed there in the glass and pottery works, attended Mass at St. 

Peter's Church, on Barclay Street, or at old St. Patrick's Cathedral, on Mott Street, in New York City. In the late 1820's, a committee headed by Bernard McQuaid-whose son was later the first Bishop of Rochester-interested the factory owners, George and Phenice Dummell and David Henderson, in securing a church site in Jersey territory for these neighborhood Catholics. The Associates of the Jersey Land Company donated four city lots on Grand Street, west of Washington Street. So moved was Most Rev. John Dubois, Bishop of New York, by this gift, that he immediately established a mission. Probably for the first time in Hudson County, Mass was celebrated on the last Sunday of November, 1829, at 52 Sussex Street, Jersey

City. Subsequently, other houses, all since demolished, were used: 51 Sussex Street, 43 Morris Street, 163 Washington

Street, 426 Grove Street, and 89 Railroad Avenue. The congregation increased rapidly, and eventually used the Town Hall at 110-112 Sussex Street. (This had also been the first edifice occupied by the Episcopalians of the city.) The plot on 

Grand Street was accepted by Rev. William Byrnes, the first pastor of St. Peter's Church in Jersey City, on March 10, 1831. 

Parishioners from old St. Peter's Church on Barclay Street in 

New York helped to dig the foundation, but it was not until 1837 that a structure was begun. Then, for lack of adequate piling, the building collapsed. It was only through the generous aid of Catholics at Sing-Sing, New York, and Boston, and of non-Catholics in Jersey City, that the first edifice was completed. There is an uncertain indication that Mass was celebrated on Christmas Day, 1835, when the building was nothing more than bare walls. Among the early priests at St. Peter's in 

Jersey City were Fathers Bernard O'Reilly, later Bishop of

EARLY CATHOLIC HISTORY 

II 

Hartford, Walter Quartier, Patrick Kenny, John Rogers, a zealous pastor in Middlesex County, and John Kelly, an outstanding public figure and pioneer priest of Hudson County for many years.

The Reverend John Kelly 

Rev. John Kelly was born in the year 1805, at Trillick, in the parish of Kiliskerry, County Tyrone, Ireland. Blessed with an amiable mother, his earnest youthful piety became the manhood charm which marked all his good works. As a boy, he taught catechism, led in prayer, made pilgrimages, and visited the sick. He came to America at the age of twenty, and was admitted in 1826 to Mount St. Mary's at Emmitsburg, Maryland. 

In 1828 he joined the Jesuits at Frederick, Maryland, but his health failed, and he returned to Mount St. Mary's. Bishop 

Dubois ordained him, September 14, 1833. He was first a curate at old St. Patrick's Cathedral, on Mott Street. Later, in charge of a district in northeastern New York half as large as Ireland, he celebrated the first Mass in Saratoga. In 1841 he was sent as a missionary to Liberia, in Africa, to encourage freed Negroes sent there from the United States. For three years he served them valiantly. Then his health failed again.

He was carried on shipboard apparently dying, but on the voyage to America he recovered.

On November 12, 1844, he was appointed pastor of St. 

Peter's in Jersey City, then a parish of five hundred souls. 

During the next twenty-two years, as the circle of his flock grew, he built churches and laid the foundations of parishes in 

Bergen Point, Greenville, Hudson City, Hoboken, and Jersey

City. He died on April 28, 1866. His grave is marked by an ornate tomb in the old Catholic Cemetery on Tonnele Avenue, in Jersey City, the resting place of early Catholics of Hudson County. To his memory is dedicated St. Paul's window (now hidden in part by a chancel organ) in the north transept of St. 

Patrick's Cathedral, on Fifth Avenue, New York City. The window was the gift of his brother, Eugene Kelly, an affluent banker. It pictures, among others, Dionysius the Areopagite, the most distinguished convert of St. Paul, at Athens, and,

according to tradition, a preacher of the Faith in ancient France and founder of the Church of Paris. Father Kelly was, of course, an important figure in the early history of St. Mary's 

Parish, which, as has been said, was part of the original parish of St. Peter's, Jersey City.5 

St. Mary's Chapel

The famine of 1847 in Ireland and the political upheavals of 1848 in continental Europe, sent hundreds of thousands of families to the United States. The immigrants had scant means for travel further than the Atlantic coast, and generally they settled in the cities along the northerly seaboard. Here many of them found themselves among kith and kin who had left the old countries earlier. Immigrants obtained employment easily, for it was a period of expansion: factories multiplied, commerce increased, railway systems were established, building construction was upon a great scale, large city parks were developed, new streets were opened and paved, sewers were laid, and water and gas systems were installed. The high tide of immigration which flooded New York City was felt in Jersey City, where between 1850 and 1860 the population nearly tripled.' As St. Peter's Church, then at 108-110 Grand Street, near 

Washington Street, was soon inadequate for the growing number of communicants, Father John Kelly, its pastor, decided in 1854 to build a church at Tenth and Erie Streets for the Catholics who lived in the northern section of lower Jersey 

City. On May 15, 1854, Most Rev. James Roosevelt Bayley, first Bishop of Newark, whose long service as secretary to Most 

Rev. John Hughes, first Archbishop of New York, had given him a clear insight into conditions, bought for the sum of $10,000 ten lots owned by the estate of John B. Coles. They were part of Harsimus which, in 1804, Coles had bought from 

Kennedy and the Freeholders of Bergen.

Four of these lots formed a plot 100 feet square. It is situated on the southwest corner of the intersection of Tenth and Erie Streets, and forms part of the present (1938) site of St.

QUEENS LONG ISLAND EAST RIVER HOBOKEN MANHATTAN HUDSON RIVER ST. LUCY'S ST. MARY'S ST. MICHAELS AIRVIEW, LOWER JERSEY CITY, 1933 BROOKLYN ST. PETER'S ST. BRIDGET S FAIRCHILD AERIAL SURVEYS IN

EARLY CATHOLIC HISTORY 

13 Michael's parochial school. The remaining six lots purchased by Bishop Bayley were on the northwest corner of the intersection. They formed a plot 100 feet wide and 150 feet deep. 

These last six lots, which were later sold to private parties, seem not to have been devoted to parochial use. 

Upon the smaller plot of four lots, there was erected, under the direction of Father Kelly, a red brick church edifice, two stories high, fifty feet wide, and nearly one hundred feet deep. 

Construction was begun in the spring of 1855, and while the structure was in course of erection, Mass was said in the house of Patrick Gibney, a gardener, who lived at 225 Ninth Street, between Grove and Erie Streets. The cost of this church was about $25,000.⁹ 

On November 21, 1855, Bishop Bayley-who had, at Rome, on December 8, 1854, participated in promulgating the dogma of the Immaculate Conception-dedicated the new church under the patronage of the Mother of God. Thereafter, until 

1859, it was called St. Mary's Chapel.10 Mass at the dedication was celebrated by Very Rev. Patrick Moran, then pastor of St. 

John's, the oldest Catholic Church in Newark. Rev. Dr. 

Thomas Hayden, Vicar-General of the Diocese of Pittsburgh, preached the sermon. Appropriately, later in the day, Bishop 

Bayley went to St. Peter's Church (which had been dedicated in 1839 by Bishop Hughes), and there, in the first nursery of 

Catholicity in Hudson County, administered Confirmation.11

St. Mary's Chapel was thus the second Catholic Church in 

Jersey City,12 and the first to be dedicated in Hudson County by a Bishop of Newark.18

Apparently, for nearly a year, children were still baptized and marriages were celebrated at St. Peter's, the mother church. 

But on Sunday, September 28, 1856, Father Kelly married Hugh 

Tierney and Bridget Halligan in St. Mary's Chapel, in the presence of Owen T. and Rose McCloskey. Thereafter, Catholics of the northern lower city took their children to the chapel to be baptized, and marriages were often performed there by Father Kelly and his assistants, Fathers James Coyle and Patrick O'Brien. But there were only twenty-one mar-

riages performed in St. Mary's Chapel before April 3, 1859, when Bishop Bayley erected the new parish of St. Mary's, and named as its pastor Father Pierre Louis Dominique Senez.¹4 

The erection of this second parish had become necessary because the population of lower Jersey City, and particularly in the northern section, had rapidly increased. In fact, St. Mary's 

Chapel had been finished but two years when there was begun an extensive railroad development which brought to the city a large number of laborers. It was about December 13, 1857, that the managers of the Erie Railroad (among them, Jay Gould) resolved to abandon its terminal at Piermont, New York, and to make Jersey City the eastern depot of the railroad. Among other works of construction, it was necessary to fill in the mud flats of the water front along the Hudson River for more than thirteen hundred feet, and to bore a tunnel through the trap-rock of Bergen Hill. The stone and earth. removed from the hill were carried to the water front, and there used both for filling in and for the support of the Long Dock on lower Pavonia Avenue, east of Henderson Street. 

The tunnel through Bergen Hill was 4300 feet long, 22 feet high, and 291/2 feet wide. In the work of excavation there were eighteen working points. The laborers employed were mainly 

Italian and Irish, and they contributed not only to the growth of St. Mary's parish, but made necessary later the organization of St. Joseph's and St. Patrick's parishes. Before the tunnel had been completed, there were several accidental deaths among the laborers.15 Frequently, too, there were bitter fights between the laborers and residents of the Bergen section, despite the sincere efforts at intervention of Father Aloysius Vanuta, the first pastor of St. Joseph's; and on several occasions acute labor troubles delayed the progress of the undertaking.¹º

There are preserved today a few memories of the early days of the first St. Mary's Church. It is known that on September 

28, 1859, a bell was installed and blessed by Bishop Bayley, who preached the sermon of dedication.17 In the fall of 1859, Henry L. Richards, Esq., a noted Catholic convert, lectured

EARLY CATHOLIC HISTORY 

15 there to an audience of Catholics and non-Catholics. His lecture, which was well received, was entitled "Reasons for Becoming a Catholic."18 The decorative beauty of the church on 

Christmas Day, in 1859, was praised by The Courier and Advertiser, a local newspaper of the time. On March 17, 1860, Father James Mackey, later a prominent pastor in Sussex County, delivered a lecture upon St. Patrick, a subject not unfamiliar in an Irish neighborhood.¹ 19 

On April 29, 1860, the Sacrament of Confirmation was ad ministered apparently for the first time in St. Mary's parish by Bishop Bayley, who imposed his hand upon and then blessed several boys and girls in their early teens, cleansed in conscience of any grievous sin, well instructed in the fundamental truths of the Catholic Faith, and heartily and fervently desiring the grace and gifts of the Holy Spirit.20

The piety of the day is reflected by a procession of children on June 11, 1860, when the Feast of Corpus Christi was commemorated. It is recorded that Fathers Senez, Sanguinnatta, Celius, and Raybaudie officiated. The procession began immediately after Vespers, which were sung at four o'clock. The line of march was from the church at Erie and Tenth Streets, south along Erie Street to Ninth Street, where the procession turned east. It then passed Grove Street, and went to Henderson Street. There it turned south to Seventh Street, where the first orphan asylum of the parish had been established. Benediction was given there in the open air. The order of the procession was: (1) Hibernian Society, (2) Sacred Heart Society, (3) St. Vincent de Paul Society, (4) orphans (eighteen), and (5) Sunday School boys and girls. There were about three thousands persons assembled.21 

On August 15, 1860, upon the Feast of the Assumption,

Father Senez was celebrant at a military Mass, which preceded a picnic held in Hudson City upon the grounds of General Edwin V. R. Wright in the neighborhood of what is now Pershing

Field on Central Avenue.22 The proceeds of the picnic went to a fund for the new church. Its site, at Second and Erie Streets, had already been purchased by Bishop Bayley.23

CHAPTER III  His Early Life and Student Days 

A PERMANENT record of the first association of Father Senez with the new parish of St. Mary's is his signature-Louis D. 

Senez-written in the baptismal register on April 3, 1859, in evidence of the fact that he had christened James Mooney, born on March 22, 1859. The child was the son of James Mooney and Margaret Kelly Mooney, and his sponsors were Michael

Flinn and Jane Foster. The record also shows that the first marriage Father Senez performed in the parish was that of 

William Heffern and Eliza Flanagan, on May 1, 1859. Their witnesses were William Ryan and Margaret Flanagan.¹ The indications are that the first residence of Father Senez in the new parish was at 209 Pavonia Avenue, one of a row of brick houses still standing between Grove and Erie Streets.2 Father

Senez was thus the first resident pastor of St. Mary's Church, in Jersey City. 

Pierre Louis Dominique Senez was born on June 29, 1815, in the village of Beauvois-en-Cambresis, usually spoken of as Beauvois, in northwestern France. He was baptized on July 

2, 1815, by Père Lorquin. His parents were Jean Baptiste Senez, a farmer, and Louise Lévèque. His sponsors at baptism were 

Florent Vaillé, an iron-worker, and Desirée Lévèque, a first cousin. The father of Dominique Senez, as the boy was usually called, was at the time mayor of Beauvois, and he owned and cultivated a farm known as Le Jeune Bois (The Young Wood), distant about a third of a mile from the center of the village. 

He also operated a brew house, built upon the farm.3³ (Beauvois may now be reached by rail or automobile. In 1938, both the railroad and bus stations are near Le Jeune Bois, which still bears its early nineteenth-century name.*) When sold, in 1884, 16

the farm had been in the Senez family for more than three generations.5

Dominique Senez was educated at Cambrai and Douai. As a growing boy, he probably attended the school of the Recollects in Cambrai. Of the critical years of his early manhood there is only a slight available record, and priests who labored with him during the later years of his life, noted that he was reticent about himself as a young man. At Douai, probably in preparation for the study of law, he attended the ancient Catholic University.Ⓡ

The Senez family tradition is that while a law studentprobably in 1836-young Senez visited Paris, and while attending services with a companion at the Church of St. Sulpice, he was so deeply moved by the eloquence of a preacher that he resolved to give up the study of the law and become a missionary priest. It is clearly established that on December 8, 1836, upon the Feast of the Immaculate Conception, he entered the Petite Seminaire of the Society of St. Sulpice at Issy near 

Paris. He was tonsured on May 30, 1837. There are still preserved the century-old written observations of the Superior of the Petite Seminaire. They indicate that Dominique Senez, though diligent, was not remarkably intellectual, but that one of his several outstanding qualities was his pleasant disposition. 

On October 10, 1837, he become affiliated with the Diocese of Paris, and entered the famous Grande Seminaire of the Sulpicians. On December 22, 1838, he received minor orders. On 

May 29, 1839, he was made sub-deacon, and on December 21, of the same year, deacon. On December 15, 1840, he was ordained as priest.8

As a seminarian, Dominique Senez met Père Marie Alphonse Ratisbonne, a converted Jew, who was in large measure responsible for the determination of the young priest to become a missionary in the United States. Another companion at the Seminaire was a young priest who later was the Most Reverend Louis M. J. de Goesbriand, the first Bishop of Burlington, Vermont. Like Father Senez, Bishop de Goesbriand was, in the 1840's, a laborer in the Diocese of Cincinnati, Ohio.

In the 1880's he invited Father Senez to conduct retreats for priests in his New England diocese.10 

Among later students at the Seminaire who came to prominence in New Jersey were the Most Rev. James Roosevelt

Bayley, the first Bishop of Newark (1853-1872), who was ordained at St. Sulpice in 1843; and the Most Rev. Michael J.

O'Farrell, first Bishop of Trenton (1881-1894), who was ordained in Ireland in 1855, after he had completed his course and had been made deacon at St. Sulpice. Father O'Farrell was pastor of old St. Peter's in New York from 1873 to 1881.11 

First Years of His Priesthood 

The first assignment of Father Senez was in the ancient Diocese of Paris, where from 1841 to 1843 he was vicar at historic Chaillot, an outskirt of that city.12 Apparently it was upon the invitation of the Most Rev. John B. Purcell, Bishop of Cincinnati, that on August 8, 1843, Father Senez applied for adoption into that diocese. He arrived in Cincinnati in the summer of 1844. His name appears on the very first page of the records of the Church of St. Francis de Sales at Newark, Ohio, where he was apparently in charge from August 10, 1844, to May 4, 1845. There he built the first parish church.¹ 

In Chillicothe, once the capital of Ohio, at St. Peter's, a motherchurch of the diocese of Cincinnati, he administered baptism three times during the latter part of June, 1845. 14 In 1846, Father Senez became affiliated with the Diocese of 

New York, of which northern New Jersey was then a part.

Bishop Hughes assigned him to St. Vincent's Church, Madison, a parish which then embraced Morris and Sussex Counties, and probably part of Warren County.15 While at St. Vincent's, Father Senez laid the foundations of churches at Dover, Boonton,¹7 and Morristown.18 At Short Hills19 and at Springfield 20 he ministered to flocks who worshipped in private houses. In 1848 he visited his native land.21 16 Upon his return to America, he was appointed a curate at the Church of the Transfiguration in New York,22 and later an assistant to Father Patrick Moran, at St. John's in Newark.23

In 1850 Father Senez was appointed pastor of the unfinished St. Patrick's Church in Newark (now the Procathedral), which he completed.24 While pastor at St. Patrick's, in 1853, he helped establish St. John's in Orange,25 and bought the land for the first St. James' Church in Newark.26 In the late fall of 

1853, for a short period, Father Senez was an assistant at St. 

Ann's Church in New York City.27 In November, 1853, he became pastor of St. John's in Paterson,28 of which, in 1854, he established as a mission the Church of St. Francis de Sales at 

Lodi, said to be the oldest Catholic Church in Bergen County, New Jersey.29

In 1858 Father Senez returned to the diocese of Cincinnati, under Archbishop Purcell. There, from March 14 to July 15, 1858, he was an assistant at the Cathedral of St. Peter in 

Chains. From August 6, 1858, to March 21, 1859, he was pastor of the parish of St. Thomas, adjacent to St. Peter's Cathedral.30 He then returned to New Jersey. About April 3, 1859, Bishop Bayley appointed him pastor of the newly erected parish of St. Mary's in Jersey City. 31 

Temporary Transfer to Hoboken 

On January 1, 1876, Father Senez was transferred from St. 

Mary's to Our Lady of Grace, Hoboken, the second oldest parish in Hudson County. His appointment to Hoboken was made by Bishop Corrigan because an experienced priest was needed to supervise the completion of the present church edifice in the Hoboken parish. The erection of this church had been begun under the preceding pastor, Reverend Major Charles Duggan, and had been hardly advanced beyond the corner stone, when funds gave out. Father Senez thus became the third pastor of Our Lady of Grace. He held this position, however, only until the following September. Then, because at sixty-one years he felt age advancing, he was returned to 

Jersey City, to the great joy of the congregation of St. Mary's.

In a sermon preached on September 10, 1876, Father Senez gave expression to his joy at finding himself pastor again at 32

St. Mary's.33 He was to serve there, during his second incumbency, for a period of almost twenty-four years. 

Later Years of Father Senez 

It was in about 1885 that Father Senez gave up his horse and buggy, which he had used for many years and had kept in a small stable which stood on Third Street, between the old parish rectory and the Institute. Perhaps this change was a result of the death of William Doherty, who had been stableman as well as sexton of the church. By trade a chair-maker, with an establishment always in the vicinity of Newark Avenue and Barrow Street, Mr. Doherty, like Ann, the Irish housekeeper at the rectory, was a parochial character. He used to try to shame delinquents into paying their weekly pew money by calling out after them as they entered church, "Tin cints! tin cints!," and by some old folk of the parish he is to this day called "Tin Cint Doherty."35

In any event, it was at about this time that the old pastor gave up the recreation of an afternoon drive. Often this drive had led him by way of the Erie Ferry from the foot of Pavonia Avenue, across the Hudson River to Twenty-third Street in New York, where probably some business or conference would call him.36 There are evidences, too, of drives in the early 

1880's to quiet places, like the fields and salt meadows then at the foot of Jersey Avenue, and the open spaces in near-by 

Greenville. Here, in what was then almost an absolutely quiet country-side, the priest would read his breviary and dwell in contemplation upon the mysteries of faith and life, and then, half dozing behind the reins, he would turn the knowing horse around to find its own way down hill along accustomed streets and around well-known corners until, it is said, it would stop at 219 Third Street with a jolt that would waken the slumbering driver.37

It was in this period, too, that the aging priest would often sit in contemplation behind a pillar near the sanctuary; sometimes he would sit there, out of sight, listening to the choir as it rehearsed under the direction of Miss Katherine Ashurst, an

accomplished organist. This was a time when rules as to church music were lightly regarded, and there seemed to be no restriction to the type of music rendered. Indeed it was not unusual to hear at services even operatic compositions of Mozart, Beethoven, Schubert, Gounod, and Verdi. In the "paid quartette," always excellent vocalists, Father Senez took great pride, for their beautiful singing helped draw worshipers from all parts of Jersey City, and added greatly to the beauties of the liturgy.38

His Clerical Associates 

Many parishioners now growing old remember the days at St. Mary's, when they listened to the impressively fluent, dignified, and aristocratic Father Carroll, to the magnetic Father Kelly, who was probably then the outstanding pulpit orator of the diocese, and, in the 1890's, to the small, boyish, scholarly Father Mooney, who, as if in conversation, delivered serious homilies, neatly analyzed and readily understood.39 

After Father Carroll's departure, about the end of the winter of 1888-89, Father Kelly became Father Senez's first assistant.

In 1890 he was permitted to go abroad. He traveled as far as 

Oberamergau, where he saw the Passion Play. Among other places, he visited in Paris the Seminary of St. Sulpice, where he learned for the first time that Father Senez had been ordained in 1840, a fact which the latter's reticence had withheld, lest his golden jubilee should have been made a public occasion. Priests at St. Sulpice who had known of Dominique Senez were surprised to learn that he was still living; he had not been heard of in years.40

Father Kelly returned to St. Mary's rich in the experiences of travel; and he brought back many excellent pictures. The following winter, his work in screening them for the interesting lectures which he gave in the auditorium of the Institute, was greatly aided by the accomplished Stephen H. Horgan. 

Mr. Horgan, still alive (1938) at eighty-five or more, was a pioneer in photo-engraving for newspapers in New York City. 

It is to his genius, experiments, and discoveries, that the art of 41

modern illustration measurably owes its origin, advance, and possibilities.42

In the early 1890's, besides Fathers Kelly and Mooney, other noted priests of the Diocese of Newark were stationed at St. 

Mary's. Among them there was Father John A. Stafford, who, like Father Mooney, was later a president of Seton Hall College. When he died, he was pastor of St. Patrick's in Jersey 

City. There was also Father Hubert J. Behr, S.T.D., who is now Monsignor, and pastor of St. Michael's in Elizabeth. 

Toward the closing years of the century—which coincided with those of the Dean's life-those who came to assist at St. 

Mary's were: Fathers James M. McCormick; Charles A. Smith, later Chancellor of the diocese, and at the time of his death, pastor of St. Joseph's in Newark; William T. McLoughlin, later well liked at St. Lucy's where he died, its pastor, in April, 1924; James T. Delahanty, now Monsignor and pastor of St. 

John's in Paterson, where Dean Senez had labored from 1854 to 1857; and Daniel J. Brady, now pastor of All Saints Church, Jersey City. Father Brady succeeded Father Mooney as first assistant at St. Mary's in September, 1897, a year after Father 

Senez had been named Dean of Hudson County at the eighth Synod held in the Diocese of Newark."4 43 At the time of Father Senez' death, nearly all his early associates had predeceased him. Archbishop John Baptist Purcell of

Cincinnati, upon whose invitation he had come to the United 

States in 1843, had been dead seventeen years.45 Bishop James

Roosevelt Bayley, his senior by only a year, whom he had known as secretary of Archbishop John Hughes of New York, had passed away, as Archbishop of Baltimore, in 1877.48 John 

Cardinal McCloskey had died in 1885.47 Dr. Jeremiah Cummings, a talented co-worker when New Jersey was still part of the diocese of New York, had died as pastor of St. Stephen's, New York City. Father Patrick Moran, whose assistant Dean

Senez had been in Newark in the early 1850's, and who had been ordained but eight years before him, had died as pastor of St. John's in Newark, and Vicar-General, in 1866.48 Father

Thomas Quinn, his junior, who had preceded him as pastor of

133 St. Johns, Paterson in the middle of 1850's, had died pastor at 

Rahway, in 1873.19 Father Daniel Fisher, his assistant at Paterson, and second president of Seton Hall in the late 1850's, had died in Hoboken in 1869, while an assistant to Father 

Anthony Cauvin at Our Lady of Grace.50 Monsignor De Con cilio, fifteen years his junior, an early scholarly assistant at St. 

Mary's in 1863-1867, had passed away in the spring of 1898, after a pastorate of thirty years in the adjoining filial parish of 

St. Michael's.51 Father Patrick Corrigan, temporary successor of the Dean at St. Mary's in 1876, and who succeeded him, too, that same year at Our Lady of Grace in Hoboken, had died, at fifty-nine, in 1894.52 And it was in November, 1899,53 less than three months before his own death, that the Dean heard of the demise of his oldest friend, Bishop De Goesbriand, who had been a fellow student at St. Sulpice in the 1830's, a colaborer in the Ohio missions in the 1840's,54 and whom in 1853, at old St. Patrick's in Mott Street in New York, the Dean had seen consecrated as first Bishop of the Diocese of Burlington, in Vermont.55 

The oldest of the Dean's early associates in America who survived him was his close friend, Father Anthony Cauvin, his senior by five years, then in retirement near Nice in Italy, where he had gone in the early 1870's, after having established 

Our Lady of Grace in Hoboken.56 The next oldest contempo rary surviving Dean Senez was Most Reverend Bernard Mc Quaid, his associate in the country-side parish of Madison in the late 1840's, whose talents he had noted, whose career he had influenced, and who had been consecrated, in 1868, as first Bishop of the Diocese of Rochester.57 

Several of the Dean's assistants at St. Mary's in the early 

1860's, were still in full strength.58 Monsignor Henry A.

Brann, pastor of St. Agnes in New York City, was regarded as an outstanding metropolitan churchman. Father Patrick

Byrnes, in ripe old age, was officiating at St. Aloysius, in Caldwell, New Jersey. Father Edward McCosker was pastor of 

St. Joseph's, in Paterson; and Father John P. Morris was pastor of Our Lady of Grace at Avondale, now Nutley. Father

John Anthony Vasallo was pastor of St. Theresa's Church at 

Summit. Father Patrick McGovern had returned to the Diocese of New York, and was in virtual retirement.59 So, too, was 

Father Thomas M. Killeen, who, until 1896, had for twenty years been pastor of St. Mary's Star of the Sea, in Bayonne. 

Of the Dean's assistants in the early 1870's, Fathers James P. Smith and Samuel J. Walsh (he served again from 1877 to 

1883) had died in the middle 1880's; and their associate, Father 

James McKernan, who affiliated after 1881 with the new Diocese of Trenton, was in ill health, an inmate of St. Francis 

Hospital, Trenton. Father Dennis J. McCartie, who, early in 

1876, had come to St. Mary's with Father Patrick Corrigan and remained until the summer of 1877, was pastor of St. 

Michael's in Newark. He was sometime Chancellor of the 

Diocese of Newark. Father John P. Callaghan, assistant at

St. Mary's, first from 1878 to 1882 and again in the spring of 

1886, was pastor of Our Lady Help of Christians, in East Orange.

In the 1880's the outstanding assistants of Dean Senez had been Fathers Patrick M. Corr, Lawrence C. M. Carroll, Charles 

J. Kelly, and Eugene A. Farrell. Father Corr had died in 1893, in Ireland, while pastor of St. Bridget's, Jersey City; Father 

Carroll was pastor of St. Patrick's in Jersey City, where in 1896 he had succeeded Father Patrick Hennessy; and Father Kelly was pastor of Our Lady of Grace in Hoboken. There, in 1894, he had followed Father Patrick Corrigan. Father Farrell had died in the fall of 1898, at St. Peter's, Belleville, where he had been pastor for three years. 60 In the 1890's, among the assistants of Dean Senez had been 

Fathers James F. Mooney, John A. Stafford, and Hubert J.

Behr. All were alive at the time of his death. Father Mooney was pastor of St. Cecelia's in Kearny, the site of which had been purchased by Monsignor John F. Ryan, now pastor of St. 

Bridget's, Jersey City.61 Father Stafford had become the seventh president of Seton Hall; and Father Behr was also then at that institution, teaching philosophy and theology.

Death and Funeral of Dean Senez 

Until 1898, Dean Senez was able to give attention to the books and records of the parish, but about that time the care of them fell to the charge of Father Brady, his first assistant. 

It does not appear that the Dean attended the funeral Mass for Monsignor DeConcilio at St. Michael's, on March 25, 1898. 

For several years his orbit had not been large; and there were days of long vigils before the Blessed Sacrament. When he celebrated Mass on Sundays, there would be placed upon the highest step of the altar a chair, that he might be spared the risk and effort of descending the steps when another priest would mount the pulpit to preach. Still he oversaw matters to a slight extent, and until shortly before his death, required that all checks should be signed by him.

One gathers that like all old men, he liked to reminisce. 

There were talks of the old days, of almost sixty years back, when he came to America, thinking of converting Indians; of forty years back, when Isaac Hecker and his Paulist companions wished the Dean to join them and be their superior; of the 

Curé d'Ars, deceased in 1859, towards the fund for whose canonization the Dean had given five hundred dollars because he believed that an outstanding parish priest was worthy of sanctification by the Church. Perhaps he did not speak of the days when, it is said, he sold his watch to help the orphans, always cherished by his charity; and there has come down to us the knowledge that his patrimony was given to the Grey Nuns of Paris, for the orphans there. 62 

When feebleness came upon Dean Senez, in 1899, he gave up his practice of celebrating Mass in the church. For a year or more before his death he made his bedroom, perforce, a Holy of Holies, and there he daily celebrated Mass. He could see through the bedroom window the steady flame of the sanctuary lamp in the old church. At 4:30 A.M. he would arise, and then, for an hour and a half, be at vigil. Before him—a candle at either side of it—was a delicately carved ivory crucifix which

portrayed realistically his suffering Savior. After Mass, there was always a prolonged thanksgiving.68

In his last days there came significant visitors. Dr. Edward 

O'Callaghan, a trustee of the church, came daily to see him. 

Twice within the last year Archbishop Corrigan called. Prelate and priest had their jokes. The Dean thought the Archbishop (of small stature) grew more and more to look like an Archbishop. Did the Dean remember, the Archbishop would like to know, when the Dean, half a century back, in Newark, had boxed the ears of the Archbishop for a boyish prank? The

Dean did not remember, but if he had punished, it was deserved, even by a future Archbishop. A few days before the 

Dean died Monsignor George Doane, the spiritual son of

Bishop Bayley, called. He remained but a few minutes, and left, inwardly stirred by many memories of the early critical years of the diocese. 64 

The Very Reverend Dean Louis Pierre Dominique Senez died at the parish rectory, 219 Third Street, Jersey City, New Jersey, in the early hours of Sunday, February 11, 1900. Besides

Father Brady, there were present Sister Loretta, a teacher at the Institute, and Sister Margaret Agnes, a teacher at the

Academy. It was the latter's delightful task to dress the altars.65

The death of Dean Senez brought to an end a priestly career of almost sixty years of steady labor in the dioceses of Paris, Cincinnati, New York, and Newark. Upon the American scene he had been, in Ohio, New York, and New Jersey, a missionary priest in fact as well as in name. He died in his eighty-sixth year, the oldest cleric in the diocese of Newark.66

In the church which the Dean had built almost forty years earlier, his body lay in state for two days. His faithful congregation and cherished friends came from all sides to evidence their respect and affection, and to look upon his features. 

Among the thousands who looked for the last time upon the remains of the Dean, were many of other faiths. Noted among

them was the Honorable Gilbert Collins, a Justice of the Supreme Court of New Jersey, long a friend and adviser of Dean 

Senez. Another was Rev. Dr. George Bennett, pastor of

Grace Church (Van Vorst), the adjacent Episcopal establishment. Dr. Bennett, like his predecessor there, Rev. Stephen 

M. Rice, had maintained an enduring friendship with the Dean. 

Through the night of February 13, 1900, Dr. Bennett was at the bier, deep in reflection. Both he and Mr. Justice Collins attended the Requiem Mass celebrated the next morning.7 

At nine o'clock the office of the dead was chanted by nearly fifty priests, who came from all parts of the Diocese of Newark. They were led by Reverend John A. Stafford, president of 

Seton Hall College, and Reverend John P. Callahan, of East 

Orange.

At the obsequies for the soul of Dean Senez, the church was, of course, crowded to the doors; and in the sanctuary there was scarcely room for the older clergy. 

Orphans from St. Mary's Asylum occupied seats immediately in front of the main altar, and the Sisters of the Poor of St. 

Francis and the Passionist priests from the Monastery at West 

Hoboken, attended in a body. The Young Women's Sodality of St. Mary's, wearing white tulle veils, was represented in large numbers. Outside the church, thousands unable to enter discussed the accomplishments and virtues of the dead priest. 

The honorary pallbearers were: County Collector Hugh 

Dugan, Commissioner of Finance John Mullins, former City 

Treasurer Patrick Nugent, Tax Commissioner Cornelius J. 

Cronin, Thomas Burns, James Clements, Anthony Dwyer, Joseph F. Farmer, James Fitzgerald, Michael Hopkins, Patrick 

Turley, John F. Lynch, Philip McEntee, Daniel Moriarity, James Rafter, and Dennis Reardon. The active pallbearers were: Counsellor James R. Bowen, Charles C. Cassidy, Michael 

Craven, Dr. Peter Hoffman, Freeholder Frank McNally, Henry 

T. Nugent, Frank O'Neill, and John Reilly.

At the Mass, Archbishop Corrigan presided. The celebrant was Rt. Rev. Robert Seton, D.D., pastor of St. Joseph's, Jersey

City, an ecclesiastic of notably classic features, and a grandson

of Mother Elizabeth Bayley Seton, foundress, in America, of the order of the Sisters of Charity. Two years later he was to become titular archbishop of the See of Heliopolis. The deacon was Rev. Bernard Henry Ter Woert, pastor of St. 

John the Baptist, Jersey City. He was to be the next pastor of 

St. Mary's. The sub-deacon was Rev. Charles P. Gillen, pastor of St. Joseph's in Paterson. He had been an assistant at St. 

Patrick's, Newark. He was the second pastor (1888-1895) of 

St. Lucy's in Jersey City, a filial church of St. Mary's. 68 Rev. Patrick Cody preached the funeral sermon. He was pastor of St. James the Less in Newark, where almost half a century earlier Dean Senez had purchased the ground upon which the congregation had built its first church. Both Dean 

Senez and Father Patrick Corrigan, pastor of St. Mary's in 1876, had numbered Father Cody among their close friends. His discourse naturally, was both an adequate review of a long and remarkable life and a proper eulogy of a capable priest who, beloved deeply and abidingly by several flocks, was, in the minds of his fellow-clerics, a shining exemplar in the service of God. The preacher dwelt upon the visits of the dead man to France, every four years, to see his mother. They had ceased after she died, about 1880, at the age of ninety-four.70

The Mass sung by the quartette was Cherubini's in C minor, which had been sung when the church was formally dedicated by Bishop Bayley in June, 1863.71 The soloists were Miss Emma 

L. Radley, soprano; Miss Anna de Wolfe, alto; Mr. Charles 

Smith, tenor; and Mr. Charles Gallagher, basso. Miss Helen

Armstrong was at the organ. At the offertory "Pro Peccatis" from Rossini's Stabat Mater was sung by Mr. Gallagher, and at the end of the Mass Mr. Smith sang the tenor solo, "My 

God and My Father."

The Dean's grave, as he had requested, is in Holy Name Cemetery, in a section reserved for the bodies of orphan children.72 

A little more than two years after the death of Dean Senez,

Archbishop Corrigan died in New York. Later there was published, in installments, his informing Register of the Clergy

Laboring in the Archdiocese of New York from Early Missionary Times to 1885. The Register is made up of detached succinct sketches of prelates and priests, and it includes Dean Senez. About two hundred words are devoted to him, and although a few early dates are not quite correct, the matter is an excellent narrative of his life. But the greatest value and the highest significance of the sketch are reverently hidden in what is apparently casually objective but really deliberately emotional. For those who know the story, the Archbishop, an impressively pious man, touches gently many mystic chords of ancient and holy memories. It is thus he concludes his account of the first pastor of St. Mary's: "He was a superior man, with a wonderful power over his people. His funeral took place 

February 14, 1900, from the Immaculate Conception Church of Jersey City, absolution being given by Archbishop Corrigan, who, when a boy, had been prepared for his First Holy Communion by Father Senez."73

CHAPTER IV  Its Dedication 

THE solemn blessing and laying of the corner stone of the 

Church of the Immaculate Conception, at Erie and Second 

Streets, occurred on Sunday, June 23, 1861.¹

Shortly before the ceremony several societies connected with the church, including delegates from the Hibernian United 

Benevolent Society, St. Patrick's Benevolent Society, and the

Confraternity of the Sacred Heart, together with a group of girls dressed all in white and a large number of boys, formed in a procession at the first church of the congregation, at the corner of Erie and Tenth Streets. Headed by the clergy, they marched to the site of the new church to participate in the exercises. Several banners and flags were borne in the procession, and conspicuous among them was the Stars and Stripes. 

The first story of the edifice had been covered with a temporary floor, for the better accommodation of those who wished to avoid the crowd gathered in the streets. Tickets had been issued at a charge of twenty-five cents. From three thousand to four thousand persons availed themselves of this opportunity to enter within the walls, and as many more were gathered on the outside.

The ceremonies were conducted by Bishop Bayley, attired in full canonicals. He was assisted by a large number of clergy from the neighboring parishes and from New York. Before the placing of the corner stone, the Bishop made an appeal to the assemblage for funds to aid in the completion of the building. He said it was the first time since he had become bishop that he had found it necessary to make such an appeal on such an occasion, but the ground for the church had been secured, a portion of the materials purchased, and the work commenced before the dark cloud of war rising over the country was bigger 30

AR WITH YOU ALL DYS TO THE END 

YET 

A LITTLE WARE ARD YOU & SET OF INTERIOR OF THE OLD CHURCH

than a man's hand, and before it was believed that the people of this nation would make fools of themselves by being led or forced into a civil war by a few fanatics. At the conclusion of his remarks, a collection was taken up, and so far as could be ascertained a large amount of money was realized. A prayer followed, and the ceremony of laying the corner stone was then performed. A small leaden box, containing copies of papers and several other articles, was placed beneath it. Another prayer was then offered by the Bishop. The ceremonial rites concluded with an appropriate sermon delivered by Rev. Clarence A. Walworth,2 of the Paulist order. At the conclusion, the various societies again formed in procession, and headed by the Bishop and clergy, returned to the church at Tenth and 

Erie Streets.

The new church edifice, as designed and actually built, was of red brick, in the Gothic style, 142 feet long by 70 feet wide. 

It was trimmed with brownstone taken from the quarries in Belleville, New Jersey. It had heavy buttresses, square towers on the front, and a lofty spire rising in the middle. The massive foundation gave promise of the magnificent superstructure which served a pious congregation for nearly forty years, and was known as St. Mary's in Jersey City. Later generations mention it as Father Senez's Church.

The church, although incompleted, was formally dedicated on Sunday, April 27, 1862, by Bishop Bayley.3 The celebration of a Solemn Pontifical Mass began at half-past ten o'clock. The sermon was preached by Rev. Thomas Scott Preston, then pastor of St. Ann's Church in New York City, and later Vicar- 

General of that diocese. The soloist of the occasion was Miss Beesman. 

On Sunday, May 31, 1863, after the interior had been finished, the church was formally opened by Bishop Bayley, in the presence of two thousand persons. It was a day of sunshine, with the temperature at seventy degrees. At half-past ten o'clock precisely, the procession, consisting of acolytes, attendant priests, the Bishop, and some fifty little girls, beautifully attired in white, entered the church by the main door and

moved to the chancel. According to the published accounts, the music of the Mass was well rendered by the choir. The organist was Augustus D. W. Beesman. A very difficult duet, a Gloria for bass and tenor, was given in fine style by Messrs. Hartmann and Vonseeler. Regina Dilecta Tabernaculi was executed in an excellent manner by the soloists and the choir. At the offertory, the Qui tollis was admirably sung by Miss Beesman. The 

Sanctus was by von Weber; the Benedictus and Dona Nobis were gems of musical perfection.

The sermon was delivered by Rev. Dr. Jeremiah W. Cummings, whose life and labors were part of the history of St. 

Stephen's Church in New York. He had been the celebrant of the Mass of the clergy and laity at St. Patrick's Procathedral in 

Newark on November 1, 1853, when Most Reverend James

Roosevelt Bayley had been installed as first Bishop of that 

Diocese.5 

Dr. Cummings took for his dedication text the prayer of 

Solomon at the consecration of the Temple. In a powerful discourse he enforced upon the congregation the necessity and power of prayer at all times and under all circumstances. With great solemnity, Bishop Bayley gave for the first time in the Diocese of Newark the papal benediction authorized by Pope Pius IX to be conferred by those prelates (among whom the Bishop had been) present at Rome at the then recent canonization of Japanese martyrs. In the evening, at half-past seven o'clock, Vespers were sung. There was a discourse by Rev. 

Patrick Byrne, a popular assistant of Father Senez. The same ticket admitted to both Mass and Vespers." 

The completed new edifice had cost $40,000 and was then the largest and best-appointed church in New Jersey. The masonry was the work of James Hall and Son, and the carpentry that of Keary Brothers (James, Thomas, and William).

These were leading building firms which, in the second half of the nineteenth century, gave employment and instruction to many in Jersey City who became skilled journeymen, and, in several instances, very successful master workmen and contractors. The church was regarded as a handsome monument to

the lower city. The roof was originally of slate, but the heavy weight presented a serious problem of safety, and after a few years a tin roof, much lighter, was substituted." 

The main body of the interior of the church was dometopped, and in days before the use of structural steel, it was considered an excellent demonstration of engineering and architectural skill, for the only supporting pillars were in the two side aisles. In the rear of the interior, a gallery and choir loft gave additional seating capacity for the parishioners, convenient space for the choir members, and, after 1867, housed an excellent organ built by the Jardines, noted organ builders.8 

The acoustical properties of the church were remarkably good, and brought out well the quality of the singers' voices.⁹ 

Missions 

Father Senez's church was the real center of the parish for thirty-eight years. During that period the congregation was blessed in the visits of many missionary priests. The first mission was probably that begun on September 29, 1866, by a group of Paulists.10 On December 18, 1874, there was a mission of another Paulist group, consisting of Fathers Elliott, Brady, Dwyer, and Deshon.¹¹ Father Deshon was one of the founders of the order of St. Paul the Apostle. He had been a classmate and roommate of General Grant at West Point.

The offering made by the congregation on the opening of this mission was regarded as the contribution of St. Mary's Parish to the erection of the present Church of St. Paul the Apostle, at Fifty-ninth Street and Ninth Avenue, New York City.12 

On June 12, 1876, the Jesuit Fathers O'Connor, Damien,

Sivers, and McCarthy, began a mission which led to several conversions. More than seven thousand of the congregation received Holy Communion.13 In 1879 the Redemptorist 

Fathers conducted the first of their several missions in the parish. The wooden cross they gave the congregation on 

March 23, 1879, is still preserved in the east transept of the present church. It is one of the few relics of the early days of the parish. In the fall of 1886, the Redemptorists officiated

again at a mission.14 In November, 1893, there was another mission conducted by them.15 Between seven and eight thousand parishioners attended. In October, 1894, they were again on hand, and more than twenty-five hundred men and three thousand women attended the services.16 The last mission in the old church was opened by Redemptorists on January 5, 1901, and lasted four weeks.17 

Confirmations 

The first three Bishops of Newark-Bayley, Corrigan and

Wigger-all visited Father Senez's church in the line of their episcopal duty. Their visits were generally made for the purpose of administering the Sacrament of Confirmation, and in the early life of the parish this usually occurred in April or May, every third year. Bishop Wigger, however, toward the end of his life, apparently came every two years. Bishop 

Bayley, we have seen, administered this Sacrament for the first time in St. Mary's Parish on April 29, 1860, which was the spring of the year following the appointment of Father

Senez as pastor. Bishop Bayley came to the parish probably in 1863, and again in 1866, and surely—and probably for the last time in May, 1869.18 

Most Rev. Michael A. Corrigan, the second Bishop of

Newark, administered the Sacrament of Confirmation at Old 

St. Mary's Church in 1873, 1876, and in 1878. In this last year, on May 12, he confirmed three hundred children. In the newspaper of the day it was noted that he congratulated 

Father Senez and his assistants, Fathers Samuel J. Walsh and

James Hefferman, and the Sisters of Charity for the zeal they had all shown in giving religious instruction to the young. 

The parish records indicate that the classes for confirmation during the 1880's and 1890's when Most Rev. Winand M. 

Wigger was third Bishop of Newark, ran from 300 to 548, and averaged about 400 members. In all the classes there was a slight predominance in the number of girls. This Sacrament was administered twice by Bishop Wigger in 1890. On April 

30, 1890, he administered it to 533 children and 40 adults. Of

the latter, ten were converts. Among those then confirmed were seven Negroes; one was the daughter of a Negro minister. The sponsors were George Smith and Mary Ann Collins. 

On November 1, 1890, Bishop Wigger confirmed 207 adults— 116 men and 91 women. Their sponsors were Bernard Laverty and Mary Laverty. 

Before the year 1900, other sponsors, whose names are indicated by the records, were: James Kirby and William Judge for the boys, and Mary McEntee and Mary Campbell for the girls (May 26, 1892); Edward O'Callaghan, James Galvin and 

Miss Fitzgerald (May 3, 1894); Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Burns (May 18, 1896); James Rafter and Mrs. Edward O'Callaghan (May 22, 1898). 

On May 28, 1900, Bishop Wigger confirmed a class of 541288 boys and 253 girls. The sponsors for the boys and girls, respectively, were Hugh Coyle, Jr. and Miss Cecelia Hope (later Mrs. John Connors). This was the last time the Sacrament of Confirmation was administered in St. Mary's Church by Bishop Wigger. He died on January 5, 1901. It was also the last time that the Sacrament was administered in the church which Father Senez had built.¹ 19 

Notable Sermons 

Occasionally the Bishops delivered sermons in St. Mary's. 

On September 25, 1870, Bishop Bayley spoke on "The Common 

School System."20 On April 28, 1890, Bishop Wigger delivered a sermon on "Christian Marriage."21 Many other high churchmen came to deliver discourses to audiences that crowded the church to capacity. On February 9, 1868, Archbishop McCloskey, of New York, the first native American to head that archdiocese, spoke upon "Catholic Charity" for the benefit of the poor of the parish, to whom the admission money was donated.22 On November 27, that year, Father Isaac Hecker, one of the founders of the Paulists, gave a lecture upon "Religious 

Conditions in America."23 

On March 17, 1863, Dean William A. McNulty, one of the successors of Father Senez as pastor of St. John's at Paterson,

delivered a lecture on "Saint Patrick."24 On October 30, 1864,

Dr. Jeremiah W. Cummings gave a sermon on the "Rules and 

Ceremonies of the Catholic Church."25 During April, 1873, the famous Dominican, Dr. Thomas N. Burke, O.P., delivered a notably eloquent sermon.26 On April 17, 1876, Father James 

Kent Stone, a convert, formerly President of Kenyon College in Ohio, and of Hobart College in New York, and later an important figure in the Passionist Order, spoke on the subject of "What People Have Lost by Unbelief."27 On May 17, 1885, Dr. James F. Callaghan, a native of Trenton, for many years editor of the Catholic Telegraph, a church paper of great influence published in Cincinnati, preached the sermon at High Mass.28

Extended inquiry would doubtless bring to light other noted figures of the early Catholic American church who filled the old movable pulpit29 which, as required, was moved along tracks from the epistle side of the church towards the center and head of the main aisle. It would indeed be an excellent writer who could adequately describe the enthusiastic interest of the pious men and women of the parish who hung upon every word from the lips of talented clerics, who, on missions, or from time to time, entertainingly gave to crowded congregations wholesome instruction and wise counsel.

Its Consecration 

In 1885 Father Senez, then a priest for forty-five years, was seventy years old.
