He had at this time completed at St. Mary's a service of more than a quarter of a century, and he looked upon a well-organized, well-ordered parish, practically free of debt, with the consecration of its church in near prospect. St. 

Mary's was probably the largest and most prosperous parish in New Jersey.30 But for Father Senez there had set in the natural decline that marks the passage of three score years and ten, and he was, of course, freed of many activities. At this period his parochial labors, among 7500 communicants, were made light by his assistants, of whom memories still endure. 

They were Fathers Patrick M. Corr (1882-1885), Lawrence

C. M. Carroll (1883-1889) and, easily chief, Charles J.

Kelly (1885-1894).31 

In the summer of 1887, St. Mary's Church presented a very attractive appearance, for its weather-stained red brick exterior had received a fresh coat of paint of beautiful light gray, with trimmings of brown. The church (in words of a newspaper reporter) "looks like a real new one and adds much to the good looks of the street upon which it is situated."32 In the following summer the interior was completely renovated and redecorated, and preparations were made for the consecration of the edifice, as the congregation had generously given in a Sunday collection nearly three times the three thousand dollars which was necessary (and had been requested by Father Senez) to pay the remaining parish debt.33 

On the morning of Saturday, October 6, 1888, Bishop

Wigger officiated at the service of the solemn consecration of the church begun by Father Senez a quarter of a century earlier.34 Catholics from all parts of Jersey City rejoiced, and there was a general desire to attend the Pontifical Mass which the Bishop celebrated next day as a fitting close to the important solemnity. His Excellency was attended by: Fathers Peter 

Cassidy, S.J., pastor of St. Peter's in Jersey City, beloved by students of the college there in the 1880's; the Very Rev. 

William A. McNulty (1829-1922), pastor of St. John's, Paterson, a militant priest and outspoken critic of corrupt government; Rev. Patrick Cody, then pastor of the Church of St. 

James the Less, in Newark, and who twelve years later was to deliver the sermon at the funeral of the Dean; Rev. Maurice 

P. O'Connor, who as a boy was a member of St. Mary's Parish, and who was then pastor of Holy Cross Church, Harrison, where he was a dominant civic leader; Rev. Charles P. Gillen, then the recently appointed pastor of St. Lucy's in Jersey City; and Rev. Patrick M. Corr, a former curate at St. Mary's, then pastor at Holy Trinity Church, Hackensack, New Jersey, and shortly afterwards pastor of St. Bridget's, Jersey City. Within the chancel were Monsignor De Concilio, pastor of St. 

Michael's Church, Jersey City, a former assistant at St. Mary's

in the 1860's, and Fathers Alphonse, Hanley, and McAvoy, Father Senez, and his two assistants, Fathers Carroll and Kelly.

A powerful, significant sermon was delivered by the Paulist, Rev. Walter Elliott. The services concluded with the Te Deum sung by the quartette, a group of singers generally regarded as excellent. In the evening, Father Senez, then in his seventyfourth year, sang Solemn Vespers, and Monsignor De Concilio spoke upon "The Necessity of Sanctifying Grace."

Thereafter, the anniversary of this consecration was religiously observed. At the eleventh anniversary, on October 8, 1899, four months before the death of Dean Senez-whose health was then failing fast-Rev. Charles A. Smith, his assistant, was the celebrant of a Solemn Mass, at which the deacon was Rt. Rev. James T. Delahanty, now pastor of St. John's, Paterson, and the sub-deacon, Father Martuccio. 35 An appropriate sermon was delivered upon this occasion by 

Rt. Rev. James F. Mooney, first assistant to Father Senez in the middle 1890's, and then pastor of St. Cecelia's in Kearny. 

Later Father Mooney was president of Seton Hall College. He was a gentleman of high rank among the scholars of the Church in America. At the time of his death, he was a domestic prelate, and pastor of St. Joseph's in Jersey City, where he had succeeded the Rt. Rev. Patrick E. Smythe.36 

Memories of Old St. Mary's 

On Sunday afternoon, June 19, 1864, St. Mary's congregation had the full significance of civil war brought to its doors, when Father Senez officiated at the obsequies for young 

Brigadier General Richard Byrnes, of the United States Army.

General Byrnes, with his wife and three little girls, had come to live at 519 Grove Street, in the fall of 1863. On June 3, 1864, the young officer had fallen, mortally wounded, while leading the Irish Brigade in the attack on the Confederate entrenchments at Cold Harbor, Virginia, one of the most cruel and bloody battles of the war between the American States.37 

The body of General Byrnes, which had been sent from

Washington to Jersey City, was borne from his home to St. 

Mary's Church. The pallbearers came from among his companion-in-arms: Colonel Richard Nugent of the Irish Brigade, Lieutenant Colonel Cartwright of the Twenty-eighth Massachu setts Regiment, Colonel John O. Mahoney of the Phoenix

Regiment, and Captain Cartright of the Twenty-eighth Massachusetts. After the last rites had been performed by Father 

Senez, the cortège proceeded to the foot of Exchange Place, and then by ferry to New York City. There, at the foot of 

Cortlandt Street, the body was received by the Sixty-ninth Regiment, which acted as a guard of honor. It was then taken by way of Cortlandt Street, Broadway, and Tenth Street, across the East River, to Calvary Cemetery. 38

This brave soldier was notedly a good Christian. His name is held in memory on at least two monuments: one is that of his regiment, the Twenty-eighth Massachusetts, near the wheat field at Gettysburg, Pennsylvania, where he stood with his men in the tensest hours of July 2, 1863, the critical second day of that decisive engagement;39 the other is at West Point, New 

York, on a monument dedicated to the soldiers of the Regular Army who died to preserve the American Union.40 

An outstanding illustration of parochial interest in the constant conflict between capital and labor is seen in the settlement of an incipient general strike, in the summer of 1898, through the efforts of Father Brady. He was then in his thirtieth year, and first assistant to Dean Senez. It was under the 

Dean's directions that he acted for the benefit of several thousands of freight handlers employed by the railroad companies, whose yards occupy the west bank of the Hudson River, from Jersey City to Weehawken. The trouble had a small beginning. 

A few workers in the ferry slips of the Pennsylvania Railroad Company at Desbrosses Street in New York City had made a sudden demand for an increase of pay, which was forthwith refused. Labor leaders interested themselves, came from New 

York to Jersey City, and began to set the stage for a sympathetic strike by all freight handlers. On the Pennsylvania

Railroad Company's piers, No. 2 and No. 3, in Jersey City, there were employed many men of St. Mary's parish whose dependent families were sizable; and several of these men were near or past the age when other employment might be obtained easily. A mass meeting of several thousand workers was held in the "peach yard" of the Pennsylvania Railroad Company, at 

Henderson, between First and Second Streets, distant two blocks or more from the church, and Father Brady secured permission to address them. He preached for arbitration, and earnestly exhorted the men not to strike, but to return to their work pending a full consideration of matters by the management of the railroad company. The men were willing, matters were duly considered, increases in pay were granted, and other adjustments were made. It was at about this time that the 

Pennsylvania Company committed itself to the establishment of its relief and pension funds for employees. Other prominent figures in the adjustment of the controversy were: Alexander J. 

Cassatt, president of the railroad company, who was the brother of Mary Cassatt, noted American artist; Frank E. Sheppard, then in charge of the New Jersey Division of the railroad; 

John F. Welch, superintendent of the company's local docks and piers; and Frank McNally, a parishioner at St. Mary's, who was master of the railroad yard at Harsimus, as the neighborhood was still called by railroad employees.41

THE REVEREND PATRICK CORRIGAN

CHAPTER V THE REVEREND PATRICK CORRIGAN 

WHEN, in January, 1876, Father Senez was transferred from St. Mary's to Our Lady of Grace, Hoboken, his successor at 

St. Mary's was Rev. Patrick Corrigan. Father Corrigan, who was thus, in fact, the second pastor of St. Mary's, had long been a familiar figure in Jersey City.¹ 

He was born at or near Ardagh, County Longford, Ireland, on January 1, 1835, and at the age of six was brought to America by his parents. They settled in St. Peter's parish, Jersey 

City, in the early days of the pastorate of Father John Kelly, under whose tutelage young Corrigan grew up and was confirmed. He studied theology at All Hallows, Dublin, and then at St. Mary's, Baltimore, where he was ordained priest on 

June 28, 1860.2 His first mission was as assistant at St. Peter's, from 1860 to 1863, where Father Kelly was then still pastor. 

Next, Father Corrigan was given charge of the Church of the Madonna, at Fort Lee, a parish which then embraced a wide district, including Englewood and Hackensack. At this latter place he organized Holy Trinity Church. In the spring of 

1866, he became pastor of St. Peter's, Jersey City, succeeding 

Father Kelly, who had died poor and in debt. Father Corrigan completed the present St. Peter's Church in December, 1866.7 

The cost of this church was $100,000. Although Father Corrigan was a child of St. Peter's parish, circumstances seem to have made his appointment not altogether popular, and despite his energy, cheerfulness, and activity, he realized that the barriers were irremovable.9 On April 16, 1871, with his assent, St. Peter's parish was placed under the direction of the Jesuit Fathers, who came into possession of property then valued at $250,000, with the understanding that there would be erected a college. Thus, St. Peter's College on Grand Street, built in the middle 1870's, 41

stands as a monument to Father Corrigan's ideas of the necessity of higher education for Catholics.10 After a trip abroad for six months, Father Corrigan began his labors as the first resident pastor at St. Bridget's.¹¹ There he watched his congregation improve materially and spiritually, and give steady evidence of their faith and generosity. While pastor at St. 

Peter's, Father Corrigan had begun to organize St. Bridget's.12

The corner stone of its first church, a small wooden structure on Mercer Street, near Monmouth, had been laid on November 14, 1869.13

In January, 1876, as has been stated, Father Corrigan became pastor of St. Mary's, Jersey City. In the fall of that year he assented to the return of Father Senez, and succeeded him in the Hoboken parish, thereby beginning a pastorate of seventeen years in that city.

As pastor of Our Lady of Grace, Hoboken, after September 1, 1876, Father Corrigan completed the present church there.14 In the late 1880's and early 1890's, he undertook the construction of the large parochial school, the support of which, in good faith but mistakenly, he thought would be borne to a great extent by the public authorities.15

He died on January 9, 1894.16 He was a saint of a jolly type.17 

Father Corrigan was succeeded by Father Charles J. Kelly, long an assistant of Dean Senez at St. Mary's (1885-1894). 

Among Father Kelly's first duties at Our Lady of Grace was that of reopening the school, which had remained closed since the preceding fall.18 So well, however, had the parish been organized by his predecessors, and particularly by Father Cauvin, its first pastor, that apart from establishing the orphans' home, Father Kelly had no building problems of any moment during his pastorate, which ended with his death, at fifty-four years of age, on November 16, 1911.19

Father Patrick Corrigan's pastorate at St. Mary's was marked by his continuance of the efforts of Father Senez to put into effect the orders of Bishop Corrigan against long funeral

THE REVEREND PATRICK CORRIGAN 

43 corteges.20

Among other outstanding incidents of his pastorate were the following: A lecture was delivered by Father 

James Kent Stone, a convert, on April 16, 1876;21 in late May and early June, a mission was conducted by the Jesuit Fathers 

O'Connor, Damien, Sivere, and McCarthy. Some conversions were made, and seven thousand persons received Communion.22 

In the auditorium of the Institute, during this period, there were several entertainments and meetings. For the benefit of the orphans of the parish, a club named "OURS” gave a ball that drew favorable comment from the Evening Journal.23

The orphans themselves gave two entertainments; at one, on 

May 25, 1876, the music was furnished by the boy's band from the Catholic Protectory of New York City.24 On May 22, 1876, there was a concert by Rose D'Evina, an eminent vocalist of the period, who had previously sung in Washington, to the delight of General Sheridan and President Grant.25 On August 31, there was held a political rally of the Hudson County Demo cratic General Committee.26 

1

CHAPTER VI  

THE population of the parish planned in 1859 had grown so fast that by 1867 it had become necessary to organize the new parish of St. Michael's,¹ from which, in turn, St. Lucy's was set up in 1884.2 Nevertheless, though the territory of the original 

St. Mary's parish had been cut in half, its population after 

1867 still grew steadily and became more and more predominantly Irish Catholic. Compactly built tenement houses were erected along Henderson Street and the side streets to the east of it, south of First Street, in the 1860's and the 1870's.³ 

The development of Grove Street, between Newark Avenue and Fourth Street, shows one aspect of the growth of a village into a city. In 1841 there were on this street fewer than half a dozen houses; the neighborhood was rural; Grove Street led, as its name indicates, to groves; and one family, a branch of the original Van Vorsts, owned practically all the land in the neighborhood. By the early sixties, several small houses and stores had been built between Newark Avenue and Bay Street, on both sides of Grove Street. On the east side, between Bay 

Street and First, there was one house; between First and 

Third, there were none. On some of the side streets-notably 

Third, Fourth, and Fifth-comfortable one-family brick dwellings came in steadily between 1855 and 1875. 

About the middle 1870's investors began to erect threefamily houses on the west side of Grove Street, and then sixfamily dwellings. From 1880 to 1890, on the east side of 

Grove Street between Morgan and First Streets, practically all the present brick structures were erected. As late as 1888, however, the entire block, 200 by 400 feet, bounded by Second, Third, Grove, and Henderson Streets, was still in the possession of the Millers, a branch of the Van Vorst family. Then, on this block, within four years, twenty-five apartment houses 44

were built, and more than two hundred growing families, numbering easily a thousand persons, nearly all Irish, came in. 

Between 1890 and 1900 several apartment houses were erected on Bay Street, west of Grove Street; also on First Street, east and west of Grove; on Second Street, east of Grove; and shortly afterwards on Henderson Street, south of Morgan and between First and Second Streets. 

Between 1860 and 1900, it is safe to say that the population in no other part of the city increased more rapidly. The official census for 1860 gave Jersey City a population of 29,266; for 

1890 it was 163,003; for 1900 it was 206,699. Until 1895 the marked increases were in the lower city, which was convenient to lower New York City by means of several ferries. 

This growth, of course, was typical. Its counterparts could then be found in hundreds of neighborhoods in the metropolitan district. And today, even in some parts of upper 

Jersey City the same rapid growth is observable in parishes less than twenty years old. Styles and names have changed slightly however; the "cold water tenements" of the 1860's and 1870's gave way to the "railroad flats" of the 1880's-90's, which, in turn, became the "apartment houses" of the present day. 

Today, in the year of our Lord 1938, the area of St. Mary's 

Parish in Jersey City, Hudson County, New Jersey, is approximately four hundreds acres, or about five-eighths of a square mile. Generally, though not exactly, this is the same ground as the Third Ward of Jersey City, once an election district for freeholders and aldermen (1871-1913). The ward lines are of slight if any significance, since freeholders are now elected in Hudson County at large, and aldermen went out in 1913 when commission government was put into force. The population of the ward is about 15,000. In St. Mary's congregation there are 3000 persons, mainly of Irish extraction. But many 

Catholics of Italian and Polish origin dwell within the boundaries of the parish and attend services in their national churches. An exact estimate of the number of all Catholics in

the parish is difficult to make, they number between 10,000 and 11,000. 7

Within the parish there are fewer than twelve vacant parcels of land, varying from 2500 to 40,000 square feet in area.

One-family houses are now rare, and the number of families in a dwelling average about four. Most parishioners live in "flats," which are built for from four to eight families. Nearly all structures are of brick. There have been no new dwelling houses erected within the last generation. Building has been at ebb for many years except for the construction, say twenty years ago, of a few large mercantile establishments; but alterations and renovations in the old dwellings, especially those originally built for one family, are being made continually. 

The main streets for business are Newark Avenue and Grove 

Street. The first leads west to Newark by way of the "Hill" or upper Jersey City; and the second runs north, to Hoboken. 

Small grocery, meat and candy stores, taverns, spot the parish here and there.8

The parish boundaries are as follows: They begin at the south side of Seventh Street and the shore line of the Hudson River. This north line runs along the south side of Seventh 

Street to Newark Avenue, which joins Seventh Street at a sharp angle. The parish line then runs south-easterly along the north side of Newark Avenue to the east side of Monmouth Street. 

At this point the boundary turns due south along the east side of Monmouth Street and runs to the north side of Railroad Avenue. Here the boundary turns almost due east and runs along the north side of Railroad Avenue to the point where it crosses Newark Avenue near Henderson Street. At this point the boundary runs for a very short distance along the north side of Newark Avenue to Henderson Street. Here it turns north and runs along the west side of Henderson to Morgan

Street. It turns here again and runs east, on Morgan Street to the Hudson River. Both sides of Morgan Street are within St. 

Mary's Parish. The boundary turns at the river and runs north to the south side of Seventh Street, the point or place of beginning. Practically the whole eastern half of this parish territory

is occupied by railroads, manufacturing establishments, abattoirs, and warehouses.10

Before 1870, the Hudson River at high tide came into Harsimus Cove and lapped the natural shore line, which then ran along Henderson Street between Second and Sixth Streets, and along Provost Street between Sixth Street and Pavonia Avenue.¹¹ In that year the Pennsylvania Railroad took possession of most of this water front, filled in the shallow reaches and muddy flats of Harsimus Cove, and built its railroad yard which for more than a generation (1870-1910) was its main freight terminal in the East. Through this railroad yard were shuttled the products of the nation: grains from the West; cotton and fruits from the South; steel and oil and coal from the Pennsylvania fields; manufactured goods from the East and New England; and importations from Europe, Asia, Africa, the West Indies, South America, and Central America. Differ ent parts of this railroad yard had specific names; for instance, there was "the peach yard" where in season horse-drawn trucks from New York were loaded with peaches sent by rail from

Delaware. 

One thousand feet beyond Seventh Street, the northern boundary of the parish, is the elevated main line of the Erie Railroad. To the immediate north of that are the wide highways leading to and from the Holland Tunnel, which connects 

New Jersey and New York. Beyond these highways, still farther north about one thousand feet, is the elevated main line of the Lackawanna Railroad. The Pennsylvania Railroad, now elevated, runs along Railroad Avenue, the main southerly boundary of the parish. An entrance to the Hudson and Manhattan Railroad, the Hudson Tubes, is on Grove Street, at Railroad Avenue. About a mile to the south are the yards of the Central Railroad of New Jersey. This, by way of the Junction Railroad of New Jersey, connects with the West Shore and New York Central Railroads at Weehawken, about five miles to the north. St. Mary's Parish, for many years the most densely populated section of Jersey City, is thus in the very heart of a continental railroad center.12

Schools 

The earliest trace of popular education in Jersey City is of a school of twenty-three pupils and one teacher, established about 1820, probably on Newark Avenue near Warren Street. 

The original Public School No. 1 on York Street, was opened in 1848. It was built on part of the site of the present school, which was erected in 1897.13

The first public school within the present bounds of St. 

Mary's Parish was originally the private enterprise of Isaac Coriell, and was located on Third Street. In 1851 it was known as Public School No. 1 of Van Vorst Township, although not owned by the Township. It was at that time attended by 474 pupils. In November, 1855, this school was replaced by Public School No. 2 of Jersey City, situated on the east side of 

Erie between Third and Fourth Streets.14 This school of 1855, with its several later enlargements, was demolished in 1902, when the present school No. 2 was erected.15 The first School 

No. 4, on Eighth between Grove and Henderson Streets, was erected in 1868, demolished in 1917, and replaced by the present No. 4, which was opened in the fall of 1919.16 

In the late 1830's or early 1840's, what may be considered the first Catholic parish day-school was organized in the basement of a house on Newark Avenue near Warren Street. It was under the direction of John Carr, who, at his death, was succeeded by Patrick Buckley. Subsequently, Morgan Nolan was the teacher.

After the first St. Peter's Church was completed, on Grand 

Street near Washington, the children were taught in its basement by Timothy McCarthy. The school was next quartered in Washington Temperance Hall, at Gregory and Henderson

Streets. Then the basement of the old church having been fitted up, the school was moved back there, still in charge of Mr. McCarthy. James Brann succeeded Mr. McCarthy, and with the assistance of several women teachers, remained principal for several years, until 1861, when the parish school at York and Van Vorst Streets was built. The instruction of the

children was then given over wholly to the Sisters of Charity and the Christian Brothers.17 

In the 1850's there were numerous small private schools in lower Jersey City. The Misses Chaydeanes kept a school at 

Grand and Greene Streets, which was maintained as late as 

1870. In 1856, Washington Hasbrouck opened at Mercer and

Barrow Streets a school for boys and girls, which, in 1866, was removed to the Lyceum Building on the south side of Grand, west of Washington Street. Here it continued under his management until 1876, a year after the Jersey City High School for boys and girls was opened on Bay, near Grove Street.18 On 

September 2, 1878, the Society of Jesus opened St. Peter's College, on Grand Street.19 Messrs. Miller and Stimets, who succeeded Mr. Hasbrouck, kept his school on Grand Street until 

1892, when they opened the new Hasbrouck Institute, at Harrison and Crescent Avenues. This was demolished about 1913, when the city began to acquire the site of the present Lincoln High School, which was opened in September, 1919.2 20 Between 1845 and 1870, in the more immediate vicinity of the present St. Mary's Church, several small private schools were conducted. Among them were Mrs. Gregg's on Sixth near 

Coles Street; Mr. Smith's on Grove near Fifth Street; and Mr.

Healy's on Wayne near Henderson Street. These were generally attended by the younger neighborhood boys and girls. 

Mr. Smith's school, opened in 1848, was attended mainly by Catholics.21

In connection with St. Mary's Chapel, built by Father John 

Kelly in 1855 at Tenth and Erie Streets, there seems to have been maintained, probably at Grove and Fifteenth Streets, a day-school for Catholic children.22 The older boys would have been taught by men and the girls and younger boys by women.23 

Information about this school is very slight, and the names of its lay teachers are lost to memory. 

It is noteworthy that in 1857 a census of children in Jersey City between the ages of five and eighteen years, showed a total of 5700. Of this number, 2200 attended the public schools. 

After allowing for those who were employed and those who

attended private (probably including the Catholic) schools, it was estimated that from one thousand to two thousand children were deprived of instruction because the schools in the city were inadequate.24

There are indications that a private academy for Catholic girls and boys was conducted as early as 1861, probably on the west side of Henderson near Eighth Street, by the Sisters of 

Charity from Mount St. Vincent, New York. In 1862 their establishment was at Jersey Avenue near Second Street, where they continued their labors and dwelt until 1906. The fortieth annual commencement of St. Mary's Academy was held on 

June 4, 1902.25

After St. Mary's Church was opened at Second and Erie 

Streets in 1863, the first church, at Tenth and Erie Streets, was used until 1866 as a parochial school; the Sisters of Charity taught the girls and younger boys, and laymen taught the older boys.20 On November 15, 1867, this building apparently became the first St. Michael's Church,27

The Catholic Institute 

On September 1, 1866, under the direction of Mr. Arthur 

Henry Dundon as principal, the Catholic Institute of St. Mary's

Parish was opened for the first time. It was continued until 

1915, when the building was demolished to make way for the present parish school. The Institute, to use its familiar name, which was given to it by the late Rt. Rev. Henry A. Brann, occupied a plot on the south side of Third Street, about midway between Grove and Erie Streets. It adjoined the brownstone rectory at 219 Third Street, which was built in 1868-1869 by Father Senez. The Institute too had a brownstone front, and it was four stories high. On the ground floor was a well-arranged auditorium, about fifty feet wide and eighty feet deep, with good-sized stage. Long wooden benches gave a seating capacity of approximately one thousand.28 The two upper floors of the building, which together contained nine classrooms, were used by the girls and very young boys; on the first floor were five rooms used by the older boys. There are recollections of very

early days when the top floor, then used by the smaller boys, was one large room with a blackboard in the center, around which gathered the pupils of various grades.29 The entrance for the girls and younger boys was the eastern doorway; the central doorway led to the auditorium; and the older boys entered by the western doorway.30 In 1884, there were thirteen hundred pupils under the instruction of five Christian Brothers and fifteen Sisters of Charity.³¹

Mr. Dundon, in his instruction of the boys, was assisted by other laymen, among whom were Mr. Cahill, and Hugh 

Coyle, the latter a native of Scottstown, County Monaghan, Ireland, who had had four years' experience as a teacher there.32 

Twenty years later, in 1886, Mr. Coyle was selected as the first president of St. Mary's Holy Name Society, which was the first branch of that Society established in Hudson County. He was at that time engaged in the undertaking business, and was a leading parishioner at St. Mary's until his death. Father 

Brann, during his curacy at St. Mary's, taught boys at the Institute.

The Christian Brothers 

In 1869, at the invitation of Father Senez, the Christian Brothers came to St. Mary's School.33 The first Director was 

Brother Bardomian. He was succeeded, in 1874, by Brother 

Abdonas Lewis, educated in Europe, and formerly an officer in the English Army. During his directorship the name "The 

Catholic Institute" was generally adopted, and the school became well known throughout the diocese. Pupils came from every parish in the city, and from several outlying communities. 

On January 14, 1886, Brother Lewis, though apparently in robust health, suffered an apoplectic stroke, and, while classes were in session, died suddenly. His successor was Brother 

Ambrose. It was Brother Ambrose who initiated the St. Patrick's Day play, which for more than fifty years had been an annual occurrence, to the substantial financial benefit of the parish. 

Brother Ambrose was succeeded by Brother Gustavus. The next Director was Brother Julian, who established the Holy

Name Society in the school. Then came Brother Andelinian 

John, who made the Institute a center for mathematical study. 

In 1901 Brother Denis Edward became Director; and after him came Brothers Gratian, Gregory, Nazienzus, and Giles Raymond.

Brother Gerardian was Director when the present school building was opened in 1916-1917. Following him were: 

Brothers Gabriel Thomas, David John, F. Lucian, Adrian, and

Malachy. Probably the best remembered and most popular of all the Brothers was Brother Malachy, who assumed charge as Director in 1925 after a service of twenty years as a teacher in the old Institute and in the new school.34

In 1927, the Christian Brothers faced many demands to supply teachers for the increasing number of parochial high schools in the eastern part of the United States, and very reluctantly they withdrew their group of teachers from St. Mary's. The association of the Brothers with the parish had covered a constant period of almost sixty years, and was probably then the longest of record in America. It was a traditional judgment of the Brothers that they had always found at St. Mary's a remarkable cooperation on the part of the clergy and the people, and that deeper friendship and kinder hospitality were to be found in no other parish.35

The Mount Sisters 

Sisters of Charity from Emmitsburg, Maryland, the foundation of Mother Elizabeth Seton, were established in Cincinnati as early as 1829; and in 1844-46, the period of the first affiliation of Father Senez with that diocese, they were conducting in that city an academy, a school, and an asylum.38 In 1853, the first year of Father Senez' pastorate at St. John's, Paterson, he placed in charge of the parish school Sisters from Mount St. 

Vincent, New York.3 37 

On his return to Cincinnati, in 1858, Father Senez found that the Sisters there, like those in New York, had separated from the Sisters at Emmitsburg.38 Probably he had some part in the arrangements which, in 1858, resulted in Bishop Bayley send-

ing from Newark five candidates to be trained at Cincinnati, under Mother Margaret.39 She had known Mother Elizabeth

Ann Bayley Seton, who was an aunt of Bishop Bayley. These five candidates later became the nucleus of the New Jersey Community of the Sisters of Charity known as the Madison or 

Convent Sisters.40 The Sisters of Charity of Mount St. Vincent, New York, familiarly known as the Mount Sisters, opened their mission in St. Mary's parish in 1860. Their first undertaking was probably the asylum. They withdrew in 1906. During this period of forty-six years they conducted the orphanage, an academy, and the parochial school for girls and the younger boys. Their activities, qualities, and influence, have been related by their own historian:41

The Superiors directing the mission [at St. Mary's] under the administration of the Mount St. Vincent Sisters were Sister Mary Basilia 

McCann, who had charge until her death, October 27, 1870, and Sister Francis Assisium Madden, her successor, who remained in office until 1906. 

Sister Basilia was a notable figure in her day. She had been a pupil at old St. Joseph's, in Emmitsburg, where Mother Seton founded the Community of the American Sisters of Charity in 1809. She was prepared for her first Holy Communion by Mother Seton herself. In 1829, at the age of eighteen, she entered the Novitiate at St. Joseph's. In due time she was sent to the Community's first mission in Philadelphia, and thence was transferred to New York, where she labored zealously at the Prince Street Orphan Asylum, as part of the brave little band under Sister, later Mother, Elizabeth Boyle. When, in 1846, it was decided to establish a separate Mother-house in New York, and the Sisters engaged in that diocese were permitted to make their choice as to whether they would remain in New York or return to Emmitsburg, Sister Basilia was one of the number deciding to remain in the North. Three years later, Archbishop Walsh of Halifax having asked for Sisters for his diocese, Sister Basilia was chosen, with three others, to make the new foundation. In 1858 she returned to Mount St. Vincent, remaining there until her appointment as Superior of St. Mary's, Jersey City, in 

1860. 

Sister Basilia, whose motherly love for little children bereft of their parents had been such a blessing to the scores of little orphans that had been under her care for so many years, brought to her new charge this

great gift of sympathy for such work, that only God can bestow. Added to her wide range of experience, she had a remarkable capacity for administration, but, better still, all who knew her throughout her religious life, spoke of her as “a real Sister of Charity." The diary of her contemporary Sisters describes her as follows: "She was tall, erect, had a dark complexion, with very piercing black eyes which would kindle in disapproval or beam with sympathy when occasion called." 

Her successor, Sister Francis Assisium is, no doubt, still remembered by many of St. Mary's parishioners. She may truly be called an apostle to New Jersey, for forty-three years of her religious life were given to the people of that State, and forty-one of these to St. Mary's parish. 

Having entered the Sisters of Charity at Mount St. Vincent in 1859, she was assigned to duty at Paterson, New Jersey, in 1860. In 1861, she was transferred to St. Mary's, Jersey City. The following year she was chosen to serve as a nurse to the wounded soldiers brought from the Civil War battlefields, as the Community had been requested by the United States Government to give assistance. The Sisters shortly before had moved from their first Mother-house at McGowan's Pass, now within the limits of Central Park, and the building was converted into a Military Hospital. In 1863, Sister Assisium returned to St. Mary's, Jersey City, remaining there until 1868, when she was transferred to New York. In 1870, Sister Assisium was brought back once more to 

St. Mary's, this time as Superior. Here she again proved herself a true mother to the orphans, as well as to the Sisters under her charge. Indeed, motherliness and holiness combined might be set down as her most impressive characteristics. One felt that she had a warm, gracious, benevolent love to give to all who needed it on earth, and yet one felt equally that her gaze was fixed on God alone. 

Sister Assisium died in the ninetieth year of her age, and the sixty-sixth of her religious life, at St. Joseph's Hospital, Yonkers, where she had been an invalid for some years. Sister Assisium's grave is marked by a granite tablet presented by the United States Government in recognition of her work for the Civil War soldiers. This civic service gave her a place, together with thirteen other members of her Community, among the Nuns of the Battlefield, whose memory has been perpetuated by a monument erected in Washington, in the District of Columbia. 

St. Mary's Orphan Asylum was maintained as a parish charity from 1861 to 1927. Before Father Senez came to Jersey City in the spring of 1859, Father John Kelly, pastor of St. Peter's, of

which St. Mary's Chapel at Tenth and Erie Streets was a mission, desired that an orphan asylum be established for the whole city, but as he was not persona grata to Bishop Bayley, the Bishop did not second Father Kelly's plan. After Father 

Senez came to Jersey City, Father Kelly again offered to undertake the enterprise, but again the Bishop declined the offer. He permitted Father Senez to build the asylum and make it parochial instead of general. Father Kelly might thus be regarded as the first promoter of that noble work.42

It is worthy of remark that as chaplain of St. Mary's Asylum, the late Monsignor Charles A. Kelly first learned to appreciate the possibilities for the development of the good inherent in children so circumstanced; that he carried into his work, as pastor of Our Lady of Grace in Hoboken, both the ideas and ideals which had been implanted in his soul by Father Senez; that the orphanage which he founded in Hoboken became, so to speak, a blessed replica of that at St. Mary's; and that he, in his paternal attitude, reflected faithfully the spirit of the old pastor in Jersey City. The orphans shared with the other children of the parish whatever advantages were offered.¹³ 

In the 1880's and 1890's, the Mount Sisters cared yearly, upon an average, for 125 orphans. Some (probably most) came from St. Mary's parish, but priests of other parishes often brought in needy dependent children if no provision could be made elsewhere. Among these clergymen in Jersey City were 

Rt. Rev. Robert Seton of St. Joseph's, Rt. Rev. Patrick W. 

Smith of St. John's, and Rt. Rev. Joseph H. Meehan of All

Saints'. They always contributed to the care of the orphans brought by them to the asylum. Many parishioners were generous, furnishing provisions, clothing material, and other items. 

Among the donors were Mr. McEntee, Mr. McCarren, and the members of a family named Reilly. Under the Mount Sisters, the orphans were taught in separate classes provided in the asylum, and did not attend the Institute on Third Street which was the parish school. 

Memories still endure of Sister Sarah Agnes starting upon her errands of charity every afternoon after school, at precisely

three o'clock; and from her the younger Sisters learned their lessons of charity to the sick poor.44 

The first house used (in the 1860's) by the Mount Sisters at Second Street and Jersey Avenue was an old mansion. In 

1882 an extension was built for a chapel. Later, across the yard to the west, on Second Street, a separate house was erected. 

This was used for classrooms and a workshop where industries were carried on to help support the orphans. Then a private house, on the immediate west, was purchased from William and Margaret Ryan. This house was moved to the rear of the 

Ryan plot (1887) and used for a laundry and quarantine. On the front of the plot, facing Second Street, a new building was then erected, three stories in height. The activities carried on by the Sisters were the same as those of the earlier periodparish school, orphanage, and academy.

In the Academy eight Sisters taught. There were eight classes, attended by approximately one hundred pupils. Elementary schooling was the usual extent of the instruction to both boys and girls; for the latter, more advanced classes were provided which covered what is now considered a two-year high-school course. For individual pupils, tuition in music and art was available. The students were chiefly day students; a very small number of girls were received as boarders. Tuition (the Sisters have no record of the terms) was probably at the rate of twenty dollars a year. 

Thirty years after she had left Jersey City, Sister Margaret

Agnes, a former teacher at the Academy at Jersey Avenue and Second Street, still treasured memories of the kindnesses shown the Mount Sisters by Philip McEntee, a type of parochial ancient, whose large family of children—and some of their children-had all been under the Sisters' instruction. In a letter to his last surviving child, a daughter, Sister Margaret Agnes expressed her particular delight in the memory of the last morning of the Mount Sisters in Jersey City, when Mr. 

McEntee had walked to Mass with Sister Francis Assisium, and afterwards had remained on the steps of a house opposite the old convent on Jersey Avenue, to watch their final departure

from the parish where they had so nobly dedicated the best years of their lives in caring for the fatherless and in showing little children the way to God.45

Catholic Influence in Public Schools 

It is appropriate to note other Catholic influences upon education in lower Jersey City.

Public Schools No. 2 (on Erie Street near Third) and No. 

5 (a primary school on Bay Street near Erie), were always within the confines of St. Mary's parish. School No. 4 (on 

Eighth Street near Grove) was within the original parish boundaries; and the grammar department of School No. 1 (on 

York Street near Washington) was attended by children living on the southern side of the parish. In these public schools of lower Jersey City, from almost the earliest times, there were Catholic men and women teachers under whose supervision and instruction came children who did not attend parish schools. 

Arthur H. Dundon, first principal of St. Mary's Institute (1866-1868), was likewise the first principal of Public School No. 4 (1868-1870). Edward Kelly, educated in Ireland, was the second principal of School No. 4, from 1870 to 1907. At his death, in 1912, he was a trustee of St. Michael's Church. 

James T. Mackey, a pupil at St. Mary's Catholic Institute in its early days, taught Greek and Latin in the Jersey City High School in the 1890's when it occupied the two upper stories of 

School No. 5 on Bay Street. He succeeded Mr. Kelly as third principal of No. 4 in 1907, and served there until 1918. 

James J. Hopkins (brother of Hon. Mary T. Norton, Member of Congress), educated at St. Peter's, Holy Cross, and Seton 

Hall Colleges, and a charter member of the oldest chapter of the Jersey City Knights of Columbus, was principal of School 

No. 2 from 1893 to 1897, of the Jersey City High School from 

1897 to 1903, and of the Dickinson High School from 1903 to 1923. In this latter year, shortly before his death, Mr. Hopkins was appointed Superintendent of the Jersey City Public 

Schools.

Four sisters-Susan, Nellie, Annie, and Marion Dutch-of a St. Mary's family, taught in the schools for different periods, from the late 1860's to the second decade of this century. 

Susan (Mrs. Thomas Keary, wife of a master carpenter upon the church built by Father Senez) taught at No. 2; Nellie taught at No. 4, and was principal at No. 21; Annie (Mrs. William Bubier) taught at No. 3; and Marion (Mrs. Miller) at 

No. 21. Misses Margaret and Catharine Jordan, of Fifth Street near Erie, about the early 1880's taught respectively in No. 1 and No. 2. Mrs. Nelson Vanderhoff (Sarah Birkbeck) taught at No. 2 in the late 1870's; the Birkbecks were among the oldest families in the parish. Mrs. Andrew A. Daly, Sr., (Susan 

McHorney) taught at No. 4 in the early 1880's. Mrs. James F.

Gannon, Sr., (Ida Rouget), of an old family in St. Peter's parish which was prominent in business in the lower city, before her marriage taught in No. 1, and before 1880 was probably the first Catholic teacher in the Jersey City High School. Theresa Coppinger likewise taught in No. I and in the Jersey City 

High School. Mrs. Alice Beirne Rankin, mother of Father 

Rush Rankin, S.J., taught in No. 5 in the 1880's, and in the 

1890's and 1900's was a prominent member of the faculty of the Jersey City Training School for Teachers, in which many young Catholic ladies were prepared for their vocations as teachers in the grade schools. Miss Daisy L. Kennedy, prominent among the ladies of the Catholic Club, taught in No. 2 for nearly thirty-five years preceding her retirement in the 

1900's. Miss May L. Sheridan, who recently retired as a teacher in the Lincoln High School, first taught in No. 2, and later in the Jersey City High School on Bay Street, and in the 

Dickinson High School. Other Catholic women of St. Mary's parish who taught in No. 2, were Agnes Reilly, Harriet Symes (Mrs. John Howley), Catherine Brady, Emma Boulanger, Mary Dugan (the second Mrs. Robert Davis), Mrs. Anna 

Brown, who was later principal of No. 19, Anna and Kate 

Rafter (daughters of James Rafter, a trustee of St. Mary's), Jennie McAghon, and M. Ethel Daly. Primary principals at 

No. 2 were Julia A. Minihan (1902-1915), Julia C. Harney

(1915-1926), and Margaret C. Hamill (1926-1934). Mary 

McNamara who recently (1934) retired as principal of No. 6, was educated at St. Mary's parish school, taught at No. 2 for many years, and was its vice-principal in 1911. In that year, nine out of the fourteen teachers in that school were Catholics; of these, five were members of St. Mary's Church. At School 

No. 4, fifteen out of twenty-two teachers were then Catholics. 

Among them were the Misses Mary and Madeline Garry (daughters of John and Agnes Martin Garry, who were two of the oldest parishioners of St. Mary's), and Eleanor Farley, now the wife of James A. Nugent, Superintendent of Schools. Her parents were likewise among the oldest parishioners at St. 

Mary's. Miss Minnie V. Shanley, who was assistant superintendent of public schols at the time of her death in 1936, was almost a lifelong resident in St. Mary's parish. Sixteen other ladies who taught in the public schools in 1911 were at that time members of St. Mary's Church. All in all, the children at the public schools in the vicinity of St. Mary's parish, continually had before them as examples of Catholic living and devotion, teachers who were excellent instructors and molders of character.46

Parish Sunday School 

In 1863 the Sunday School of St. Mary's parish listed 42 teachers and 650 pupils. The average attendance was 587 pupils. Of these, 183 were studying Bible History. There were in the infant class, 234 enrolled.47

Thomas J. Kennedy, a bookkeeper in a publishing house in 

New York City, settled in St. Mary's parish about 1875. Interested in the religious education of children, he thought that provision should be made for the instruction of Catholic boys who attended the public schools in the vicinity of St. Mary's 

Church. He requested Father Senez that he be permitted to conduct a Sunday School for these boys. His request was granted, and to him was given complete control in all matters connected with this laudable work. A few gentlemen offered their services as teachers, and the Sunday School for public school boys was maintained for about twenty years (1875-

1895) in the basement of the church. There, after the celebration of Mass for the children at nine o'clock, instruction in catechism was given to the Catholic boys who attended the public schools.

The boys were asked to contribute one cent a week if possible, and the money thus collected was used to furnish catechisms, prayer books, and beads. In June, at the end of each school year, there would be competitive oral examinations. 

Small money awards, ranging from ten cents to a dollar, would be made, according to the proficiency shown by the pupils. 

Reading material in the way of simple religious magazines was from time to time distributed among them. The attendance varied from twenty-five to sixty.48

In those days, in the Institute, there was generally held Sunday School for the children of the parochial school, both boys and girls; and girls from the public schools were there given instruction in catechism by the Sisters of Charity. 

Circulating Library 

Under Father Senez, Mr. Kennedy, about 1878, conducted a circulating library. The first eight hundred books had been obtained in 1863; they were given by the children of the Sunday 

School.49 A weekly charge of five cents a volume provided for new acquistions. Every Sunday afternoon from three to five o'clock, Mr. Kennedy and an assistant would attend at the library, which was kept in a small basement room in an extension on the east side of the church. This library extension opened upon the basement near the altar, just as the baptistry on the floor above opened upon the main church. Among Mr. 

Kennedy's co-workers was Mr. Albert Birkbeck, still alive (1938) at seventy-seven years of age. He taught under Mr. 

Kennedy from 1875 to 1878, and had charge of the library from 1878 to 1880. Other assistants in the library were John J. 

Murphy, now Monsignor, and the late Hon. Joseph F. Farmer,

Judge of the Second Criminal Court of Jersey City. An assist ant catechist and librarian in the early 1890's was the late John F. Carey, a letter carrier, who became prominent in building

and loan circles and social and religious confraternities in the Greenville section of Jersey City. As far as can be ascertained, the library and the Sunday instruction of the public school boys were discontinued in the early 1890's, when Mr. Kennedy decided to spend his Sundays at home. No one was found to succeed him, altogether there were many requests for the continuance of these good works. Probably the books of the library became part of that of the Catholic Club, which was opened in 1893.

The development of the parish circulating library by Father Senez, and Mr. Kennedy's participation in it, were signs of the times. In 1866, in Bergen Township, the Bergen Library Association had been formed; and about a year later, there was a similar effort made in Hudson City, which, however, lasted only a few years. In 1873 the Board of Education established a library at the Jersey City High School on Bay Street, but there too interest died out. That library was open only on Saturdays, and the general impression mistakenly was that it was intended for only teachers and pupils. The Board of Education ceased to make appropriations.50

In 1879 the P. Lorillard Company settled in Jersey City. 

About 1884 it established for its workers, many of whom were members of St. Mary's Parish, a library at Boeraem Hall at 

Grove Street and Newark Avenue, where it also maintained an evening school until the middle 1890's. The libraries of the High School and of the P. Lorillard Company became, in 

1891, the nucleus of the present Jersey City Free Public 

Library.51 

Among those who in the early days gave books to the Public Library was Father Patrick Hennessy, pastor of St. Patrick's Church.52 A member of its first board of trustees was 

Michael Murray, a cashier of the Equitable Life Insurance Company, who resided in St. Bridget's Parish.53 The outstanding figure in the Public Library for a score of years was its President, Leonard J. Gordon, an official of the P. Lorillard Company. 

54 Since the rise of the Free Public Library in the 1890's, and the erection, about 1900, of the present city library building

at Montgomery Street and Jersey Avenue, within easy reach of St. Mary's parishioners, there has been no actual need for a parish library.

Institute Hall 

In its early days, the Catholic Institute was one of the largest public buildings of Jersey City, and at times its auditorium, called Institute Hall, was used for fairs, meetings, and other assemblies.55 Upon the completion of this building in 1866, there was held a fair, the proceeds of which were used to pay for the building in part. The success of the fair, which netted several thousand dollars, was due mainly to the energy of the late Rt. Rev. Henry A. Brann, then an assistant to Father Senez.

Father Brann was a son of James Brann, one of the early schoolteachers at St. Peter's Church, and was successful in raising among the Catholics in Jersey City other large sums of money which went toward the erection of the Institute.56 

On June 12, 1867, the ladies of Grace Church (Van Vorst), the neighboring Episcopal Church organized in 1847, held in the newly completed Institute a strawberry festival; and from the tenth to the fourteenth of December of the same year, they held there their annual fair, open both afternoon and evening, for the sale of "useful and ornamental articles," with a supper furnished each evening, for an admission of twenty-five cents for adults and ten cents for children.57 

On November 2, 1867, the Institute housed a Republican meeting addressed by the Honorable Frederick F. Frelinghuysen, one of the ablest and most distinguished men the State of New Jersey has produced. He was Attorney-General of New 

Jersey from 1861-1866, United States Senator from New Jersey from 1866-1869, and again from 1871-1877. He was a member of the Electoral Commission which decided the dispute between Hayes and Tilden as to the presidency. Later he served as Secretary of State under President, Arthur, who offered him the position of Chief Justice of the Supreme Court, which he did not accept. Earlier, in 1870, he had been named Minister to England, but declined to serve.58

On November 26, 1867, almost a year before the election of 

General Grant as President of the United States, the General 

Grant Club held at the Institute a meeting to further Grant's nomination. Early in 1868, when the railroads laid their plans to make the eastern margin of the parish a railroad yard by appropriating an area equal to thirty-five city blocks, there were spirited anti-monopoly meetings held in the Institute. A meeting held on March 26, 1868, was suddenly adjourned by Mayor Gopsill because a speaker openly advocated killing the local State Senator, who was favoring the railroads.59 

In the 1870's and 1880's, speakers of repute spoke in the hall of the Institute, sometimes as lecturers, sometimes as spellbinders. Among the latter was Henry Ward Beecher, who in the presidential campaign of 1884 spoke in support of Grover Cleveland, the successful candidate. His opponents in that campaign were James G. Blaine, Republican candidate, and 

Benjamin F. Butler, Greenback candidate. Apparently without the knowledge of Father Senez, and to the great dismay of the Democrats in the community, Father Carroll, then a young curate, attended a Butler meeting held in the Institute and spoke vigorously in his behalf. This unusual performance brought a delegation of Democrats the next day who complained to the pastor. His remark, if tradition is correct, was that Father Carroll had lost some of his usefulness, and that thereafter no political meeting of any kind would be held in the Institute. Father Carroll continued as an assistant for several years, but no political meetings were held at the Institute during the remaining years of the life of Father Senez.60 In the early 1900's, however, the hall was at the disposal, at a price, of both Democrats and Republicans in both national and local campaigns. The last noted speaker there was Raymond Robins of Chicago. In 1907, he spoke in behalf of Mark 

M. Fagan, the second Catholic mayor of Jersey City (19011907), then running (unsuccessfully) for his fourth term as mayor of the city.61 This was six years before the adoption of the present form of Commission Government, when Mr. 

Fagan was again elected mayor (1913-1917).

Plays and entertainments by the pupils of the school and the academy were given frequently year after year in the auditorium of the old Institute. In the 1870's many Catholics gathered there on special occasions. On May 12, 1873, the Catholic 

Union of New Jersey (1873-1876), established in the larger parishes of the diocese for the protection of Catholic interests, delivered at the Institute an address of congratulation to 

Bishop Corrigan, then recently consecrated."

St. Francis Hospital 

62 There have been religiously preserved the letters and documents concerning the early activities in America of the Sisters of the Poor of St. Francis; and from these a historian of that congregation has drawn most of the following narrative: 

Our Reverend Foundress, Mother Frances Shervier, 64 arrived in America for her first visitation on June 24, 1863. She embarked for 

Europe on August 29, 1863. On this voyage our Mother traveled alone. 

The Chronicles relate: 

"All deeply felt and regretted this. But God foresaw, and in 

His infinite kindness came to their assistance; for when the Sisters, sad and anxious, accompanied dear Mother on board the vessel, to their great joy they met Reverend Father Senez, Pastor of St. Mary's Church in Jersey, who at once took dear Mother under his care."

A letter of our revered Foundress, written on board the ship, reveals her gratitude, as the following lines will show: "Sister Felicitas will have told you of the good company I have found and all I can say regarding it, is that I heartily thank our Saviour for it every day. The good Father keeps me so engaged that I can scarcely do anything else than say the prayers I am obliged to. At one time he reads in the German language and 

I have to make corrections. Then I have to do the reading, and then he complains that I read too rapidly. We then entertain each other in French, when I have to translate what was said into German. The conversations of the good gentleman are extremely useful and edifying. In all justice he can be called a true man of God." 

In a second letter we find: "Father Senez is very kind to me; without him I do not know what I would have done on this English vessel."

It was at the southwest corner of Coles and Fourth Streets that St. Francis Hospital was founded in 1864 by the Sisters of the Poor of St. Francis. The first establishment was in a three-story frame building, which had previously been a private dwelling of unpretentious character. The building still exists. (1938), but the ground floor is now a store. There was in 

1864 no private institution of the kind in New Jersey except St. Mary's Hospital in Hoboken, founded by the same order of 

Sisters on January 8, 1863.66 The foundation of St. Francis

Hospital preceded by four years that of the City Hospital of 

Jersey City.67

The Order of the Sisters of the Poor of St. Francis was established in France, in 1845, in the city of Aix-la-Chappelle. It is definitely settled that the first representatives of the Order in America arrived in Cincinnati in August, 1858, and shortly afterwards took up temporary quarters there in a dwelling not far from St. Thomas' Church, of which Father Senez was then pastor. The honor of bringing this Order to America belongs to Mrs. Sarah Peters of Cincinnati, a convert, who was an outstanding figure in Catholic charitable circles of Ohio in the third quarter of the nineteenth century. Father Senez apparently was aware of the excellent work of the Order in Cincinnati, and when the opportunity came, he secured, in 1864, from the mother-house in Cincinnati, the first four Sisters of the Order who came to Jersey City.

Part of the story is in the Chronicles of the Sisters of the 

Poor of St. Francis:

At the beginning of January, 1864, Father Senez urgently begged that Sisters, previously appointed, should be sent at once to Jersey City. 

Sister Dominica's answer regarding this request was as follows:

REVEREND FATHER: 

"Cincinnati, Ohio, January 9, 1864.

Well aware that the winter season is the hardest for the poor to make a living, I would be much inclined to send you at once three or four of our Sisters.

Grave reasons, however, compel me to check my wishes and to delay for a few weeks. One reason is that I must consult Rev-

erend Mother, regarding the regulations in this matter. I have already written to her, and do not doubt but that she will gladly consent to your wishes. Convinced of this, and trusting in God's assistance, I appoint the first week after Easter as the time of our arrival in Jersey City; when we will begin to practice the duties of our holy vocation. I hope, Reverend Father, that you are satisfied with this arrangement, and will recognize our good will in the matter. 

In one of her letters, Reverend Mother calls you her Archangel Raphael, and she expresses therein her deep feeling of gratitude to God who sent you, Reverend Sir, to be, as it were, her visible Guardian Angel on the journey. Our whole Community on this account feels indebted to you for the kind assistance you afforded to her. For this kindness I express to you, in the name of the 

Community of which I am privileged to be a member, our sincere thanks, and join thereto our poor prayers that God may shower abundant graces and blessings upon you. I have communicated to Reverend Mother your permission for her to write to you, and I am convinced that she will make use of it at the first opportunity. 

After completing our preparations, I shall inform you as soon as possible of the day of our arrival in Jersey City. Mrs. Peter requests me to send you her hearty congratulations upon your happy return. 

Commending ourselves to your holy prayers, I remain your humble servant,

SISTER DOMINICA OF ST. FRANCIS." 

In the evening of Low Sunday, April 3, 1864, the first Sunday after Easter, Sisters Blanca, Theresia, and Aegidia, who had been appointed for the foundation in Jersey City, left Cincinnati. Accompanied by good Sister Felicitas, they arrived at their destination on Tuesday morning, April 5, 1864.

Father Senez met them at the railroad station and cordially expressed his joy at their arrival. Sister Blanca had been appointed Superioress by the Reverend Mother. The beginning in Jersey City, though modest and poor and filled with the hardships and difficulties always so necessary for the successful accomplishment of any good work, was nevertheless consoling and fraught with blessings, as far as the narrow field of labor would permit. Then, too, the Sisters felt themselves well protected under the paternal care of Father Senez. The first dwell-

ing of the Sisters was only temporarily given for their use through the kindness of the Halliard family,68 who, in the meantime, had to occupy another house. In spite of the limited space, several sick women were at once admitted. About three months later, Father Senez was able to provide for the Sisters the house at Fourth and Coles Streets. It was furnished in the best possible manner for the sick and for the reception of destitute children. The care of such children in Jersey City was a work that could not then be avoided. 

In the world, Sister Blanca was known as Anna Catherine

Muhr, Sister Felicitas as Amelia Dorst, Sister Theresia as Magdalena Lenz. Their birthplaces were in Germany. Sister 

Aegidia was known as Elise Condé; her birthplace was Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania. 

Sisters Felicitas and Blanca had come to America in 1858 and 1861 respectively, after having entered the Congregation in Germany. Sisters Theresia had emigrated to America several years before becoming a religious. She died in Jersey City on 

January 27, 1866, of typhus fever, which was then raging.

The first hospital at Coles and Fourth Streets was fitted with a veranda and a paved courtyard. The four courageous nuns installed half a dozen beds, and took upon themselves the endless labor and difficulty of setting the new institution upon its feet. The rent was often paid by friends of the Sisters. Toward the end of the Civil War, this first St. Francis Hospital sheltered fifty or sixty children of Union soldiers killed in battle or dying subsequently of their wounds. Their mothers had been lost to them or were unable to maintain the family burden.

At first the scope of the Sisters' activities was limited, and a fairly accurate description of the hospital would be an "asylum for the sick," as the Sisters performed their labors mainly outside the institution, visiting the homes of the helpless poor, attending the sick in their own beds, and taking in under their own roof the helpless orphans to whom they gave a common

school education. In the case of the girls, they trained them in the essentials of housekeeping. During the first seven years of its existence, they admitted only women and children, and sent the male patients to St. Mary's in Hoboken. There were no regular attending physicians, but the Sisters in their work of nursing relied on professional assistance by occasionally, as necessity demanded, calling in doctors. 

During the first year of its existence, only twenty-five patients were treated in the first hospital on Coles Street; in the second year, forty-five; in the third year, thirty-eight; and in the fourth year, thirty-five. This year, 1867, represented the extreme low mark in the experience of the hospital, and it was a matter of serious consideration whether it should be continued. But in 1868, forty-nine patients were treated; and in 1869, sixtyeight.

By the spring of 1860, the work had begun to increase to such an extent that it was necessary to seek new quarters. It was then that Father Senez, to shelter this charity, gave up his own residence. At his invitation, the Sisters settled in the rectory, then on Second Street east of St. Mary's Church. Meanwhile, in the fall of 1868, Charles H. O'Neill, the first Catholic Mayor of Jersey City, and Henry Carroll, a prosperous baker, both trustees of St. Mary's Church, had contributed one thousand dollars and two hundred dollars respectively, to a fund for a proposed new hospital.69

Permanent quarters were now desirable and necessary. In the fall of 1869, the nucleus of the present site of the hospital on East Hamilton Place, still one of the lower city's few attractive spots, was obtained. Chiefly through the generosity of 

Mr. John Halliard and Mr. Harold Henwood, two converts, there was then purchased, at a cost of $32,000, a plot and a three-story residence at the corner of Pavonia Avenue and East 

Hamilton Place. This enlargement of accommodations per mitted the treatment of male patients hitherto sent to Hoboken, and the total number of patients, which in 1870 were 73, rose to 355 in the following year. The three-story residence was remodeled to suit the needs of the hospital; and in 1870, a

ST. FRANCIS HOSPITAL

new wing of five stories on a plot of ground twenty-five by seventy-five feet was completed at a cost of $25,000. 

St. Francis Hospital then emerged from the obscurity of its early years and became conspicuous among the institutions of the metropolitan area. It attracted the attention of publicspirited and enterprising physicians, and particularly that of Theodore R. Varick, M.D., who was recognized as one of the ablest surgeons of New Jersey. In July, 1871, he, with friends and associates, organized the first regular staff of St. Francis 

Hospital, eight in number. They were:

OFFICERS OF THE BOARD President JOHN J. PRENDERGAST, M.D. 

Curator-HENRY MITCHELL, M.D. 

Secretary-JOHN D. MCGILL, M.D. 

SURGICAL STAFF THEODORE R. VARICK, M.D. 

JOHN D. MCGILL, M.D. 

JOHN J. PRENDERGAST, M.D. 

DANIEL L. Reeve, M.D. 

MEDICAL STAFF JOSEPH E. CULVER, M.D. 

THEODORE F. MORRIS, M.D. 

SAMUEL R. FORMAN, M.D. 

HENRY MITCHELL, M.D. 

It was necessary to enlarge the hospital in 1873, in 1889, in 1914, and again in 1928. It now occupies a plot one hundred by two hundred feet, bounded by Erie Street, Pavonia Avenue, East Hamilton Place, and Ninth Street. 

A happy day for the Sisters, the staff, and for Father Senez, was Saturday, October 4, 1890, in the twenty-fifth year of the founding of St. Francis Hospital. Then there was completed the replacement of several dwelling houses by a single hospital building of modern design, and of a capacity thought sufficient (but actually not) to take care of the growth of the institution for a long time to come. And on that day, the hospital was formally dedicated and opened to the public with impressive ceremonies, in the presence of a notable assemblage of Church dignitaries, eminent physicians and surgeons, and prominent citizens.

In the early days of St. Francis Hospital, the outstanding 

Catholic physician of St. Mary's Parish was the highly successful Dr. Edward O'Callaghan, who seems, however, not to have been connected officially with the hospital. Dr. Thomas 

James McLoughlin, long a parochial figure, served in various capacities at the hospital from 1876 until his death in 1915. 

After 1912 he was its medical director, a post which his son, Dr. Frank J. McLoughlin, has held since 1924. Dr. Matthew

Joseph Smith, of a highly honored old family of the parish, brother of the Rev. James J. and the Rev. William A. Smith, served well in various capacities at the hospital from 1889 to 

1898, when he died. 

Dr. John J. Mooney, a parishioner at St. Mary's and brother of the late Rt. Rev. James F. Mooney, served zealously, principally as surgeon, from 1896 until his death in 1924. He was chief of staff during his last three years. After 1890, several other Catholic physicians who settled in St. Mary's parish became affiliated with the hospital. Many of them still survive; and the services of all, both Catholic and non-Catholic, are duly indicated in other books, in which are also preserved the pertinent facts of the later and current history of the hospital. 

Figures familiar to the older parishioners of St. Mary's were these physicians at the hospital: John A. Broderick, John E. 

Corrigan, John J. Craven, Joseph F. Finn (a resident of Greenville), Patrick J. Hamill, Edward P. Hart, George J. Hornung, John Nevin, and Edward H. Salmon.

The Sisters of the Poor of St. Francis, of course, have ministered to the unfortunates of all creeds and races, and the hospital has always been supported by the public. Generous donations have come from time to time to the hospital from the local railroad, industrial, and other business concerns. Also, beginning with two fairs held simultaneously in St. Mary's Institute and St. Michael's Church (the old edifice at Tenth and 

Erie Streets) in December 1870, and lasting two weeks, the Catholic women of Jersey City have year by year befriended the Sisters by donating the proceeds.70 In the early days Father

Senez used to send to the hospital the pennies and two-cent pieces given in the collections at St. Mary's."¹ 

After the removal of the hospital from St. Mary's parish in 

1871, the Sisters of the Poor were in the territory of St. 

Michael's parish, which had been established in 1868. And while Father Senez, who for many years was confessor and spiritual adviser of the Sisters, stands out as the important factor in the establishment and growth of St. Francis Hospital, it is very clearly evident that he was dutifully aided by other pastors, notably Fathers Anthony Cauvin of Our Lady of Grace, Hoboken, and John Kelly of St. Peter's, Patrick Corrigan of that parish and St. Bridget's, and Patrick Hennessy of St. Patrick's, all of Jersey City. One senses, too, though generally in the background, the presence of the noble figure of Bishop Bayley, he himself the grandson of a renowned physician of the early days of the American republic.72 

The Catholic Club 

Father Senez had longed for the day when he should have in St. Mary's parish a building devoted entirely to the interests of young men and women. On October 19, 1891, it was announced that a Club House would be erected, at a cost of ap- proximately $55,000. It was by reason of the indefatigible labors of Father Charles J. Kelly that the venerable pastor's hopes were fully realized, and on the evening of December 28, 1892, the new Catholic Club, located on the west side of Jersey Avenue between Second and Third Streets, was opened. It was built of pressed brick with gray stone trim, and was four stories high, with a frontage of fifty and a depth of one hundred feet. On the ground floor were bowling alleys, accommodations for cycling enthusiasts, and baths. On the second floor were the reading room, a spacious combination game and smoking room, a billiard room, and a coat room. The reading room, in the front of the building, looked out on Jersey Avenue and was the full width of the building. The game and smoking room was on the easterly side between the reading room and the billiard room; the latter, situated in the rear, was also the

full width of the building, and in it were six billiard and four pool tables. The coat room was on the west side of the building. All these rooms opened upon a large common center hall. 

On the third floor were the ladies' parlor in front, the music room in the rear, and a well-equipped library centered between them on the east side. On this floor the hall arrangement was similar to that of the second floor. The fourth or top floor of the Club House was equipped as a gymnasium, and was used for receptions, dances, entertainments, and lectures.73 

Membership was open to all Catholic men over eighteen years of age. The initiation fee was three dollars, and the monthly dues were fifty cents. Associate membership was open to the ladies at a yearly fee of one dollar. They enjoyed the privileges of the club on Thursday evenings and all afternoons, except Sundays and holidays.74

The printed program of the second anniversary of the Club, which was marked by a reception, promenade concert, and entertainment, held on January 10, 1895, carries a list of approximately twelve hundred active and associate members, representing nearly every well-circumstanced family of the parish. 

The club committees endeavored to make their departments interesting. The Bowling Committee arranged a successful series of games with other local clubs, including the Jersey City Club. The bowling team easily led the others in the Diocesan 

League. The Committee on Games arranged monthly progres sive euchre parties which were well attended and at which valuable prizes were distributed. The Cyclers' Department, a feature of the club, boasted of many expert riders among its large membership; two were attendants in the gymnasium for the instruction of members desiring to learn how to ride a wheel. The Billiards Committee, supervised by expert billiard players, taught the members, who improved year by year. 

Many of them were noted locally for their skill. The Library 

Committee formed a literary circle which engaged in the systematic study of different periods in the history, the art, and literature of ancient and modern nations. The Library Committee also organized a dramatic club which offered an oppor-

CATHOLIC CLUB HOUSE

tunity to members of histrionic taste or ability to improve themselves. There was also a musical club, which included branches for both vocal and instrumental practice and performance.75 

One year typifies the activities of the Catholic Club. On January 6, 1897, its Bowling Team defeated the Atlanta Wheelmen of Newark. On February 16, more than six hundred attended a dinner in honor of Bishop Wigger in the Club House. On 

April 5, Rev. John J. Tighe delivered a lecture on "Irishmen in the Civil War" to an audience of more than five hundred. 

On May 19, Mr. Henry Austin Adams, a converted Episcopal minister, delivered a lecture. On May 26, the Basketball Team was victorious in a game with a rival team. Those devoted to this sport arranged for an athletic carnival for the benefit of the Cadet Corps of the Catholic Institute. It was held on 

June 5, at Baldwin Park, an old open-air resort on the Hill, between Newark Avenue and Wayne Street. On August 3, the Club had its field day at the same park. During October, November, and December, there were entertainments and dancing every Thursday evening. On November 2, the Dramatic Union employed a professional coach to aid in its production on November 18 of "Tragedy." 76 The Union was among the leading organizations of its kind in New Jersey, and owed much to the constant care and direction of Louis N. 

Paladeau, Sr., Chairman of the Entertainment and Dramatic 

Committee.77

It was in these days that the Catholic Cyclers had their runs to the near-by towns of Morristown, Hempstead, Tottenville, Suffern, and Millburn. Frequently on Sunday there would be "a century," a run of one hundred miles. One course was from the club house to Newark, Passaic, Paterson, Montclair, through the Oranges to Springfield, Plainfield, Metuchen, Rahway, Elizabeth, Newark, and thence to the Club House.

Invitations were extended to other cyclers to attend these trips. Those who finished the distance within twelve hours received a "Survivor's Medal." A vacation tour to Niagara

Falls was frequently arranged, the cyclers starting from the 

Club House.78

The Catholic Club was a center of attraction until the year 1900, and from that time on it was gradually superseded by other activities.
