CHAPTER VII  RIGHT Reverend Monsignor Bernard Henry TerWoert, gen erally regarded as the second-but in fact the third-pastor of St. Mary's Church, was born in Jersey City on April 20, 1852, on First Street, near Coles Street.¹ He was baptized in old St. Peter's Church, then the only Catholic church in Jersey 

City. It occupied a plot on the north side of Grand Street, west of Washington Street, which was later the site of St. 

Aloysius Academy, a building now (1938) used by the Fathers of the Society of Jesus.2 His parents were John TerWoert and Veronica TerWoert. 

John TerWoert was born on February 20, 1821, in or near the city of Düsseldorf in the Rhine Province, Germany. He was the only son in a family of several children, and his kinfolk carried on the trade of dyers. He came to America about 

1840, and found employment in New York City with the firm of B. T. Babbitt and Company, manufacturers of soap and cleansing agents. He retired about 1880, having served for many years as a foreman in the packing department. He lived a quiet, laborious, and thrifty life. Veronica, his wife, bore him six children, of whom three died very young. To the three surviving children, a son and two daughters, these pious parents gave the best education then conveniently obtainable under wholly Catholic auspices. In the 1870's, the daughters were educated under the Mount Sisters at St. Mary's Academy, Jersey Avenue and Second Street, and from this school they were both graduated. John TerWoert died on February 21, 1896, at 102 Palisade Avenue in Jersey City, the residence of his daughter Veronica, with whom he and his wife had made their home after her marriage to Henry Smith in 1875.

The life and character of Father TerWoert's mother are revealed by a statement of the son at the time of her death in her seventy-third year, in St. John's parish, Jersey City: 75

She was a woman of strong will and noble aspirations. She was left an orphan at nine years of age at Recke, a village in the Province of Westphalia. She kept the family together by her labors at the spinning loom. She came to Jersey City when twenty-three years old, unprotected and unassisted. She resided on Second Street with the family of Mr. 

Dummer until she married my father. Then they moved to their new home in First Street.... She had a pleasant, suggestive way of saying things, and I have received in this kindly way many excellent suggestions from her. She suggested that it would be well always to say a word of praise for the good qualities my people possessed.... I can truly say that she was unselfish, pure, and always devoted to the best interests of her kingdom-the home. She lived and died in the practice of her faith. She was unostentatious, and talked more by her example than by her words. She led a life of faith, always keeping God before her eyes, always truthful. Her advice was welcome to all and given in a deferential manner.4

When cholera raged in 1854 in Jersey City, Mrs. TerWoert, after she had prepared her husband's supper and cared for her children, would leave her home, a basket on her arm containing clothing and food for sufferers, and with motherly kindness sit at the bedside and watch the sick during the night. 

When death overcame them, she would help prepare them for burial; and when her labors of love and duty had been performed, she would return to her family. Fearless of contagion, she attended patients night after night.5

During the last eight years of her life, Mrs. TerWoert was a daily attendant at early Mass at St. John's Church, of which her son was the first pastor. Of her good works among the poor and needy, she said nothing. She was survived by her son, and two daughters, Veronica who had become Mrs. Henry Smith and who died at Jersey City in 1933, and Sophia, in religion Sister Laurentia, who joined the Order of Franciscan Sisters in 

Cincinnati in 1877 and who died June 1, 1898 at Columbus, Ohio.®

The parents of Father TerWoert, with other early pious 

Germans of Jersey City and vicinity, on November 15, 1863, founded St. Boniface's Church, now (1938) at 262 First 

Street. This early German Congregation was under the direc-

tion of Father Dominic Kraus (1831-1885) the first rector of St. Boniface."

His Early Life and Student Days 

Bernard Henry TerWoert was the first young man born in the immediate vicinity of St. Mary's Church, who was raised to the dignity of the priesthood. The first school he attended was kept by Mr. Smith, on Wayne Street near Henderson 

Street, in Jersey City. He was prepared for First Holy Communion and Confirmation by Rev. Januarius De Concilio, who in the 1860's was an assistant of Father Senez. The interest of the first pastor of St. Mary's helped advance the young man in his studies for the priesthood, and his pastor's example naturally fostered in him the desire and choice of his vocation. 

For his preparatory studies, Bernard TerWoert attended St. 

Francis Xavier College in New York City, and St. Charles 

College at Ellicott City in Maryland. At St. Vincent's College in Latrobe, Pennsylvania, he received a formal certificate that he was a worthy candidate for Holy Orders, and by virtue of this recommendation he was admitted, in the fall of 1871, to the Seminary at Seton Hall. There he was ordained a priest on May 22, 1875, by Bishop Corrigan.10

First Years of His Priesthood 

From 1875 to 1877, Father TerWoert was an assistant at 

St. John's Church, Orange, New Jersey, under Rev. Hugh P. 

Fleming, who was then struggling manfully against adverse conditions." Here, Father Senez prior to 1854 had secured the site for the first frame church of that parish.12 Under 

Rev. Alphonse M. Steets, in 1877, Father TerWoert became an assistant at the Church of the Immaculate Conception, Montclair.18 Later in the same year, he was a curate under 

Rev. Thomas M. Killeen at St. Mary's Star of the Sea, Bayonne.14 In 1878, he was stationed at St. Michael's Church, Newark, a parish then recently organized by Rev. Patrick 

Leonard 15

From 1878 to 1884, Father TerWoert was pastor at St. 

John's Church, Lambertville, Hunterdon County, first under Bishop Corrigan, and then (after the establishment of the 

See of Trenton in 1881) under Most Rev. Michael J. O'Farrell, the first bishop of that diocese.16 During the six years at 

Lambertville, Father TerWoert showed his executive ability by thoroughly organizing the parish, forming several church societies, building a school and a convent, and acquiring the site of the present church there. He also built a church at 

Flemington. All these enterprises were carried out with a thoroughness that evoked commendation,18 and in large measure they led to his selection on November 20, 1884 as the first pastor of the Church of St. John the Baptist in the Marion section of Jersey City.19 By accepting this appointment, Father 

TerWoert availed himself of his right to remain a priest of the Diocese of Newark, instead of continuing his accidental affiliation with the Diocese of Trenton.20 

St. John's, Jersey City

Under the direction of Bishop Wigger, Father TerWoert planned well and wisely the new parish of St. John the Baptist. 

Its territory had been set off from St. Joseph's and St. Paul's, then very extensive parishes on Bergen Hill.21 Facing his task enthusiastically and vigorously, Father TerWoert on Sunday, December 7, 1884, in a dilapidated room in the old St. James 

Hotel in Marion, celebrated his first Mass in St. John's parish. 

The attendance was slight. The collection for the day amounted to thirty-four cents.22

Within two weeks after his arrival in November, however, he had bought four lots at Van Winkle and Huron Avenue. 

They formed a plot one hundred feet square. Here he erected, so that it faced Van Winkle Avenue, a small frame church which accommodated six hundred persons. It was heated by two pot-bellied coal-burning stoves, and was lighted by gas. 

Even before the white coat of plaster had been applied to the interior of this church, the new pastor celebrated the Christmas 

Mass, 1884. 23

In March, 1886, he acquired eight more lots. These fronted on the Hudson County Boulevard (then Nelson Avenue). 

Four of them, at the corner of Van Winkle Avenue, adjoined the four lots first purchased. This second purchase gave St. 

John's parish the whole frontage of Van Winkle Avenue from the Boulevard to Huron Avenue. Upon the central part of this second acquisition, Father TerWoert erected, facing the Boulevard (then Nelson Avenue), the first brick school building (still standing but not now in use). This was completed and opened for the children in September, 1887. In that year, to the south of this school was begun the erection of the parish rectory (still standing and in use). This was completed and ready for occupancy in 1888.24

Three years later, Father TerWoert made a third purchase of several (probably ten or twelve) lots adjoining the first and second purchases. By this third purchase, made in 1891, St. 

John's parish became the owner of the entire block bounded by the Boulevard, St. Paul's, Van Winkle, and Huron Avenues, excepting, however, four lots situated at the north end of the block. These four lots had a width of fifty feet on both the 

Boulevard and on Huron Avenue, and a depth both on St. 

Paul's Avenue and on their south side, of approximately one hundred and fifty feet. Upon these four lots, fronting on the 

Boulevard, was a frame building, and in the rear, on the Huron Avenue end, were stables. These four lots and the buildings were owned by the Lannon family, whose members had kindly promised Father TerWoert that they would sell to St. John's parish at the convenience of the family. He confidently relied upon their promise, which was faithfully kept. Accordingly, he was enabled meanwhile to develop his plans as he wished. In 1895, the Lannon property was conveyed to St. John's parish, which thereby became the owner of the entire block. From that year until 1918, the Lannon home was used as a convent.

In 1892, the frame church had been removed from its site on Van Winkle near Huron Avenue, to a site on the Boulevard just south of the Lannon house, for use, of course, while the

present St. John's Church was being constructed. On November 24, 1892, Father TerWoert and his congregation joyfully watched Bishop Wigger lay the corner stone of the present magnificent granite church building at the corner of the 

Boulevard and Van Winkle Avenue. On November 14, 1897 

it was dedicated, Monsignor De Concilio preaching the sermon. A few years later, the original frame church was moved again, this time from the Boulevard to a site on Huron Avenue. 

There it was used as a parish hall until 1914, when it was demolished by Monsignor Smith after the completion of St. 

John's present parochial school, which is at the corner of the Boulevard and St. Paul's Avenue. The present parish convent was built in 1902 by Rt. Rev. Patrick W. Smith, who in 1919 renovated it and changed its front to match that of the school.25 

In the 1920's, Monsignor Smith added to St. John's Church the tower which makes the edifice a landmark, and in the 1930's he directed the redecoration of the interior. The notable mosaic works which now adorn St. John's Church reflect Monsignor Smith's long, patient study and excellent taste, as well as the devotion, generosity, and affection of two generations of parishioners at St. John's, who loved and honored their two painstaking pastors. 

St. John's parish buildings-the present school, convent, first school, rectory, and church itself—all built upon a slight rise of land above the Boulevard which they face, are remarkably attractive. They evidence the wisdom and foresight of Monsignor TerWoert. Happily, he lived to see his original and well-begun plans completed successfully by a younger priest of great understanding, who, like him, cherished every consecrated value of the house of God.28

During Father TerWoert's pastorate at St. John's, the population there grew from 1100 souls to over 4000; and in the last year of his pastorate (1899), the revenue of the church amounted to $20,000. He had paid $26,000 for land, $21,000 for the parochial school, $15,000 for the rectory, $6000 for the convent, $10,000 for minor improvements, and about

$110,000 of the $200,000 which was the approximate cost of the church edifice.2 27 

Hudson County Catholic Cemeteries 

In addition to his cares as a pastor of this growing parish, Father TerWoert fulfilled to the great satisfaction of two bishops, many duties connected with the management of the Hudson County Catholic Cemeteries. He was a member of the Cemetery Board; and from 1888 to 1916, he held the important and responsible position of superintendent.28 The 

Board gave him full power. Under his administration many improvements were made at the Holy Name Cemetery. He directed the erection of the first office at Montgomery Street and West Side Avenue at a cost of $7000, and greenhouses at a cost of $4000. He built a carriage house and stable at an outlay of $2500. He directed the finances of the cemetery so that he was able to purchase ground for its extension at a cost of $109,000. He also improved the whole property by an expenditure of $75,000. Of this last sum, approximately one third was spent in erecting a new office building at the foot of Glenwood Avenue. By his excellent management, an initial debt of $208,000 was paid out of the regular revenues of the cemetery. He planned, and saw erected, the granite receiving vault, which cost $50,000, and he directed the erection of the iron fence which extends along West Side Avenue in front of the cemetery.29 Later (1905-1910) it was through his close attention to detail and good management and to his business skill, that the Diocese of Newark secured the land for Holy Cross Cemetery at North Arlington, New Jersey.30 

His Silver Jubilee

Father TerWoert celebrated the Silver Jubilee of his ordination on Tuesday, May 22, 1900, at St. John's Church, in the presence of his parishioners and a distinguished gathering of priests from the metropolitan district. An outstanding feature of the celebration was the reception after Mass, given to Father TerWoert by five hundred children, whose prayers, aspirations,

and communions, as a spiritual bouquet, affected him deeply. 

The children sounded their note of simplicity by their songs of welcome, greetings, congratulations, and jubilee. There was also a lighter touch in their ribbon drill, and the songs "Under the Old Umbrella," "Catch the Little Bean Bag," and "Jolly Little Waiters." The Celebrant received thoughtful gifts from the St. Aloysius Society, the Children of Mary, the school children, the Young Ladies' Sodality, and from personal friends who desired their names withheld.

Father TerWoert's Silver Jubilee reached a fitting climax in the school hall, when Father Delehanty, his assistant, on behalf of the parishioners presented him with a purse containing $1000, as a token of appreciation of his faithful work. 

The gift was a surprise. In a feeling response, Father TerWoert related many instances of his ministry, and spoke of many of the distinguished workers in the preaching vineyard. The committee who collected the money that filled the purse came from the older families of St. John's parish. They were: 

Edwin Barry

Herman Brede William Connors Timothy Conroy John Dorton 

Michael Downs

John Evans Thomas Haley John Haskins 

Patrick Heffron 

Thomas Howe 

John Keane

Patrick Kennedy 

Dennis Mullins 

Peter Murphy 

James McCrarn

John McDonald 

Frank McElhone 

Walter O. Reed 

Patrick Rodgers 

John Walsh 31 

Appointment to St. Mary's 

When Father Senez died on February 11, 1900, Bishop

Wigger was in Europe accompanied by Father Lawrence C. M. 

Carroll. Vicar-General John J. O'Connor immediately made preparations to hold a concursus, as prescribed by the Council of Baltimore and the general law of the Church.32 

In point of influence and population, St. Mary's Church was then most important, and one of the few irremovable pastorates in the diocese. There were several priests available for the position of pastor of St. Mary's, and speculation was rife. In the forenoon of May 22, 1900, it was announced at South

4 Orange by Bishop Wigger that Father TerWoert had been appointed and would shortly enter upon his new duties.³ 33 This promotion crowned twenty-five years of faithful labor. 

On all sides it was regarded as a reward for well-performed, faithful service, and it was believed that Bishop Wigger and his advisers reached their decision upon a consideration of Father TerWoert's marked spiritual and intellectual worth, and his remarkable executive ability as an organizer and a builder. The consensus of opinion within and without church circles was that his appointment was opportune. 34 

Upon the following Sunday, May 27, 1900, at all the Masses in St. Mary's Church, Father Daniel J. Brady, administrator of the parish after the death of Dean Senez, formally announced to the congregation the appointment of the new rector. He added: "Father TerWoert comes with the approbation of the Bishop, and everything the Bishop does is well done. Father TerWoert's appointment to the pastorate of 

St. Mary's is peculiarly appropriate. He was brought up in it, and was the first young man of the parish to be raised to the dignity of the priesthood. He was identified in his early days with the Institute of the parish, and when he returned from the seminary, a young priest, he said his first Mass in St. Mary's 

Orphan Asylum. . . . He is an amiable and benevolent man, who will take care of the Orphan Asylum and School." Father

Brady also announced that Father TerWoert would make his appearance at the church upon the following Friday morning, June 1, 1900, and that in the evening a reception would be tendered him at the Institute Hall on Third Street.35 

At St. John's Church, on Thursday evening, May 31, 1900, after solemn Vespers had been sung and before Benediction was given, Father TerWoert bade farewell to his flock of the preceding sixteen years. As he mounted the pulpit to deliver his farewell sermon, the congregation was intensely silent. 

He began touchingly, alluding to the fact that for the last time as pastor he was to address his parishioners. He was to render, of course, a financial account of his stewardship, but more important was the greater account of his care of souls

as a preaching priest, an account which he must render to his God. "We work among you as priests," he said, “anointed to preach the Word. We work to better acquaint you with the 

Sacraments and the value of prayer. When I first came here, you were few. You now number four thousand souls. Some words I may have said that left wounds in the heart, but where conscience has dictated to me to excoriate, I have done so irrespective of persons and although one half of my congregation might have walked out. But I have no personal grudge against any of you. I leave you as though you were my children. If I said anything to hurt your feelings, all I ask is that you forgive. I certainly forgive all who may have offended me. I could have grabbed money. I do not love money. I ask you to pray for me. I go to a place you all know.

There is a great deal to do there. I will have to work harder in St. Mary's than I did here, and I would be a foolish man if I did not think I would have to do so. I feel that I shall be strictly responsible for the many souls of that parish. I will ask you to pray for me. I will ask you to pray for my health and strength and for whatever little talent I may possess for preaching the Word of God. I ask you to pray for those whom 

I loved [he here pointed to the tablets inscribed upon the altar of Carrara marble which he had donated in memory of his father and mother] and from whom some day I hope never to be separated. When you come into this church for Holy Communion and look upon this altar, erected to their memory, pray for them."

After telling of the unceasing toil of the Dominican Sisters who had charge of St. John's parochial school, he said: “They have been my right arm. They shall shine as stars in the 

Kingdom of Heaven, for God said so. They deserve your prayers." The parish had been blessed, he said, with good, sincere, well-meaning men in the persons of his two assistants, Fathers Delehanty and Sheedy, "who came here to work for God and have shown untiring zeal in His service." As long as they all had been together not an unkind word or look had passed among them. "And now," he said, "I thank all of you

for what you have done. I will not forget to pray for you, and for those who are gone, and I will promise to pray for them at the Altar of the Cross. Now I ask Almighty God to bless you all, and I will give my solemn blessing in the Name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost, Amen."36

CHAPTER VIII  

IT WAS thus evident to Father TerWoert, before he came to St. Mary's Parish on June 1, 1900, that his labors there would not be light. As a diocesan consultor, he had known of the physical decline of Dean Senez, and of the latter's advance in years. He had opportunity to observe too that the able and willing first assistants there were naturally circumscribed, and could make few important changes. He was also familiar with the history of the parish, for in it he had grown to manhood, and there his family for many years had continued to dwell. 

They had maintained, however, their affiliation with St. Boniface's Church until they went to St. John's. 

The matters of the boundaries between St. Michael's and 

St. Mary's parishes, and between St. Mary's and St. Peter's, demanded the immediate attention of the new pastor. In the fall of 1900, these were modified and defined with certainty. 

By the advice of the Diocesan Council, and with the full assent of Father TerWoert as pastor of St. Mary's, Bishop 

Wigger, on November 22, 1900, directed that Seventh Street should be the dividing line between St. Mary's and St. Michael's parishes. This resulted in 1100 persons being added to the congregation of St. Michael's,¹ which in 1899, by a similar adjustment between it and St. Lucy's, had lost a population of 

1500 souls. By these re-adjustments, each of these three parishes have come to have a width, from north to south, of about one third of a mile: St. Lucy's runs from the Hoboken 

City line (Twentieth Street) to Thirteenth Street; St. Michael's, from Thirteenth Street to Seventh Street; and St. Mary's from 

Seventh Street to Railroad Avenue. Originally, in 1859, the whole of this territory was St. Mary's parish. 2 It was also on November 22, 1900, that Bishop Wigger settled any misunderstanding as to the lines between St. Mary's 86

3 and St. Peter's parishes, where the two adjoin in the neighborhood of Newark Avenue, Warren, Morgan, and Henderson

Streets. After consultation with others, including Father 

Charles J. Kelly, formerly first assistant at St. Mary's, Bishop 

Wigger stated in effect that Warren Street and Henderson Street south of Morgan Street were within the limits of St. 

Peter's parish, except that all of the west side of Henderson Street, north of Newark Avenue, was to be considered as part of St. Mary's. That alignment is still maintained. From that neighborhood a large Irish Catholic population has, within the last forty years, practically disappeared. Hundreds moved of necessity when the Hudson and Manhattan Railroad Company in 1905 acquired for its purposes the large thickly settled block bounded by Henderson, Steuben, and Warren Streets, and Railroad Avenue. This loss of population, however, was felt mainly by St. Peter's parish, although many Italians who lived on Railroad Avenue generally worshiped at Holy Rosary Church on Sixth Street, which is convenient to the Italian settlement of lower Jersey City familiarly called "The Village." Another important care of Father TerWoert was the proper housing of the priests and of the Christian Brothers. After a usage of more than thirty years (1868-1900), the rectory at 219 Third Street showed signs of wear. The original rectory at 248 Second Street, then (1900) a dwelling at least fifty years old—it had also been St. Francis Hospital in 1868-1869, and had housed the Christian Brothers since 1870-was inadequate for further use. Father TerWoert, therefore, abandoned 

219 Third Street as a rectory, and leased for the use of the priests the substantial brownstone dwelling at 565 Jersey Avenue, to the immediate south of the Catholic Club. This had formerly been the residence of the family of William McCarren, an early prosperous parishioner. The new pastor then saw to the remodeling, renovation, and repair of the old rectory at 219 Third Street, with the result that in the early fall of 1900, the Christian Brothers were comfortably installed

there. Their old dwelling at 248 Second Street was then demolished, and the land left vacant.® 

Probably the tornado of August 24, 1901, was to accelerate the plans of Father TerWoert, but as early as June 7 of that year, he had already begun to concern himself with plans for a new rectory on Second Street."

At St. Mary's, the year 1901 was eventful. On January 5, Most Rev. Winand M. Wigger, third Bishop of Newark, who was distinguished for his truly apostolic poverty, died at Newark in the twentieth year of an episcopate that was always arduous. On the same day, the Redemptorist Fathers opened at St. Mary's a mission which lasted four weeks. It was the first of numerous missions during the pastorate of Father 

TerWoert.10 

On January 24, Father TerWoert announced the appointment as sexton of Joseph Storzinger in the place of Peter

McNamee, a faithful worker for nearly twenty-five years who had given up his position a few months after the death of 

Dean Senez.11 

On February 11, the anniversary dinner of the Catholic Club was a testimonial in honor of the new pastor. Among those present was Mayor Edward Hoos, who, with his family, then occupied the large old frame dwelling (still standing) at the south-east corner of Third Street and Jersey Avenue, opposite the Club House. The speakers at the dinner included General 

James R. O'Beirne, a valiant veteran of the Civil War, then 

Corporation Counsel of the City of New York, and the Hon orable James F. Minturn of Hoboken, later State Senator from 

Hudson County and Judge of the Circuit Court.12 The latter was the first Catholic to be appointed (1908) a Justice of the 

Supreme Court of New Jersey. 

About this time Father TerWoert considered it best that new arrangements be made in regard to music. He assigned the selection of singers to Mr. Charles N. Smith, who succeeded 

Miss Helen Armstrong. She had resigned on April 1, after serving as organist for six years. Educated at St. Mary's Acad-

emy, she had often generously participated in many parish entertainments.13

At the end of April, 1901, Father Daniel J. Brady, the first assistant curate, who had served in that capacity since May, 1898, was transferred to another parish. There had unfortunately arisen differences between him and the pastor. Thereupon, several parishioners, friends of Father Brady, raised a purse for him, and some severed their association with the parish.14

In June, as usual, there were several weddings; that on the 

17th, of Miss Mary F. Coakley and Mr. Joseph F. Hallahan of Philadelphia was duly noticed in the newspapers.15 

On Sunday, June 23, Rev. Daniel F. Hogan, who had been reared in the parish, celebrated his first Solemn Mass. Father 

Charles J. Kelly, the popular curate of the late 1880's and early 

1890's, preached an appropriate sermon. It was the last of its kind in Old St. Mary's. 

On June 28, "The Lilliputians in Fairyland" was presented¹º by the Girls' Department at the Institute as a closing exercise for the year; and at the Club House on the following evening, St. Mary's Cadets, made up of Academy boys, gave a military drill.17

It was in these days that from the old Jersey City High School, conducted within the confines of the parish from 1875 to 1903,18 there was formally graduated a young man later to be known as the Most Rev. John A. Duffy, Bishop of the important See of Buffalo.19

On July 2, one hundred boys of the Sanctuary Society (several were members of the Tesla Club, formed for the study of electricity) enjoyed a trolley ride and picnic.20 It was a custom of the times, and an appreciation by the pastor and congregation of the boys' services and devotion at the altar. 

On Thursday, July 25, Father TerWoert, as a Diocesan Consultor, participated at Newark in the ceremony of the consecration of Most Rev. John J. O'Connor, the fourth Bishop of Newark.21

The Tornado of August 24, 1901 

On Saturday, August 24, 1901, shortly after noon, a tornado moving north and northeastward damaged slightly several properties in Bayonne and Greenville. It then struck lower 

Jersey City. There its path lay generally to the east of Jersey 

Avenue west of Barrow Street, and along Erie Street, as evidenced by the havoc it wrought in a distance of the mile between Grand Street on the south and Ninth Street on the north. On York Street between Jersey Avenue and Barrow 

Street, it ripped off and rolled up like rugs, the tin roofs of several dwellings. In Van Vorst Park, it blew down twenty-five or thirty fifty-year-old maple trees, and thus destroyed the beauty of that breathing place. A few hundred feet north of the park, St. Matthew's Lutheran Church, west of Barrow 

Street, felt a heavy blow which partly stripped the tin roof. 

Next in the path of this "twister" was the Bijou, now (1938) the Palace Theatre. Here the wind tore through the box-office lobby on the ground floor, and sent into the auditorium a pressure which caused the rear wall of the theatre to collapse. 

Bricks piled up on the sidewalk and roadway of First Street, and some fell upon the stage of the theatre and the theatrical properties.

Apparently the vortex of the tornado did not quite touch the ground, although at the intersection of Second and Erie Streets diagonally opposite St. Mary's Church, there was left a tangle of fallen electric wires and branches of old trees. 

When it struck St. Mary's, the storm was of terrific force. It ripped sheets from the tin roof, and with an indescribable crash, it toppled over the steeple. The church bell of five hundred pounds went hurtling down on the vacant lot at 248

Second Street, which had been the site of the house used in turn as rectory, hospital, and dwelling for the Christian Brothers. The broken steeple crashed through the roof of the church at the ridgepole, fell into the interior, broke through the main flooring, and came to rest in the basement. In passing, it destroyed the choir loft and, generally, the furnishings of the church, and damaged beyond repair the great organ

built in 1867 by the Jardines.22 The altar lamp remained lighted. Father Charles A. Smith, who was in the church at the time, was not injured.23

Still speeding north, the storm ripped off part of the roof of 219 Third Street, then occupied by the Christian Brothers. 

It struck Temple Israel, the Jewish synagogue on Fifth above Erie Street, and visited some unwelcome attention upon several houses on Eighth near Grove Street, and upon the brewery of Lembeck & Betz on Ninth near Henderson Street. At Hamilton 

Park, it uprooted more old maples, and whirled some of their branches against houses on Ninth Street. Still going northeastward, it did damage in Hoboken, West Hoboken, Weehawken, Union Hill, Fort Lee, and Edgewater, before its fury was quite spent. 24 That afternoon, at the rectory, there was a conference of a perplexed pastor and his assistants, Fathers Charles A. Smith, Michael McGuinness, John F. Boyle, and Patrick A. Maher.

What to do for the morrow? It was the pastor's thought that the parishioners should attend the neighboring churches in the lower city. Father McGuinness, however, made the suggestion that the Masses the next day should be celebrated in the auditorium of the Institute, a plan which was favorably received. 

The new sexton obtained several yards of white muslin, upon which he painted roughly an announcement to the effect that 

Masses would be said at the Institute. Before four o'clock in the afternoon, this improvised sign had been affixed to the corner of the church at Erie and Second Streets, where it was seen by the crowds who had come to look upon the damage done by the storm. Before midnight, probably every Catholic family in the parish knew the arrangements for the next day.25 

A topic of conversation was the probability of the old church being repaired for further use. But an inspection made within the next few days by Mr. James Carlin, a mason and builder of long experience, led to the conclusion that it would be better to build a new edifice.26 This judgment, which coincided with that of other experts who were later consulted by Father TerWoert, was followed. There is no record that after 27

August 24, 1901, old St. Mary's Church was ever used for divine worship. The last funeral Mass was celebrated 

August 22, 1901, for the soul of Catherine Sharkey, aged thirty-five, who was buried from the home of Patrick Hanlon at 379 Grove Street.28

Immediately after the tornado, Bishop O'Connor wrote to Father TerWoert that he would approve of whatever course Father TerWoert might wish to take in regard to repairs of the damaged church.29

The old church was dismantled, its foundations were uprooted, and its memorial windows entirely destroyed, save for several pieces of stained glass still treasured in some households. But there clung to it sacred memories, in the love and reverence of the older parishioners and their kinsmen.30 

Planning for the New Church It having been determined that the old church should be demolished, Father TerWoert went to work with his usual vigor. In September, 1901, he bought from William Howeth, grocer, butcher, and milk dealer long established at 52 Erie.

Street near Third, his holding, which comprised a plot of fiftyeight by forty feet. On the east it adjoined what was then the home of the Christian Brothers, known as 219 Third Street.

On the south it adjoined the ruins of the old church. By this purchase, there was thus secured for St. Mary's parish the entire frontage on Erie Street between Second and Third Streets.81 

Mr. Howeth, a Presbyterian and a lifelong friend of Father

Senez, had brought up there a large family of capable children; one of his daughters married Dr. Seth A. Sprague, a noted physician of the lower city, whose father before him had practiced medicine.32

The task of razing old St. Mary's was entrusted to the 

Thomas Krekeler Company, of Brooklyn. There was apparently some pressure brought to bear by Father TerWoert, for the demolition was completed early in the spring of 1902, a larger force of workmen than originally contemplated having been engaged.33 Plans for the rectory and basement of the

NEW ST. MARY'S CHURCH

new church had meanwhile been drawn. Those for the rectory were furnished by Fohnachen, Lawrence and Donnell of 111 Fifth Avenue, New York City; and those for the basement of the new church by Scheckel and Ditmars of the same address.84 

After numerous bids had been received, the contract for the masonry was awarded to John Wheelihan and Sons, a capable local firm of long experience and excellent repute. Contract for the carpentry was awarded to John Kiernan, who in his line was likewise highly regarded. Work upon the buildings was begun in the spring of 1902.35 The combined final cost of the rectory and the basement church, when completed, was $109,- 

643.24. Their furnishings, respectively, cost $9569.09 and $3354.00.36 An organ costing $3000 was the gift of John Mullins.37

In the presence of 15,000 persons, the corner stone of the new church was laid by Bishop O'Connor on Sunday, September 21, 1902, at two:thirty P.M. In attendance was a large number of clergy, not only from neighboring parishes, but from all parts of the diocese. They sat upon a temporary platform. In the adjacent Erie and Second Streets, during the ceremony, the laity stood at attention. The sermon for the occasion was preached by Father James F. Mooney, the former curate, who was warmly applauded, particularly by those who had been under his instruction and were indebted to him for his very kind performance of many priestly offices.38 A collection then taken up amounted to $1035.54. 39 In Catholic circles, Father TerWoert, after his successful administration at Lambertville, always held the reputation of being an excellent builder and a skillful manager of parish finances.40 Knowledge of his achievements at Lambertville and at St. John's parish in Jersey City preceded, of course, his appointment as pastor of St. Mary's, but few if any persons thought that in what was his third pastorate, the important task of building would be suddenly thrust upon him. The havoc of the tornado, however, raised unforeseen and urgent problems.¹¹ His chief duty was to provide a suitable house of worship for a congregation of approximately 14,000 men,

women, and children.42 It is safe to say that, for more than a quarter of a century, some aspect of that duty was continually before him, and that even after 1927, when his flock had come into possession of the present church edifice, his thoughts were still upon its proper and complete adornment.48 

To describe in detail all the ways and means by which Father TerWoert assured himself of the steadily generous support of a congregation of modest circumstances, would be a long recital. On his side were his noted business acumen, the constant labor of repeated appeals for funds, and the equally constant judicious investment of accumulations lest the material interests of the parish suffer loss and depreciation. This phase of duty was made more, rather than less, important because the funds arose mainly from the toilsome labor and careful thrift of simple men and women. But though a complete picture be not furnished, its beginnings may be here sketched, as illustrative of the cooperation between a capable pastor and an appreciative flock in a populous urban parish during the first quarter of the nineteenth century.**

The visitation made from house to house by Father TerWoert and his assistants in the fall of 1901, is reflected in a financial statement distributed by him to his flock in November of that year. There were approximately 1600 contributions on a list which carried none of less than a dollar. The total of the collection was $10,212.50. Of this sum, the largest contribution was that of John Mullins, a prosperous furniture dealer and investor, who a generation or so earlier had been a hodcarrier. He gave $1000. The next largest contribution was $500, from Hugh Dugan, Collector of Hudson County, who in the early years of his manhood had been a carpenter. Both men were markedly charitable. In the will of the former-who died a millionaire-he was shown to be the benefactor of numerous charities. Seven contributions, each of $100, were made by men who had prospered in business, medicine, law, and politics. A notable donation was one of fifty dollars from the Father Senez Branch of the Ladies' Catholic Benevolent 

Association. Approximately forty contributions range from

twenty to fifty dollars; these are from doctors, lawyers, real estate brokers, retail storekeepers, masters in the building trades, and the more prosperous saloon-keepers. There were 

103 donations of ten dollars each; they came from a book agent, a butcher, an undertaker, a restaurant keeper, a milk dealer, a fruit dealer, a florist's wife, a junkman, a young building contractor, a young doctor, a young lawyer, several comfortably circumstanced widows, a few school teachers, and several clerks in public offices.45 46 Thirty-five residents of Jersey Avenue contributed $619. 

Approximately 1450 contributions ranged from one dollar to five dollars, and aggregated nearly $6000. Among these last are the contributions of the densely populated Grove Street, on which 272 contributors gave $924.50. This sum came mainly from fathers and elder brothers who were brakemen, switchmen, foremen, engineers, teamsters, molders, factory hands, journeymen in the building trades, porters, and truckmen. Among the younger contributors were the young men and women who found employment as bookkeepers and as clerks in stores, either in New York City or in Jersey City.

As one now pores over the list, many memories are revived, and the names reflect personalities. One contribution of ten dollars represents numerous appeals among the lowly by a pious woman whose own contribution of two dollars, therefore, is more impressive than the heavily typed listings of the materially prosperous. More than once there appears the widow's mite. And there stands out blessedly the names of parents who contented themselves with little that their children might secure the advantages of long schooling. The names of those who but a few years before were in Ireland, are close to those who were three generations removed from the old country.48 Street numbers are not given, but the streets are in an ordered arrangement; and the order of listing raises no difficulty in the way of simple folk finding out how their neighbors are helping a good cause. One misses the names of a few individuals; perhaps they were mindful that the old pastor, Father Senez, issued no such lists, and they would 47

religiously keep their light from shining before men by contributing otherwise and anonymously. But random tests for names are invariably successful, and the list is a practical directory of almost every family then in the parish.49 Pondered upon, it is essentially a testimony which records the beginning of the solution of the ancient problem of a generation providing for itself and those who were to follow it, a fitting place to worship God. And it demonstrates the confidence with which they would respond to the directions and efforts of a capable pastor whose heart and mind were set upon building even better than he had already built for his first two flocks. 

Noteworthy in itself as was this collection of November, 1901, yet in the following month, at Christmas, from practically the same givers, there was collected $3265.40.50 This was likewise a remarkable sum for the time, since its purchasing value, according to accepted standards, would now (1938) be nearly $10,000.

Dedication of the Lower Church 

On Sunday, October 18, 1903, Bishop O'Connor dedicated the completed basement of the present church edifice. Though but a basement, it was cheerful and inviting, in contrast to the old Institute where services had been held since the tornado. 

Its seating capacity of 1600 was more than taxed.51 Father 

Maher was celebrant of the Mass of dedication; Rev. Michael 

J. Corcoran, later pastor of St. Michael's, 52 Jersey City, was deacon, and Father Boyle, sub-deacon. The Bishop was enthroned on the Gospel side of the altar. Rev. Father Thomas 

Burke, a priest of the Congregation of Saint Paul (Paulists), then beginning a parish mission, preached a memorably eloquent sermon on "The Church, the Teacher of Mankind." 

Collections were taken for the new Cathedral and the parish church building fund.

The basement served as a church for the congregation until 1926, when the upper church was completed.53 Its fittings were plain but substantial; and it was well ventilated and lightsome. Wainscoted walls and a flat metal ceiling gave it a

97 neat appearance. The pews of quartered oak were, of course, comfortably roomy. The main altar was erected in a circular alcove on the north end of the structure, and was lighted from without by a cupola of ground glass. The organ was installed at the east end of the transept.5* Exteriorly, the structure was uninviting, for the roof was flat and the entrance was an unattractive wooden kiosk which jutted into Second Street. 

The granite of the lower building, however, was a promise of the enduring magnificence to come; and against the comparatively low structure, the rectory—and after 1915 the new school-stood out in happy and splendid relief.55

Father TerWoert and the Catholic Club When Father TerWoert came to St. Mary's in 1900, the 

Catholic Club was still a center of attraction, but within a few years it began to lose popularity. It seems to have been assumed that the new Pastor did not favor the Club, and that he put an end to its existence. It is true that Father TerWoert's austere nature did not make him popular generally, nor with the Club members in particular. Of course, he did not court popularity. For several years there was a more-or-less strained attitude at times between him and some members of the congregation, so predominantly Irish. There were those who manifested no very kindly respect for him, and he, not unlike most Teutons, had a disposition which gave little or no evidence of the charm that might attract the average Celt, if there is such a creature. Probably because he was confronted with several weighty and important problems, he came to regard the Club as of comparatively little importance, and, except during the first year or two of his pastorate, he appears not to have taken any great interest in its activities.56 

But as a matter of fact, there were other current causes which made the Pastor averse to the continuance of the Catholic Club of the earlier days. After an existence of eight years, much of its novelty had worn off, as is not unusual with organizations of its kind.57 Many of its older members had died.58 Marriage, too, with its consequent domestication, had

brought about the withdrawal of many of the younger men of its early days.59 Then again, while the cycling boom of 18961897 had attracted many new members, it did not eventually strengthen the Club, as the quick growth of the cycling division made it difficult for the directors of the Club to provide cyclists with the accommodations they desired. This difficulty brought about the organization of the Niantic Wheelman, of which the leading members were cycling enthusiasts who had been very prominent in the Catholic Club.60 The final result was that the latter lost many members whom it did not regain, even when, about 1900, cycling ceased to be a vogue.61 

It was about this time also, that there began a noticeable shift in the population from lower Jersey City to the Hill sections. And as time went on, other parishes established their own social centers, notably St. Michael's, in 1896.62 Then, too, the discomfort (1901-1903) of attending services in the old Institute did not make St. Mary's parish especially attractive to newcomers. Moreover, the Elks and the Knights of Columbus were increasing in membership. The latter organization in 1902, through the kindness of Father TerWoert, 6 had the use of the Catholic Club House for its social workers in the labor field, but in 1906 it acquired the old Palma Club House at Bright Street and Jersey Avenue. This club house of the 

Knights of Columbus became a kind of successful competitor of the Catholic Club, and the latter gradually appealed less and less to the Catholics of the city at large. More and more it came to be only a parish center.64

There was not, however, a sudden stop in the activities of the Catholic Club. Father Brady had in 1898-1899 supervised improvements and renovations, including the installation of electric lights throughout the building; and in 1901, the bowling alleys were practically rebuilt under the supervision of Mr. 

Joseph Duane, a very popular member of the Club.65 Also year by year during the first years of this century, the young and old of the parish continued to enjoy bowling and basketball games, athletic meets and carnivals, card parties (euchre was then popular), cotillions, dances, entertainments, plays,

minstrel shows, picnics, stags, and smokers, all without number.66 In 1904 a parish fair netted $7100.19; in 1909 another fair netted $5996.50;67 and both were held in the Club 

House.68 After 1901, however, there seems to have been no annual dinner at the Club; but with a new church then to be built and paid for, perhaps that omission was not remarkable.

Latter Days of the Catholic Club 

An observer of the decade from 1900 to 1910 might conclude that in lower Jersey City, the Catholics were now a trifle less stratified socially than they had been in the decade from 1890 to 1900. Not that at any time there was essentially any great difference between what was and what was not "hightoned." Generally, however, the first members of the Catholic 

Club had been drawn from among the better circumstanced families of the parish, but with time, prosperity became more widely spread, and the property of the comparatively wealthy few was divided among families which today would be considered large.

Another indication of the levelling process after the turn of the century, was the closing of the parish academies, both of St. Michael's about 1900,69 and of St. Mary's in 1906. This was probably a wholesome development, for it was an evidence that elementary instruction for Catholic children, within parish limits at least, was not to be dependent upon the parents' ability to pay; and that for the Catholics in a new and democratic civilization, there should be no occasion for what might be regarded as class distinction. It should be remembered, however, that a commendable feature of education at academies is that in some measure parents contribute to its support according to their means, and incidentally give to the Sisters a revenue that is, of course, carefully and wisely administered. 

Holy Name Club 

It was in this decade, furthermore, that interest was revived in the Holy Name Society, which had lost ground in the early

days of the Catholic Club. (A good observer says, the Club destroyed the Society for the time being.) Father Michael 

McGuinness, who came to the parish in 1901, found but twelve members in the Holy Name Society; but within a year the membership had increased to more than eight hundred. 

This growth was augmented by the formation in 1904 of the Jersey City Federation of Holy Name Societies, and in 1905, of the Diocesan Holy Name Union." 

71 In 1909, the Catholic Club was reorganized as the Holy 

Name Club, with Father Thomas J. Martin72 as its first president. It had the sanction and interest, of course, of Father 

TerWoert; and its activities were of a kind with those of the later days of the Catholic Club.73 Prominent figures in the 

Holy Name Club between 1910 and 1917 were: John M. 

Boylan, Patrick Callahan, James F. Clerkin, Thomas Corydon, Andrew A. Daly, Jr., Frank Daly, Robert Downing, Thomas F. 

Duff, James J. Ferris, James McCue, Thomas Maypother, John 

Moriarity, and Joseph Rafter.74

During the construction of the present parish school (19151916) children usually sent to the old Institute were instructed in the Club House.75 In 1917 the Holy Name Club, which in 1910 had an indicated membership of one thousand, was greatly reduced, as many of its members were departing for service in the World War. After due consideration of all his problems, Father TerWoert concluded that the building should no longer be used as a club house; and in 1918 it was leased to the public authorities for use as a center for the employment of labor, for the administration of the Workmen's Compensation Law, and for other social legislation.76 

Before the building was leased to the public authorities, the population of the parish, moreover, had suffered a slow, steady, and marked decline. The indications are that in 1900, there were almost 14,000 members of the congregation, while in 1920, there were fewer than 10,000." Furthermore, automobiles, motion pictures, and new varieties of entertainment were engaging the attention of the young, and for all practical

ΙΟΙ purposes, the auditorium in the new parish school (and later the basement of the completed church) afforded ample facilities for amusement and entertainment on a large scale. 

A considered judgment would be that the Catholic Club and its successor, the Holy Name Club, were of marked social value to lower Jersey City. The Club House, well arranged and economically and skillfully directed, was a convenient rendezvous for young and old, and especially for the young; and at a time when the field of cheap and convenient amusement in the city was very restricted, it was well that those who desired by bowl, play billiards and pool, cards, and other games, might enjoy themselves in the best surroundings. The spacious fourth floor of the Club House was well adapted to dances which were easily supervised, and where, under excellent auspices, the association of young men and women fostered friendships which frequently led to Holy Matrimony.78 

The Convent Sisters 

On October 12, 1905, Bishop O'Connor notified Father

TerWoert that the Mount Sisters had been called back to New 

York, and that after the following July (1906) they would no longer be connected with St. Mary's.79 Thereupon, Father 

TerWoert through the Bishop arranged with Mother Mary Xavier Mehegan,80 the venerated Foundress (then in her eighty-first year) of the Sisters of Charity of New Jersey, usually called the Madison or Convent Sisters, to send to St. 

Mary's parish a community of Sisters to manage the orphanage and to instruct the girls and very young boys in the parish school. They came in the fall of 1906.81

The Convent Sisters were not strangers in Jersey City. The 

Mount Sisters, who had served at St. Mary's for forty-six years, had until 1847 been connected with the original Sisters of 

Charity at Emmitsburg, Maryland, a foundation of Mother

Elizabeth Ann Bayley Seton.82 It was also from the Maryland foundation that the Cincinnati community of the Sisters of Charity had sprung. 83 Mother Mary Xavier Mehegan (18251915), the first Superior of the New Jersey Sisters, had orig-

inally been a Mount Sister.84 In 1859, when the New Jersey community was established in Newark, she became the Mother Superior of a group of five New Jersey nuns. These nuns, in 1858-1859, had been trained by Mother Margaret in Cincinnati,85 where Father Senez was then stationed.86 Mother

Margaret had known Mother Seton, and in 1853 had become the First Mother Superior of the Cincinnati community. 87 The Mount Sisters from St. Mary's parish had been in charge of St. Peter's School from 1863 to 1865, when they were replaced there by the Convent Sisters.88 These later established the first St. Aloysius Academy on what had been the site of the first St. Peter's Church, on Grand near Washington Street.89 

It was in the late 1860's, too, that the Convent Sisters came to St. Joseph's parish in Jersey City.90 Later, in 1886, when the first parochial school in St. Lucy's parish was opened, it was also conducted by the Convent Sisters, who lived at St. Mary's 

Convent in common with the Mount Sisters there.91 

The company of Convent Sisters who in 1906 replaced the Mount Sisters at St. Mary's parish, numbered twenty-two. The first Sister Servant (their name for Superior or Mother of the house) was Sister Maria Aloysius Farrow, born in Irvington,

N. J., in 1860 and baptized at St. Leo's Church there. Before 

1878, St. Leo's had no resident pastor, and Sunday Mass was said from the neighboring Seton Hall College. Each Sunday the visitor was hospitably received into the Farrow home, where he ate his breakfast. One of these visiting priests, Father

Sebastian G. Messmer, later Archbishop of Milwaukee, thus formed a life-long friendship with the family and with Sister Aloysius. After an elementary education in the schools of her native town, she studied at St. Mary's Academy in Newark, where the future nun specialized in music under Professor Hattersley. For a short time, she gave private instructions in music in her own home; but on July 2, 1881, she entered the 

Community of the Sisters of Charity at Convent. 

Sister Aloysius' great musical talents were put to good use in her new station in life. First at St. Elizabeth's at Convent, then at St. Joseph's in the Roxbury district of Boston, and

afterwards at St. Aloysius Academy in Jersey City, this gifted musician gave encouragement and inspiration to the very large number of boys and girls who came to her for their education; very many of her pupils now living look back to her "music room" with grateful affection. For the last twenty-three years of her life, as Sister Servant at St. Mary's, she continued to teach music while having the additional care of the orphans. 

Her death occurred June 7, 1929, on the feast of the Sacred

Heart. Amiable, kind, charitable to the poor, loved by the orphans and by all with whom she came in contact, Sister Aloysius was devoted to advancing the interests of those under her charge. Upon her wise judgment, in many parochial undertakings, Father TerWoert relied absolutely. 

Following her in order as Sister Servants at St. Mary's were 

Sister Louis Gonzaga Coady, Sister Marie Julia Lenihan, Sister Maria Augustine Flynn, Sister Mary Gertrude Quinlan, Sister Anna Raphael Bradley, and Sister Maria Angela Whelan. 

These other Superiors were not long in office; of them Sister 

Louis Gonzaga probably exerted the greatest influence in the parish school, as she served longer as Principal.92

In 1906, when the Convent Sisters took over the parish school and the orphan asylum, few teachers were called for, as the Christian Brothers were then in charge of the instruction of the boys, a situation which existed until 1927. 93 The withdrawal of the Brothers in that year, resulted in an increase in the staff of teachers furnished by the Sisters. It is recorded, incidentally, that the orphans in the asylum in these days shared with the children of the parish whatever educational advantages were offered.94

After 1906, the girls of the parish school on Third Street, upon completion of five years of schooling there, were sent for further education to the building at Jersey Avenue and Second Street, where the academy and asylum had formerly (18631906) been maintained by the Mount Sisters. In 1917, there were 1139 pupils in the parish school and the teaching staff consisted of five Brothers and seventeen Sisters.95 In 1932, there were 825 children in attendance at the parish school on

Third Street, and the teaching staff numbered fifteen religious and three lay teachers.96 In 1938, there are 611 children receiving instruction from fifteen Sisters and two lay teachers.97 

Monsignor TerWoert's judgment of the work done in the parochial school by the Convent Sisters was that of a pastor who was gratified that his school children met tests given by the public schools, a result that was likewise satisfactory to the Sisters and the curates who directed the school. One tradition of the Convent Sisters at St. Mary's has been that many vocations to the higher life have been the proof of the religious training given in the parochial school. The Sisters have always sought to inspire in the children childlike confidence, unaffected ease, and impartial judgment, and a spirit that creates and insures docility and respect for authority. It has been their observation that the religious point of view notable in St. 

Mary's graduates enables them to meet the difficulties of life with a truly Christian spirit of forbearance.98 

The Sisters' present convent is the rectory built by Father TerWoert in 1902-03 and renovated and remodelled by him in 1927-28.99 It is a four-story light brick and brown granite stone building, forty feet wide and eighty feet deep.100 It is known by the street number as 246 Second Street. Part of it is on the site of the very early rectory (1863-1868) which was adjacent to the church built by Father Senez.101 Later (1868-1870) this early rectory became the hospital of the Sisters of the Poor of St. Francis, and still later (1871-1900) it was occupied by the Christian Brothers as their dwelling. 

This early dwelling was demolished in 1900 by Father TerWoert.

The Sisters both from Mount St. Vincent (1862-1906) and from Convent (1906-1928) had dwelt in the old convent at 

Second Street and Jersey Avenue from 1862102 to August 15, 1928, when they took possession of the present convent. In its entrance hall hangs a large painting of the Virgin. This picture, in a Gothic frame, was long a striking ornament of the sanctuary of the church built by Father Senez, 103 and is among the few relics of that early church. Two crucifixes in the

convent, one in the refectory and the other in the community room, date back to the days of the Mount Sisters, and are cherished in tender remembrance.

The New School 

About the year 1913, an important change in parish plans was made. Generally it had been assumed that the accumulations of funds (1903-1913) from the Church Debt Society, which then amounted to $46,887.75, were to be expended in the construction of the upper church. Most parishioners were surprised when Father TerWoert announced that under the Bishop's instructions, the church funds would be used for the construction of a new school to replace the old Institute.104 

When Father TerWoert became pastor of St. Mary's in 

1900, the Institute erected thirty-four years earlier showed, like all city schools of its period, many signs of wear. Public 

School No. 1, built on York Street in 1848, had been demolished in 1897 and a new school erected in its place; Public 

School No. 2, erected on Erie Street in 1855, was, with its additions, about to be razed to give place to the present building which was completed in 1904; and all the other public schools in the lower city were soon to be replaced or abandoned.105 The steady tread and scuffle of children's feet year after year had left impressions, sometimes grooves, upon floors and stairways. The need for modern schools throughout the lower city was all too evident. Advances had been made in lighting, plumbing, heating, and ventilation; and the use of structural steel instead of wood now assured fireproof buildings. At St. Mary's, however, the erection of the basement of the present church seems to have delayed for several years the demolition of the old Institute and the construction of the present parish school.108

It was on Sunday, January 18, 1914, that Father TerWoert made the formal announcement that a new parochial school would soon be built.107 The plans were drawn by John T. 

Rowland, Jr., an experienced architect of Jersey City, who has supervised the erection of most of its numerous public and

many of its parochial schools. His father, a mason contractor, who kept his establishment on the south side of First Street east of Grove Street, was from 1880 to 1900 well known to the people of St. Mary's parish. He had been engaged a generation earlier on the construction of St. Joseph's Church at 

Baldwin and Pavonia Avenues.108 

Exercises were held at the Institute on Friday, June 12, 1914, to commemorate the closing of the old building, which was soon to be razed to make way for the present school.109 That evening two thousand former students assembled at the Holy Name Club House at 571 Jersey Avenue, and led by a band of musicians, marched to the old school, built almost fifty years earlier. There were speeches and musical selections until ten o'clock. Charles P. Olwell, a member of the House of

Assembly of New Jersey (1907-1910) acted as master of ceremonies. He first presented the pastor, Father TerWoert, who explained that the old school had long been inadequate, that he had purchased for the parish the apartment houses to the east of it, and had with him the plans of the new school. 

These he gladly exhibited and explained. He was enthusiastically applauded. 

The next speaker was Rt. Rev. Henry A. Brann, D.D., then pastor of St. Agnes Church in New York City. Monsignor 

Brann, as we have seen, had been a popular curate under Father 

Senez in the middle 1860's, when the Institute was built.110

After having successfully undertaken the collection of the money to build it, he had been among its first principals. He told his audience how the funds were raised and the building erected. An excellent talker, he made a speech, described as interesting, and treating fully the struggles, trials, and tribulations of the old days. Unfortunately, the details of his discourse are not now ascertainable.

Another speaker was the late Vice-Chancellor John Griffin (1858-1927), an outstanding parishioner from 1880 to 1900.

As a little boy, in the 1870's, he had attended the Institute.¹¹¹ 

In the practice of the law, he was a counsellor well fitted to

advise. As Counsel to the Board of Freeholders of Hudson

County (1896-1913) he manifested unfailing devotion to his work and to the interests to the public. His fourteen years (1913-1927) on the equity bench of New Jersey were marked with great toil and greater honor.112 Vice-Chancellor Griffin was followed by Brother Eliphus, director of the Christian Brothers, who was at that period teaching the boys. Other alumni of the Institute who spoke were: the Honorable Thomas 

J. Brogan,113 then City Attorney, later Corporation Counsel, and now (1938) Chief-Justice of the Supreme Court of New Jersey, the first Catholic to hold that position; and Father 

John J. Murphy,114 then stationed at St. Bridget's, Jersey City, and now Monsignor and pastor at Sacred Heart Church in the Vailsburgh section of Newark. All the speakers praised the old school, and the Brothers who had taught them. Musical selections were rendered by Father John E. Kiernan,115 Mrs. 

May McCarthy, and John W. Torney. After the meeting, the younger folk returned to the Holy Name Club House to dance. 

On the ladies' committee were the Misses May McGrath, Theresa Conk, Mary O'Connell, Kathryn Grady, Anna Tewlin,

Nora Fitzgerald, and Mary Hagan. They had been among the more recent graduates of the Sisters' school. 

The gentlemen on the committee in charge of this last gathering in the auditorium of the old Institute included Mr. 

Olwell, Mr. Thomas P. Ward, who had officiated as secretary for the Committee, Monsignor Murphy, James T. Mackey, Nicholas P. Wedin (sometime Sheriff of Hudson County), William H. Clossey, Frank P. Lehane, Joseph Dwyer, David A. 

Newton, John J. McCabe, George A. McCabe, Joseph F. 

Brandt, Joseph G. Miller, Bartholomew J. Kiernan, Luke J.

O'Malley, and Matthew A. Donellan. At some time during the preceding forty years, all these men had been under the instruction of the Christian Brothers at the Institute. Several had been members of the Catholic Institute Alumni Association, which existed for several years after its formation in 1908. Among others prominent in this Alumni Association were Joseph L. Davis, Joseph F. Farmer, James A. McCormack,

Thomas J. McInerny, Charles E. Sweeney, James A. Sullivan, and Benjamin Treacy.' 116 117 One month later, on July 12, 1914, Bishop O'Connor officiated at the laying of the corner stone of the new school. 

Addresses were made by Monsignor TerWoert and the Rev. 

John J. Murphy. Mr. Rowland, the architect, was present.'

The new school, known as St. Mary's School, was completed and ready for use in the fall of 1916. It had cost approximately $115,000.118 The opening was marked by a fair, held in the spacious auditorium of the new building, which netted about $6000.119

The school is a three-story brick and terra-cotta building. It has sixteen classrooms, a music room, a drawing room, a prize room, a library, a storeroom, and two offices, all in use.120 It has a frontage of 157 feet on Third Street, and is almost 100 feet deep.121 Adjoining it in the rear and towards the west, but facing Second Street, is the well-planned convent of the 

Sisters, 122

Financing the New Church 

In 1913, Father TerWoert issued a financial statement which carried in full detail the receipts and disbursements of the parish from June 1, 1900, the day on which he had become pastor, to December 31, 1913, the day of its date.128 In twelve years and seven months, the congregation, under his skilled direction, had for parish purposes contributed what for that period was the large total of $516,474.81. From this sum had been paid all the current expenses, as well as the costs of acquiring lands, and of building and furnishing the new rectory and the basement of the present church. There was on hand, safely invested, a balance of $77,610.13, as compared to a balance of $2,899.87, the cash on hand on June 1, 1900, and a net debt which then amounted to about $28,000. This debt apparently included a mortgage of $16,000 on the Club House. 

A close study of Father TerWoert's statement repays the student, and develops many points of interest. But its deepest significance is not apparent, and lies beneath the surface. It actually demonstrates mathematically a constant well-developed

spirit of generosity on the part of the congregation, made up almost entirely of wage earners of Irish birth or descent, who loyally supported, as they were bound by precept, a pastor of marked business ability, although not a few of them, in a thoughtless or impatient moment, might call him "The Dutchman." That term, with his excellent sense of humor, he occasionally used in banter when speaking of himself.124

Church Debt Society 

In a record of the material aid essential to the spiritual development of the parish, it would be an oversight not to dwell upon the intensive work of those young men and women who, in the first quarter of the twentieth century, religiously collected an important item of revenue of the church. There were two periods of operation. The first period was that of the Church Debt Society from 1902 to 1920; and the second, that of the New Church Building Fund Association from 1920 

to 1927.

It had been in no small measure the activities of the Church Debt Society that enabled Father TerWoert, in 1913, to announce that the splendid rectory on Second Street and the large basement of the present church had been fully paid for, and that the congregation had on hand a balance of $77,610.13. 

This sum, with which he originally intended to meet the cost of the upper church, became, due to the change in plans, the nucleus of the sum of approximately $115,000, expended in 1914-1915 in the erection of the new parish school. 

It is difficult after a score of years to obtain exact details as to the operations of the Church Debt Society, but as its work and that of the later New Church Building Fund Association were similarly done, a consideration of the activities of the latter Association will suffice, for their common concern was to bring in regularly, month by month, a steady flow of revenue.

Among the original Church Debt Collectors before 1916 were: Anna Anderson, Claire Blanchfield, Florence Boylan, Frances Boylan, Anna Cassels, Sarah Cassels (Sister Mary

Anna), Mary Crystal, Anna Cunningham, Margaret Deven,

There is preserved a well-worn account book, kept from 

May 1, 1920 to December 31, 1926. Like a collection list, it signifies more than names and figures. From it, it may be gathered that during the first three weeks of 1920, Fathers 

Fitzpatrick, Henebry, and Finnerty canvassed the parish for members of the New Church Building Fund Association, and succeeded in enrolling 1721 persons, each of whom promised to contribute fifty cents a month. All made their initial contributions, which amounted to $860.50. Thereafter, until the end of 1926, collections were made by young men and women of the parish; their total number, through these seven years, was seventy-three. In 1920, there were employed upon this work forty-six persons, thirteen were men, and thirty-three were women. In 1926, there were employed five men, and thirty-four women. The collection made by the three curates in May, 1920, was outstanding, as the records for the remainder of the year indicate the average monthly collection was but $574. In 1921, it was $456. It then gradually declined until, at the end of 1926, it had fallen to $201. One notes the decrease of members through the years on the outer edges of the parish-Henderson Street, Grove Street, Monmouth Street, Newark Avenue, and Seventh Street.

During these seven years, Father Henebry was the "archcollector," as he is styled in the old account book. One pauses at the reverent R.I.P. against the names of both James Doyle and Nellie McCabe, earnest workers, whose parochial activities were not limited to the task of collecting. Marriages of young

III 

women members of the Association are indicated; and it is apparent that their new duties made necessary the appointment of successors. Removals of collectors from the parish are also evidenced. The regularity of the entries of the amounts received from the various collectors demonstrate that all the members took their duties very seriously and made prompt returns. The businesslike pastor is revealed again by entries so made that a glance reveals how the collections, month after month and year by year, compare, and this quick view is achieved through a simple system of single entries. One can easily visualize the collectors as, month by month, usually in their own neighborhoods, they climbed flight after flight of stairs.

There are preserved some of the small placards issued as membership cards in the New Church Building Fund Associa tion.126 Upon each is a small encircled picture of the flat-roofed basement of the present church, of the rectory then at 246 Second Street (now the Convent), and of the present parish school. Over the rector's name are stated the object, rules, and advantages of the society; under it is the significant thoroughgoing admonition: "Keep this card in sight; the collector will call the first week in each month." One concludes that these collections for school and church in an industrial city of America were of a faithful piety akin to that which centuries ago was the spiritual foundation of the universities and cathedrals of the historic towns of Europe; and that these collectors for 

St. Mary's were moved by something even greater than the admirable spirit of the well-sung ancient messengers who, upon their appointed rounds, were never delayed by heat or cold, storm or flood.127
