CHAPTER IX FATHER TERWoert and HIS COMPLETED WORK 

IT WAS in the spring of 1924 that Father TerWoert, at the age of seventy-one years, made his final and major appeal to his flock. On Sunday evening, April 29, 1924, in the school auditorium, an audience of three hundred men of the parish responded to his call for a discussion of the ways and means to raise $100,000 within a brief period, so that the work of completing St. Mary's Church, then already in course of construction, could proceed with the least possible delay. He was a commanding figure as he came upon the stage to open the meeting. Terse and forceful as was his custom, he wasted no time upon the preliminaries. In what he said, he told the whole story.¹ 1 The campaign, he revealed, was not the result of a hasty decision, nor had he decided upon a means of raising the required funds without due thought and deliberation. "I have been planning and working for this drive, myself and a gentleman I have brought here tonight, for more than a year,” he declared at the outset of his remarks, and "I have discussed the proposed undertaking with the Bishop and I have his hearty approval." 

As I told the Bishop, so now I wish to tell you, I am not doing this for myself. I am here today, and may be gone tomorrow. Whatever benefits are derived from the campaign will accrue to you, your children, and your children's children.

I know that you are all as desirous as I am to erect a fitting place of worship; a suitable temple to honor and glorify God. 

It is a noble task, worthy of the best efforts of you who have so much at heart the welfare of the parish, of which you are and have been such loyal members.

When I first came here you know what were the conditions prevailing in the parish of St. Mary. There was facing 113

us a debt of $28,000, but that was lifted in six months with the zeal and vigor that have always characterized your response to the call of your church and pastor. Then came the tornado, and all that had been accomplished in your years of effort and sacrifice was leveled in twenty-five minutes. Other parishes might have been discouraged, but not St. Mary's.

We all realized that the thing to do was to get on the job at once. The tornado wrecked our church on Saturday. The very next day, the old schoolhouse many of you knew so well was utilized as a place of worship. Then it became immediately necessary to put the priests' house in a condition fit for living therein. New property was bought at a cost of $65,000, and a home for the Christian Brothers was secured at a cost of $5500.

Then arose the need of a new school; and three additional pieces of property, costing $17,500, passed into the possession of the parish. Finally came the purchase of another parcel of ground, with $12,000 as the consideration. You men in those days of wonderful sacrifices proved that you felt best when you were spending your money in behalf of the church. During all that time, while the splendid structures now listed among the parish possessions were being erected, there was no diminution in the funds you poured into the parish treasury to meet current needs.

Now the time has arrived when there is only one thing needed: the completion of our church. We need for this purpose $100,000, in addition to the $600,000 which has been contributed by you and set aside to erect this place of worship. 

To collect the sum essential for the beginning of this great task, I am sincerely desirous of enlisting the services of a gentleman who has achieved wonderful success in raising large sums of money in brief periods for Catholic Church welfare work.

This man is now engaged in raising $250,000 for the completion of a Cathedral in the Wheeling (West Virginia) Diocese. He is at the present time also at work on other

drives in different sections of the country. I consider his plan of campaign peculiarly well adapted to St. Mary's parish, and 

I feel certain that you will tender him your hearty support in his work among you. Since I came to the pastorate of St. 

Mary's twenty-four years ago, I have never had reason to be disappointed in you. I know that it is within your power, and that you will only too willingly assume the task of erecting our church, and that at its completion, in accordance with the great generosity you have always displayed, it will be without a penny of indebtedness."

The goal of $100,000 was not reached. After six weeks, however, the subscriptions amounted to $54,210, a sum which was slighly increased by a few later contributions. For the convenience of those who were unable to meet their pledges at once, subsequent monthly meetings were held. The final formal words of the appeal for funds, as they appear in the sixth and last number of "The Local Motive," which was published weekly during the campaign, are these: "What good is it to be promising, if we fail to pay? No good—absolutely no good at all. On the contrary, an unfilled promise is a boomerang that wrecks our reputation. And to pay promptly -ah, that's the point. 'He who gives quickly, gives twice.'

Cynics claim to find in the adage a meaning never put there by the author the chance of getting from the prompt giver a second donation. Making a pledge and paying it quickly are credentials of St. Mary's parishioners. Making a pledge and paying it as quickly as possible also indicates the Parishioner of Quality." 

The list of subscribers to this collection of 1924 number 983 (families rather than individuals), and carries the names of many who appear on the subscription list of 1901. The presence of numerous new, and the absence of many old names, indicate that for a quarter of a century the population of a city parish is markedly transient. 

Fifty-nine extraordinary contributions range from $150 to $2000. One gift of $2000 is the pledge of the girls of the paro-

chial school. A second gift of that amount is a contribution of a group of sisters, the Misses Fitzgerald, whose parents and kin were among the first members of the parish. The wisdom of investments in real estate in lower Jersey City, made by the older generation, was now manifest. From James J. McCue comes a contribution of $1500, and Michael J. Heffernan and 

George Richter each contribute $1000. In all these cases the gifts are a testimony of the fact that bread cast upon the waters a quarter of a century earlier had returned many fold, for these men in their younger years had been contributors according to their means, then comparatively slight. The Holy Name 

Society, now a vigorous unit, donates $1000. The other nine hundred and odd contributors represent prosperous real estate brokers, stock brokers, furniture dealers, plumbers, physicians, lawyers, tradesmen, and the families of school teachers active and retired, of secretaries and stenographers, and those engaged in different kinds of hard labor. 

One notes donations by Rev. Dr. Bryan, rector of Grace 

Episcopal Church of Van Vorst; Hebrew merchants; The 

Ladies Auxiliary of the Third Ward Democratic Club; numerous former parishioners then dwelling outside the parish. Mrs. 

Veronica Smith gives in memory of her parents, John and Veronica TerWoert. A widow's mite, contributed in 1901, is reflected in substantial donations from her son, and his son and daughter; and another mother, who had contended herself with little that her children might secure the advantages of extended schooling, is remembered in the subscription of her quiet, diligent and bereaved husband. Vigorous politicians of the lower city give generously.

These figures for Grove Street and Jersey Avenue indicate that Irish Catholic families are settling on Jersey Avenue after leaving Grove Street, where Jews, Italians, and Poles are now (1938) well established.

Henderson Street is represented by only three families as against fifty-seven in 1901; Railroad Avenue by eight as against twenty-six; Newark Avenue by fourteen as against thirty;

Thus it is seen, the congregation has become smaller. 

The officers in the campaign of 1924 were:

Chairman, William F. Sullivan, and Vice-Chairman, John J. Corcoran, who were both then, as now (1938), lay trustees of the church; 

Secretary, William H. Clossey, and the Associate Secretaries, Margaret Fitzgerald, Agnes Farmer, Helen McCauley, Josephine Mulligan, Thomas A. Copeland, and Patrick J. Brennan; 

Floormen, James J. Ford, William A. McDonald, James M. 

O'Rourke, Michael Downs, John A. Crystal, and Thomas 

Heffernan. The Campaign Manager employed by Father Ter- 

Woert was Daniel B. O'Loughlin, a resident of Philadelphia, whose business it was to manage similar campaigns for funds. 

It had been Father TerWoert's hope that the names of all the 1016 families in the parish would be listed in a souvenir book as having made contributions in accordance with their means, but the book was not published. His views, at seventyone years of age, were thus set forth:

Once again has St. Mary's parish been subjected to the acid test of loyalty and fidelity to church and pastor, and once again has that element of the parishioners, always to be found in the vanguard when the welfare of their holy faith is concerned, acquitted itself with glory and credit.

Always, in all parishes, are to be found those who fail to consider seriously their duty towards the support of the church and spiritual

directors. Their neglect in many instances is due rather to carelessness and thoughtlessness than to a studied effort to shift to the shoulders of others the entire burden, instead of assuming the portion of it that is rightly theirs.

And always, in all parishes, are to be found loyal, devoted, faithful parishioners, ever ready and uncomplaining, no matter what may be asked of them by their Church and pastor. These admirable adherents of their holy faith perform prodigies in its behalf whenever and wherever they are summoned to its standard.

They perform not only their full duty, but invariably they take up the burden of others as well able to assist as themselves. A mere handful at times, when assemblages are called to further this or that project for the best interests of their Church, always they may be depended upon to make up in enthusiasm and generosity what they lack in numbers. 

They are the stalwarts, the champions, the bone and sinew of the parish. Without them, failure would result where always splendid victories are achieved. Were it not for such as these, the Catholic 

Church would fall behind, rather than press ever onward. 

St. Mary's is particularly blessed in having within its membership a large contingent of stalwarts of this character. They have attended all meetings of the campaign now closed, except where circumstances absolutely prevented their presence. They have given not only all, but, in many instances, more than they could afford. They have been loyal to their God and their church; they have upheld the hands of their pastor; they have proven they are of the mettle of which true 

Christians are made.

To such as these, the success of the present campaign is sufficient reward for the sacrifices they have so cheerfully made. Quietly and unassumingly, they have gone about the task of giving, without thought of the distinction which their gifts might bring them. They would have been as well pleased had their names been withheld from publication; had no mention whatever been made of their generosity, their true Catholic spirit, their burning desire to participate whole-heartedly in any movement designed for the benefit of their Church and pastor. 

But justice demands that their names be enrolled high up in the hall of fame of St. Mary's parish, not only because of their great work for their holy religion, but to serve as an example for those who have not yet come to a full realization of the duty they also owe to their sustaining faith. It is for this reason principally, that the names of all subscribers are printed in "Local Motive.”2

Work on the upper church was begun in the fall of 1924 by Hugh Montague and Son, who practically and satisfactorily

completed it two years later. The whole church edifice represents an investment of a million dollars.4 5 In 1924, when Robert J. Reiley, a noted contemporaneous church architect, prepared his plans for the superstructure of the new St. Mary's, his chief problem was to pay proper regard to the limitations placed upon his designs by reason of the fact that the substructure, that is to say the present church basement built by Father TerWoert in 1902-1903, predetermined the location of the exterior walls and the columns of the upper church. When the basement was built, sketches for the superstructure had been made, but they could not be found in 1924.

The plans of the lower church, however, were of course, still available; and the reputation of the eminent architect, William H. Schickel, who had designed it, gave confidence that the foundations were well laid and adequate to support any superstructure. Moreover, an independent investigation which was made in 1924 by Mr. Reiley, gave double assurance that the church proper, which he and Monsignor TerWoert envisaged, could be safely built." 

The architectural style selected for the superstructure was 

Byzantine Romanesque. An outstanding example of this archi tectural design is the great Cathedral of Monreale, built in 1167, five miles southwest of Palermo, in Sicily. In large measure, the Italian Cathedral was the model of the present St. Mary's. Byzantine is usually regarded as the architectural style of the East, and Romanesque as that of the West, but along the trade routes of the Mediterranean the two styles seem to have met and mingled, and there thus resulted the charming combination seen at Monreale. St. Mary's church suffers, of course, like many another city church, because it is set into a closely built neighborhood, and it has none of the advantages that its simple and stately lines would have if it were upon a slight eminence and in proper surroundings in regard to landscape. Nevertheless, it is imposing externally.⁹ 

The weathered granite of the basement of the last generation blends well with the light yellow of the brick, and the terracotta and the limestone of the more recently erected super-

structure; and their combined effect is pleasant to the eye. Seen from the hill section of Jersey City, the church, for lack of a tower, is not an outstanding edifice, although, by those who know its location and its slanting roof, it is easily distinguished from the adjacent buildings of the lower City.10 The question of erecting a tower was duly considered by Monsignor Ter- Woert. He finally determined that it should not be built. 

Uppermost in his mind, of course, was the havoc wrought to the old church by the tornado of August 24, 1901. 11 The present church of St. Mary's parish, at Erie and Second 

Streets, occupies a plot of land which is about one hundred feet wide and two hundred feet deep. The present parish rectory, known as 219 Third Street, is built into the northern end of the edifice, and its interior is dominated by the circular apse of the church. In great part, the site of this new church is the plot upon which, in 1861-1863, Father Senez built the edifice which is now familiarly spoken of as Father Senez's church.12 

When the new church was built, in 1926, Monsignor Ter- Woert had practically accomplished the tasks he had clearly foreseen in 1900, for then, in his farewell sermon at St. John's, he had asked his congregation to pray for him in his labors at St. Mary's. And while it is true that the demolition of the material works of Dean Senez - the old church, the Institute, and the old Academy and Asylum-brought sorrow to many because of the holy memories and the blessed associations they all evoked,¹³ yet in 1930 few of the old parishioners remained. 

At hand was a new generation of worshipers, and at their service and for their convenience and edification, were modern buildings and accommodations, free of debt, as had been ably and carefully planned by Monsignor TerWoert, who always steadily saw his problems in their many aspects. 

Dedication of the New St. Mary's 

On Sunday, September 26, 1926, Monsignor TerWoert witnessed with deep content the solemn dedication of the new St. 

Mary's Church, which he had erected.14 Rt. Rev. John J.

15 Murphy was celebrant of the first High Mass, Coram Pontifice, at eleven o'clock. Bishop O'Connor blessed the new church before the Mass and immediately after the procession of the visiting and local clergy from the rectory to the church.¹ 

He was assisted by Rt. Rev. Thomas H. McLaughlin, then 

President of Seton Hall College. Rev. James J. Smith of St. 

Mary's Church, Rutherford, and Rev. William J. Landers of

St. Aloysius, Jersey City, were respectively deacon and subdeacon. The master of ceremonies was Rev. Thomas F. Curry. 

The deacons of honor were Very Rev. William A. Griffin, diocesan director of the Society for the Propagation of the 

Faith, and Very Rev. William F. Lawlor, diocesan superintendent of parochial schools. 

A mixed choir of twenty-six voices sang Victor Hammerall's 

Mass. Professor John J. Keogh presided at the organ and directed the choir. He was assisted by Mrs. Ella Newman, formerly organist at St. Mary's. The soloists were Courtney 

Casler, baritone, Mrs. Lela Gordon Sailing of San Francisco, and the Misses Irene Lynch, Marion Vernon, Mary Languinette, and Anna Forystal, and Messrs. John Veigh and William 

Clancy. Mrs. Newman sang Gounod's "Ave Maria" at the

Offertory. As the procession entered, a "Vivat," arranged by her, was rendered by the chancel choir of sixty-five boys and the regular choir. As a recessional, the full choir sang Handel's "Rejoice in the Lord." Mrs. Sailing, one of the soloists, had studied for the lyric stage in Leipsic and other European musical centers. 

Rev. Edward A. Gilgin, of the Order of St. Sulpice, preached the sermon at the Mass. He took his text from Psalm CXXI: "I rejoiced at the things that were said to me: we shall go into the house of the Lord." He stressed the beauty of the new edifice, but emphasized that it was not an end in itself but a means to the service of God and the sanctification of souls. He dwelt upon the efficient services rendered the parish by the Sisters of Charity and the Christian Brothers, and the seventy-five vocations they had helped cultivate, which is one of the best proofs of a sound religious life.16

Solemn Vespers in the evening brought another crowded attendance. Monsignor TerWoert occupied a seat close to the 

Bishop's throne on the Gospel side of the sanctuary. Father 

Landers was celebrant, and Fathers Fitzpatrick and Henebry were assistants. Mr. Casler rendered an "Ave Maria" set to 

Mascagni's famous intermezzo in Cavalleria Rusticana; and Mr. 

Clancy sang Mozart's "O Salutaris." Rev. John E. Kiernan, who as a boy had grown up in St. Mary's, preached the sermon, in which he paraphrased interestingly the early history of the parish. At his first Mass, celebrated twenty years earlier in the basement of the church, the sermon had been delivered by Monsignor TerWoert. The collections at both services amounted to $5072. It was worthy of remark that on Jersey City Heights within the same week, there were dedicated the Simpson Grace Methodist Episcopal Church at the Boulevard and Tonnele Avenue, and the Temple Beth-el at the Boulevard and Harrison Avenue.¹7 

Father TerWoert, Priest and Builder

A layman, reading the letters, news-articles, financial statements, and other written and printed material which Father TerWoert preserved, comes easily to the conclusion that had this priest followed a worldly career, he would have been a dominant figure. That he was a marvel of energy is evidenced by testimony of long standing. As early as 1884, an older priest, from whom he sought advice, wrote him: "He [Bishop 

Wigger] replied that you were energetic and that Bishop 

O'Farrell would not part with you."18 This is in the early days of the Diocese of Trenton, when Father TerWoert was pastor at Lambertville, which originally was in the Diocese of Newark. A quarter of a century later, the historian of the 

Diocese of Trenton, probably reflecting a traditional judgment, characterizes Father TerWoert, in the language of the period, as "strenuous."19 And about the same time, it was a matter of comment by Bishop O'Connor and the members of the Diocesan Council that Father TerWoert, their colleague (18961914), without any apparent fatigue, had the strength and endurance to render in connection with the cemeteries many services which the Bishop appreciated so highly.20

In his thoroughness, Father TerWoert, in the opinion of some persons, may seem occasionally to have overshot a mark. 

For instance, on the eve of Christmas in 1888, he addressed to his brethren at St. John's in Jersey City a circular letter, in which he praises the handsome generosity of what was then a poor but growing congregation.21 Pointing out its increasing needs, he expresses his desire to show the results of the generosity of his flock- a desire becoming more manifest in the buildings, "a splendid pile that when finished will be a lasting monument of ardent zeal for the welfare of our holy religion, and a silent witness to remind the children of a faith enlivened by good works." There follows a short review of the first four years of the parish: all the real estate had cost $7937.23; the little church with its furnishings, $3220.76; and the parish school with every item of expense, $14,673.53. There was no debt; the property was the congregation's; and "no man waves a bond and mortgage over our heads as an evidence of indebtness." He then continues: "How you must hate to think that so many, some of whom you know perhaps, try to escape without doing anything except to take their share of the honors. 

Whilst you always try to proportion your gift according to your means, you see others around you, with ample means, giving according to their meanness. But let them go; they will bewail their mistake some day." And at the end of the letter his usual thoroughness shows itself: "The envelope in which this circular is enclosed, you will be good enough to return with your Christmas contribution enclosed. P. S. If you can't be present at the Holy Sacrifices of the Mass on Christmas, then send your envelope with someone that you know will present it."

A considered judgment, however, is that this letter of December 20, 1888, written by Father TerWoert in his thirtyfifth year, reveals him as he was essentially throughout his long active life: a priest quite determined, for himself and the souls under his care, to demonstrate that the Catholic faith is a living faith, and that upon both pastor and flock there constantly rests the duty of timely and adequate provision for the

means of practicing and cultivating the Faith, of educating the children in it, and giving sufficient comfort to the priests and Sisters. He had thought out these matters, and was a realist who saw the necessity of the material side of life. And, if perforce, in the performance of his duties he found it necessary to criticise those who did not perform theirs, he meant to make his criticism impressive.

He was outright in deeds as in words. There is an indication that in the early 1880's, when an injunction was delaying the building of the school sewer at Lambertville, his first parochial charge, he disregarded the injunction and had the sewer built.22 

And there is a tradition that while St. John's Church in Jersey 

City was in the course of construction and there was suddenly handed to him a writ, he chased the process server in an attempt to make him take back the paper.23 Of course, the numerous changes that followed his arrival at St. Mary's in 1900 have no special significance, for new pastors like new brooms often sweep clean; and he had special problems. Once, bent upon ridding that parish of an offensive saloon, he created a sensation by naming from the pulpit well-known police officers whom he regarded as laggards in the performance of their duty. He remarked that they were "as gentle as doves."24

About 1913 and 1914, there are letters that indicate a determination upon his part to be aloof. One earnest and patient co-worker, seeking highly necessary cooperation in a diocesan matter, does not find it easy to establish communication with Father TerWoert.25 Concerning changes in assistants at St. 

Mary's, Father TerWoert sends by a friend of talent26 a message to Bishop O'Connor. The latter feels it a duty to write firmly: "I only ask you to possess your soul in patience," and quoting current slang by an expression which still survives, the Bishop assures Father TerWoert thus: "There is no intention of 'getting your goat' or anybody's goat for that matter."27

Perhaps Father TerWoert had used the expression first. Indeed, in this particular situation, it is to be remarked that there was an attitude on the part of the priest which was similar

to that taken by him more than ten years earlier, when Bishop Wigger expressed in writing his great surprise that Father 

TerWoert had presumed to dismiss summarily a young curate.28

Bishop O'Connor's letter bears also upon Father TerWoert's resignation from the Diocesan Board of Consultors after a service of eighteen years. What the Bishop writes is: "Your resignation from the Board of Consultors was, as I understood it, addressed to the Board, and a resolution was passed at the last meeting accepting the resignation and expressing appreciation of your services, which resolution the Secretary (Rt. Rev. 

Joseph H. Meehan), was directed to send you." Although the resolution had been unanimously adopted, the Bishop is almost abrupt; no personal note comes in. In view of the resignation, the method of communication, and the Bishop's tone, one senses a matter of considerable importance in the background, especially since in the preceding summer (1913), Father Ter- Woert had announced to the Bishop that he desired to be relieved of his responsibilities in regard to the cemeteries in 

Hudson County.29

Perhaps, however, it was the poor health of the priest in these years that was having its effect upon his outlook. For we know that toward the end of 1909, when he was fifty-six years old, Father TerWoert's robust health gave way after a long period of strenuous labor, which included the management of the Holy Name Cemetery in Jersey City, and the acquisition, organization, and early development of Holy Cross Cemetery at North Arlington, New Jersey. Through the winter of 1909 and well into the spring of 1910,30 he suffered from an attack of phlebitis marked by an embolism, which, however, was duly absorbed in the course of time, although for several years he showed its effects.

There is happily an affectionate letter, towards the end of 1913, from his old friend and instructor at Seton Hall, Archbishop Messmer of Milwaukee, at whose consecration as Bishop of Green Bay on March 27, 1892, Father TerWoert had read the papal brief of appointment, and whose elevation as Archbishop of Milwaukee in 1903 had given the younger man

cause to rejoice. In his journeys to and from Rome, the Archbishop usually stopped for a night or two at St. Mary's rectory, where he was entertained by Father TerWoert. He was surprised and grieved in the fall of 1913 to find the priest markedly ill and nervous. The old instructor was eager to have his pupil come to Milwaukee for a visit, which would brace him up and send him back to Jersey a healthy man after they had refreshed themselves, like the old chums they were, "with happy memories of Seton Hall-old Seton Hall.”31

In his younger days, Father TerWoert was well known as a gifted speaker;32 and his almost gigantic stature and bright red beard made him an attractive and commanding figure in the pulpit. And though for many years preceding his death, an affliction of the throat prevented him from delivering frequent sermons, yet on those rare occasions when he preached, his message was always impressive. An excellent example of pulpit oratory was his tribute at Christmas time in 1917, to the young men then in military service; after a score of years it is still well remembered.34 us.

The World War (1914-1918) was the third within the lives of some of the oldest members of St. Mary's parish. It was brought home to them, as to all Catholics, by the widely circulated statement made on April 6, 1917 by James Cardinal

Gibbons, who said: "It behoves every American citizen to do his duty, and to uphold the hands of the President and the legislative department in the solemn obligation that confronts "35 And it was near the church door itself that Father TerWoert, in the spring of that year, blessed the flag pole, still standing, which was the gift of Division No. Six of the 

Ancient Order of Hibernians.86 Upon it, Peggy Ferris, a daughter of James Ferris (1855-1914), a deceased trustee and noted benefactor of the church, raised the flag for the first time. Soon afterwards, on a summer Sunday morning as the people entered for Mass, the ladies of the Red Cross solicited funds for the wounded.37 Then came the selective draft, the drawings of the numbers of the men, the assignment to camps, the voyages over-seas, the actual service, the anx-

iously awaited news, the armistice, the treaty of peace, the return home, and finally the parish re-union, held in the Auditorium of the School on November 28, 1919. Rev. John J.

Finnerty, an army chaplain who had been a curate at St. Mary's, was the orator of the celebration.3 38 In all, for service on land and sea and in the air, 386 men of St. Mary's enlisted. Sixteen died in service.39 They were:

Father TerWoert, if the occasion demanded, would manifest physical as well as moral courage. Even today after almost forty years, among the older parishioners at St. John's, his first charge in Jersey City, there are recollections of his determination and thoroughness.40 Before that parish was well built up and still had its lonesome stretches, Father TerWoert, in face-to-face encounters, would rout hoodlums who annoyed

and accosted him;41 and by his presence, he succeeded not infrequently in keeping his parishioners from the haunts of the sporting coteries who cruelly matched young men to fight for blood, and from the establishments of dance hall proprietors who were potential nuisances. 42 He was no respecter of persons, knew no fear, and was often blunt. During his early years at St. Mary's, his duty as he saw it required him, now and again, to talk earnestly, frankly, and masterfully, in and out of parish gatherings, and occasionally from the altar, by way of admonition and correction. But withal, he was essentially gracious, especially in his later years.43 

He was strong-minded, and even, after a fashion, imperious; and in appearance he was stern. But his intimate friends who understood him, found him human at heart, and to the sincere quality of his friendship and generosity many could testify.¹¹

Two, of several incidents, show him as a man of tender feeling. 

After the World War, at the assembly held on November 28, 1919 in the auditorium of the new school for the unveiling of the bronze tablet which honors the soldiers of the parish, he presented, with solemn ceremony, a gold cross to every mother who had lost a son during the struggle, and a bronze cross to every boy who after service had returned home safe.

Then, some five years later, though he had banned a public celebration on the occasion of his golden jubilee as a priest, he commemorated it, deeply significantly, by presenting to each of his three curates a gold chalice.* 45 The daily tasks and duties which Father TerWoert had religiously performed as the busy pastor of congregations who looked on, as churches, schools, rectories, and other parish buildings rose in the air, sometimes told upon his health. 

This was especially apparent at St. Mary's, and after he had passed his fifty-fifth year. Nevertheless, he could generally be relied upon to give time and attention to affairs and problems which were extra-parochial. His services as a diocesan consultor¹6 covered eighteen years (1896-1914), and, as already

13I seen, he was for nearly the same period concerned with the management of Holy Name Cemetery in Jersey City.“ 

47 Now and again matters of consequence engaged Father TerWoert's abilities. In 1901, he acted for Bishop O'Connor upon a problem raised by a group of Polish Catholics who would not remain affiliated with the general body of their coreligionists;18 in 1904, he was one of the incorporators of the 

Diocese of Newark, then established as a corporate legal entity under the statute of laws of New Jersey. For several years after 1905, it was his special task to negotiate the purchase of lands for the Holy Cross Cemetery in Arlington, New Jersey, to secure the passage of important legislation in regard to its operation, to direct its proper development, and to manage its finances.49 In 1915, when nearly all saloonkeepers in Jersey City by opening their places on Sundays violated the provisions of what was familiarly known as the Bishops' Law, Father TerWoert was a member of the group of pastors whose task it was to help secure obedience to the statute.5⁰ And in the years after the World War, his membership in the Friends of Irish Freedom was the result of no sudden resolution or a mere gesture, for long before that he had been deeply interested in the United Irish League, and had attended at least one national convention of that body.51 He was a remarkably active man. 

Letters of Monsignors James F. Mooney52 and Hubert J.

Behr written to Father TerWoert more than a generation ago, still breathe affection.53 Neighboring shepherds of other faiths admired him too; a note of thanks to him for a copy of a work of St. Francis de Sales reveals the high esteem in which one Protestant minister held him.54 Bishop O'Connor, younger by but two years, wrote a meaningful letter of congratulation on the priest's approaching golden jubilee of his ordination (May 25, 1925), and noted pleasurably that the occasion was to be marked by the priest's appointment as Monsignor.55 The formal document was received on June 3, 1925.56 In a congratulatory message, a former parishioner at St. John's, Jersey

City, was of firm opinion that the honor was long overdue.57

One imagines, however, that a bishop had a better perspective; and so a laborious career of half a century was properly and happily appreciated and marked. But the worn and experienced laborer in the Lord's vineyard, one thinks, then attached no very deep spiritual significance to the purple robe.58 

Orphan Asylum Discontinued 

In 1925, before the death of Sister Maria Aloysius Farrow, it was deemed advisable by Father TerWoert to discontinue St. Mary's Orphan Asylum, which had been founded by 

Father Senez in 1860 with the approval of Bishop Bayley.59

The children in residence were placed in homes, or were transferred to other orphanages under the direction of the Sisters of Charity; and there was thus brought to an end the last main phase of parochial endeavor with which Dean Senez had been connected.

Many of the girls sheltered in the orphanage developed into sturdy, self-reliant young women; and a list of the various callings they followed show that some filled important clerical positions while others became teachers in the public schools.60 

It has been observed that all reflected credit on the good Sisters who reared them. The Sisters sought to cultivate in every orphan girl a spirit of self-helpfulness, a sense of responsibility, and the idea that she should consider herself as a potential, respectable and respected citizen; and all these laudable objectives went back to the first days of old Sister Assisium of the Mount, whose benevolent lifework was spent in the service of the little ones, beloved of God.61

Death and Funeral of Monsignor TerWoert 

In the 1920's, Father TerWoert was subject to the affliction of an infected gall-bladder, and while more than once his condition was afterwards very serious, yet through careful dieting he generally enjoyed good health until near the end of his life, when hardening of the arteries set in. The immediate cause of his death was pneumonia."

Monsignor TerWoert died in his eightieth year at his summer home in Belmar, New Jersey, on Tuesday, November 8, 1932, at seven o'clock in the morning. In point of service, he was then the oldest priest in the Diocese of Newark. His body was brought to the present parish rectory at 219 Third 

Street, Jersey City, the following evening, Wednesday, November 9. Rev. Robert G. Fitzpatrick was in charge of the funeral arrangements. On Thursday, November 10, a Mass attended by the children of the parish was celebrated, and in the late afternoon his body was placed in the church, where vigil was kept by a guard of honor. That evening, the office of the dead was recited in the presence of twelve hundred parishioners and priests. The visiting clergy were led by Fathers John 

Cassidy, S.J., of St. Peter's, Michael Hannon of St. Patrick's, Mark J. Duffy of St. Aloysius, William Looney of Assumption, Morristown, and Thomas J. Herron of St. Lucy's,

Jersey City. They read the Scripture lessons.63 

Within the Sanctuary were Fathers Robert G. Fitzpatrick, Philip J. Henebry, and Benedict W. Howe, then curates at 

St. Mary's, and Fathers John J. Murphy of Sacred Heart 

Church, Vailsburg, John J. Sweeney of St. Michael's, West 

End, Long Branch, Aloysius Merrity of St. Michael's, Michael 

Magnier of St. Patrick's, Charles Hunter of St. Joseph's, Harold 

Fitzpatrick of St. Bridget's, and John O'Brien of All Saint's, all churches in Jersey City.84

THE REVEREND THOMAS FLEMING BURKE, Ph.D., S.T.D.

CHAPTER X THE REVEREND THOMAS FLEMING BURKE, PH.D., S.T.D. 

THE Rev. Thomas Fleming Burke, Doctor of Philosophy and of Sacred Theology, the fourth pastor of St. Mary's Church in 

Jersey City, was born in the adjacent city of Hoboken on 

May 12, 1888.¹ His parents were John Burke and Elizabeth

Fleming Burke. He was baptized, received his First Holy 

Communion, and was confirmed at Our Lady of Grace Church, Hoboken, where the Rt. Rev. Charles J. Kelly, a former assistant of Dean Senez at St. Mary's, was pastor from January, 1894 until his death on November 11, 1911.

Dr. Burke attended the parochial school of Our Lady of 

Grace, from which he was graduated in 1902. In the fall of 

1903, he entered Seton Hall High School. On June 17, 1908, he was awarded the degree of Bachelor of Arts at Seton Hall College. His seminary studies were pursued at the North 

American College, Rome, Italy. On May 11, 1910, he received from St. Thomas Academy at Rome the degree of Doctor of 

Philosophy, and on June 24, 1912 he received from the Uni versity of Propaganda the degree of Doctor of Sacred Theology. 

Most Rev. Joseph Ceppetelli, Patriarch of Constantinople and Vicar-General of Cardinal Respighi, Vicar of Rome, ordained Dr. Burke on May 5, 1912 at the Church of St. Apollinare. Dr. Burke celebrated his first Mass at the Altar of St. 

Thomas Aquinas in the Church of Santa Maria sopra Minerva, a church of the Dominican Fathers at Rome, on May 6, 1912.

His acolyte was the Most Rev. Frank J. Monaghan, the present coadjutor Bishop of the See of Ogdensburg, New York.

On September 1, 1912, Dr. Burke celebrated at Our Lady of 

Grace Church, his first Solemn Mass in the United States. The preacher upon this occasion was Rt. Rev. Monsignor Eugene 

Carroll, who is still pastor of that parish. Dr. Burke's first appointment was to the Church of the Assumption at Morristown, where he was assistant to the late Very Rev. George F. 135

Brown. On the death of Dean Brown on March 25, 1914, Dr. 

Burke became assistant to Rt. Rev. Edward J. Ellard. A coassistant at this time was the late Rev. Thomas A. Mullins, who had been educated at St. Mary's Institute and whose parents were early parishioners at St. Mary's. On June 11, 1917, Dr. Burke was appointed assistant to the Rt. Rev. 

Edward A. Kelly, pastor of St. Patrick's in Jersey City.

In 1921, he was appointed Professor of Religion at St. Elizabeth's College, Convent Station, New Jersey, where he lectured until 1923. During 1922 and 1923, he taught philosophy to a small class of nuns at St. Vincent's Academy, Newark. 

In June, 1923, he was appointed Professor of Moral Theology and Canon Law in the Seminary of the Immaculate Conception at South Orange, and became a Professor of Philosophy and English at Seton Hall, under the administration of Most Rev. 

Thomas H. McLaughlin, then rector of the Seminary and president of the College. During 1924 and 1925, Doctor Burke also taught Religion to all the students of St. Vincent's Academy in Newark; and for a period of three months beginning 

April 30, 1925, he was administrator of Mount Carmel

Church, Orange. 

Dr. Burke's first appointment to a pastorate was at St. 

Virgil's Church, Morris Plains, on September 10, 1926. There he remodeled the church and the convent, in preparation for the fiftieth anniversary of the founding of the church. 

He was appointed Promoter Justitiae of the Diocese of Newark on May 18, 1926 by Bishop O'Connor, an office to which he was reappointed by Bishop Walsh on January 9, 1934. Bishop 

O'Connor also appointed him Examiner for Seminarians and Clergy in 1924, an office to which he was reappointed by 

Bishop Walsh on January 9, 1934.

On June 3, 1933, he was appointed missionary rector of 

St. Mary's by Bishop Walsh.

In 1935, under the direction of Dr. Burke, several changes were made in the Sanctuary at St. Mary's, and the interior of the church was decorated. A substantial sum for the cost

THE REVEREND THOMAS FLEMING BURKE, PH.D., S.T.D. 137 

of this decoration was a gift from the estate of the late Rt.

Rev. Bernard Henry TerWoert.2 

Interior of the New St. Mary's

A description of the exterior of the new church has already been given (pp. 120, 121). Within the church, the recent changes and decorations offer a dignified and a beautiful interior, in keeping with the original plans. 

Affixed to the main wall of the vestibule of the church are two marble memorial tablets, one to Dean Senez who built old St. Mary's, and the other to Monsignor TerWoert who built the present church.³ On entering the church proper, one is impressed at once by the dignity and majesty of the interior, for the eye is carried down a line of arches to the great half dome of the sanctuary under which stands the altar.¹ Mr. 

Reiley, the architect, very happily found the lower church so generous in proportion, that he was enabled to effect in the interior of the upper church a spaciousness generally lacking in the Catholic churches in large cities, where the need of obtaining the maximum number of seats is usually a very considerable problem. The resultant wide side aisles of St. 

Mary's Church thus make it well adapted on special occasions to liturgical processions. The seating capacity is 1060. 

Over the entrance doors, at the rear of the interior of the church, are painted the coats of arms, with the mottoes, of the five bishops of the Diocese of Newark: Bayley, Corrigan, Wigger, O'Connor, and Walsh. These insignia date (that is, note or fix) the period of the existence of the parish, and the time of construction of the present church. Thus, an investigating student would conclude that the building had been consecrated while Archbishop Walsh, the latest of the bishops, was alive, and that the parish itself was organized in the days of Archbishop Bayley, the first Bishop of Newark.

The barrel vault of the nave of the church is decorated in blue, and the supporting limestone pillars, which flank the side aisles, are gray. The whole interior is dominated by the sanctuary. The canopy of the altar is supported by pillars of differ-

ent marbles, and its dome is formed of glass mosaics which, in color and design, correspond with the three rose or Catharinewheel windows, placed over the organ loft and at the ends of the transept.

The main altar is a beautiful design in marble and glass mosaic. Its perfect proportions fit well into the apse of the church, and its beauty of line and harmony of color attract and hold the attention of the worshipping congregation. 

Under the half-dome canopy is a circular liturgical tabernacle of bronze, donated in memory of the late Joseph T. Sack.

Extending over the altar platform and steps and reaching into the Sanctuary, is a large Oriental rug, the gift of Mr. 

Thomas Boylan. To the right and left are chancel benches of wood, carved in the Romanesque style of the church; and a bronze door, in a frame of marble and glass mosaic, marks the place of the ambry. On the Sanctuary wall, behind and above the altar, is a semicircle of well executed life-size paintings, representing the Apostles (with Mathias, however, in place of Judas), who are grouped around a fountain of living waters. 

The texts concerning the living waters are: 

Zacharias XIV: 8And it shall come to pass in that day; that living waters shall go out from Jerusalem: half of them to the east sea, and half of them to the last sea: they shall be in summer and in winter.

Jeremias II: 13For my people have done two evils. They have forsaken me, the fountain of living water, and have digged to themselves cisterns, broken cisterns, that can hold no water. 

John IV: 13 and 14Jesus answered, and said to her: Whosoever drinketh of this water, shall thirst again, but he that shall drink of the water that I will give him, shall not thirst for ever. But the water that I will give him, shall become in him a fountain of water, springing up into life everlasting. 

Apocalypse VII: 17For the Lamb, which is in the midst of the throne, shall rule them, and shall lead them to the fountains of the

THE REVEREND THOMAS FLEMING BURKE, PH.D., S.T.D. 139 

waters of life, and God shall wipe away all tears from their eyes.

In the dome, above the semicircle of the pictured Apostles, is the main painting in the church. It is an allegorical representation of the historically and scripturally significant Tree of Jesse. In the center of this painting is the figure of the Blessed 

Virgin, patroness of St. Mary's Church. The inspiration for the artist is found in Isaias, who more than seven centuries before the birth of Christ thus spoke of the spiritual kingdom of the Saviour: 

Isaias XI: 1And there shall come forth a rod out of the root of Jesse, and a flower shall rise up out of his root. And the spirit of the Lord shall rest upon him, the spirit of wisdom and of understanding, the spirit of counsel and of fortitude, the spirit of knowledge and of godliness. And he shall be filled with the spirit of the fear of the Lord. He shall not judge according to the sight of the eyes, nor reprove according to the hearing of the ears. But he shall judge the poor with justice and reprove with equity for the meek of the earth; and he shall strike the earth with the rod of his mouth, and with the breath of his lips he shall slay the wicked.

The altar on the east side of the church is dedicated to the Blessed Virgin. It was the gift of Cornelius J. Cronan (18491927), at times a trustee of the church corporation. Mr. Cronan, a native of Cork, in youth came from Piermont, New 

York, to Jersey City. Here he achieved an enviable reputation as citizen, official, man of affairs, and trustee of important estates. The altar on the west side of the church is dedicated to Saint Joseph. This was the gift of the late James McCue (1870-1934) and family. Born and reared in the parish, Mr. 

McCue was for many years a useful member of the Board of Freeholders of Hudson County, and at his death, he was 

Superintendent of the County Alms House." 

5 Both the side altars are of similar marble, and are set in deep recesses. In architectural and decorative treatment, they correspond to the main altar: the light brown of the panels of

the latter finds its counterpart in the golden panels of the small altars; the geometrical ornamentation of all three is similar in design; and the decorative motive effect of the symbolic Tree of Jesse upon the wall of the main sanctuary is effectively repeated, though on a smaller scale, on the walls behind the side altars. These two smaller sanctuaries are identical, save that that of the Blessed Virgin is indicated by a symbolic rose of Sharon, and that of Saint Joseph by a lily. 

The donors' names are affixed, unobtrusively, to the bottom steps of the altars.

Upon the north wall of the baptistry-which is at the southwest corner of the interior of the church—is a fresco depicting in Byzantine style the baptism of Christ; above it is a representation of Noah's Ark, an ecclesiastical symbol of baptism.7 

At the bottom edge of the fresco, in Latin text, is the initial question and answer of the ritual of this Sacrament. In English it is: Q. What dost thou seek of the Church of God?

A. Faith. 

The white marble Gothic baptismal font is one of the few relics of the early days of the parish. It bears the inscription: 

ONE GOD-ONE FAITH-ONE BAPTISM 

1872 An edifice as architecturally sound and as spacious as St. 

Mary's, afforded to artisans great opportunity for other decorative effects. Those who furnished the stained glass windows for the church evidenced rare and skilled judgment, both in plan and execution. The windows in the clerestory, though they have colorful borders, are essentially simple in geometic design, and of quiet, almost plain, tone. They please, but, as was intended, do not attract. With the three rose or Catherinewheel windows (one over the entrance and two in the transept), the result is striking. Viewed from the main aisle under the transept, each produces a rich, though subdued, kaleidoscopic effect, to which additional interest is lent because the color schemes of all three are different. The emphatic beauty of the ornamental stained glass dwells, however, in the eighteen pictorial windows of the nave. There the designers,

THE REVEREND THOMAS FLEMING BURKE, PH.D., S.T.D. 141 

very appropriately for a church under the patronage of the Blessed Virgin, decided to illustrate the mysteries of the 

Rosary-joyful, sorrowful, and glorious-fifteen all told. A sixteenth window, in the nave near the sacristy, depicts St. 

Patrick, a single figure, well chosen of course for a congregation predominantly Irish in origin. The seventeenth window, which is on the west side of the altar dedicated to St. Joseph, portrays the boy Christ in Joseph's carpenter shop. In the eighteenth, which is on the east side of the altar dedicated to the Blessed Virgin, Christ is represented as He blesses the little children. Thus, though the subjects of the first fifteen windows were uppermost in the minds of the designers, the selection of the last three was quite in keeping with sentiment, tradition, and Holy Writ.8

In the decorative scheme of the whole interior, unity has been achieved in no small measure by an unbroken frieze of repeated, but varied, geometrical figures and designs. This frieze is an adaptation of a mosaic ornament in a twelfthcentury Italian church. By means of this frieze, the architect and the decorators have blended the rich tone of the solid oak of the sanctuary, of the pews, the confessionals, and of the organ loft; the soft brown of all the walls; the medium gray of the high columns of Indiana limestone; and the blue and gold coffering of the vaulted ceilings of nave and transept. 

The result is an all-pervading soft and pleasant harmony of quiet color. Upon a late summer afternoon, when the long line of western windows is lighted by the sun, the martyrs, saints, and prophets seem transfigured. All is cloistered peace. 

And at any time of day, and from any point of view, the beholder is impressed by a beauty of line and proportion.

Consecration of the New Church 

The solemn ceremony of the consecration of the present St. 

Mary's Church was performed on Saturday, May 2, 1936.10 The 

Most Rev. Thomas H. McLaughlin, then Auxiliary Bishop of

Newark, and now Bishop of the newly erected (1938) Diocese of Paterson, officiated. The present church is the third church

edifice for St. Mary's, the parish being the second oldest parish of the city, and the first parish in Hudson County to have been erected by a Bishop of Newark. This occasion of the consecration of the present church, marked the first consecration solemnized by Bishop McLaughlin after his own consecration as Auxiliary Bishop, July 25, 1935.11 (Bishop McLaughlin in 

1908 had been an assistant at the adjacent St. Michael's.) 

Following the consecration of St. Mary's, Bishop McLaughlin officiated at a Solemn Pontifical Mass.12 Rev. Dr. Thomas F. 

Burke, the pastor, served as assistant priest. 

On the night before the consecration of the present church, the relics of the altars were set apart. The ceremonies were begun on Saturday morning at seven o'clock with a procession which went three times around the outside of the church. In this rite, the relics were carried. This was repeated within the edifice, and was followed by the blessing of both the exterior and the interior of the building, the anointing of the altar with chrism and oil of catechumens, and the entombing of the relics. Of the relics, those of Saints Donatus and Fortunatus were placed and sealed into the main altar, while those of Saints Claudius and Pontianus were similarly entombed in the altar of St. Joseph.

The twelve crosses on the walls of the church, which are symbolic of the Apostles and are the insignia of consecration, were then anointed with chrism. This ceremony preceded the second consecration of the altar, and the blessing of the altar linens, before the singing of the Mass.18

Music for the services was rendered by children of St. Mary's 

School. The officers at the consecration were: the Rev. Thomas 

F. Duffy of Our Lady Queen of Peace Church, North Arlington, and the Rev. Francis Ballinger of Our Lady of Victories, 

Jersey City, who were the chanters; the Rev. Aloysius J. Merrity of St. Michael's, Jersey City, who was deacon, and the 

Rev. Joseph L. Donnelly, curate at St. Mary's, who was subdeacon. Visiting priests included the Reverends Robert G. 

Fitzpatrick of Corpus Christi Church, Hasbrouck Heights, and 

Philip J. Henebry of St. Virgilius, Morris Plains, who had

Doctor Burke's Silver Jubilee 

Doctor Burke observed his silver jubilee in the priesthood, on May 5, 1937. The occasion was duly marked at St. Mary's by the celebration of a Coram Pontifical Mass. Bishop Walsh presided at the ceremonies. The Most Rev. Frank J. Monaghan, Coadjutor Bishop of Ogdensburg, New York, who had served at the first Mass celebrated by Dr. Burke in Rome, delivered the sermon. With the bells of Grace (Van Vorst) 

Episcopal Church tolling, the procession of bishops, monsignors, priests, Knights of Columbus color guards, and acolytes, moved through the streets from the rectory to the church.15

Nineteen monsignors, nearly one hundred priests, Company 

F of the New Jersey Color Guard led by Captain Thomas

Gantly, and numerous acolytes, participated in the ceremonies. 

The Mass, of which Dr. Burke was celebrant, was sung by the 

Religious Teachers' Filippini of Villa Lucia, Morristown. The nuns of St. Mary's School, and the graduates attending the high schools attired in caps and gowns, occupied a prominent part of the church. The parishioners and friends of the celebrant were ushered to their seats by members of the Holy Name Society. Among the guests was Rev. J. A. Frampton, Rector of Grace (Van Vorst) Episcopal Church. 

In the evening, Dr. Burke was entertained by the children of the parochial school at a play. On the following evening there was a social celebration in the State Armory at Montgomery Street and Jordan Avenue. At this parish gathering, before a crowd estimated at six thousand persons, a bronze commemorative plaque was presented to Dr. Burke. The presentation was made by Honorable William F. Sullivan, a

trustee of the church and the present Register of Deeds of Hudson County. Mr. Sullivan was honorary chairman of the committee of arrangements; its executive chairman was John J. 

Quinn, and its associate chairmen were John J. Corcoran and 

John J. Donohue. Miss Marcella Eileen Monahan was its secretary.

At the several Confirmations through these years, the priests of the parish, in their instruction of the candidates, were assisted by the Sisters, notably Sister Theresa Symphorosa, Sister Benita, and Sister Louis Veronica. 

The largest class for Confirmation in the time of Bishop 

O'Connor was that of May 10, 1911, when 324 girls and 293 boys, a total of 617, were confirmed. The smallest class was that of 1918, when 65 boys and 95 girls, a total of 160, were confirmed. The average number confirmed by Bishop O'Connor was 400.

During the last year of the administration of Bishop O'Connor, it was necessary, because of his illness, that other bishops administer the Sacrament of Confirmation. On May 19, 1924, the Most Rev. Thomas J. Spreiter, Vicar Apostolic of Eshowe and titular Bishop of Thaenitanus, South Africa, confirmed 

151 males and 196 females, a total of 347, all of whom had individual sponsors; and on April 28, 1927, Most Rev. John 

J. Collins, S.J., Bishop of the Vicariate Apostolic of Jamaica, confirmed a class of 427, made up of 4 adults, 194 boys from the parish school, 13 boys from the public school, 197 girls from the parish school, and 19 girls from the public school. 

The first visit of the Most Rev. Thomas Joseph Walsh, fifth Bishop and first Archbishop of Newark, for the administration of the Sacrament of Confirmation, was on May 16, 1931. 

The class consisted of 123, of whom 17 were adults, 29 were boys from the parish school and 16 were boys from the public school; 27 were girls from the parish school, and 34 were girls from the public school. There were individual sponsors. 

The small size of the class was due to the fact that it was at this time that the required age for confirmation was eleven

The Archdiocese of Newark 

Meanwhile, great honors had been conferred by the Holy See on the State of New Jersey. The state was made an ecclesiastical province, separate and distinct from New York. 

The Metropolitan See is Newark, and the Suffragan Sees are 

Paterson, Trenton, and Camden.

S.T.D., J.C.D., was appointed the first Archbishop of Newark. 

Archbishop Walsh was installed in the Cathedral of the Sacred 

Heart, Newark, and received the sacred pallium from the

Apostolic Delegate, the Most Reverend Amleto Giovanni 

Cicognani, D.D., on April 27, 1938.
