Laughlin, S.T.D., LL.D., former Vicar General and Auxiliary

Bishop in the Diocese of Newark, was appointed the first Bishop of Paterson. He was installed in St. John's Cathedral in that city by Archbishop Walsh on April 28, 1938. 

On March 3, 1938, the Most Rev. William A. Griffin, D.D. was named Auxiliary Bishop of Newark and Titular Bishop of Sanavo. He was consecrated by Archbishop Walsh on 

May 1, 1938 in the Cathedral of the Sacred Heart, Newark, where five days previously the first Archbishop of Newark had received the insignia of the new and well-deserved honor from Archbishop Cicognani.

CHAPTER XII  

St. Mary's Conference of St. Vincent de Paul

ST. VINCENT DE PAUL was born April 24, 1576, in France, at Pouy near the boundaries of Bearn and Gascony, of an obscure but virtuous peasant family. In childhood, he was a shepherd. At the age of twelve he was entrusted to the Franciscans of Dax, a neighboring city. At sixteen, he became a tutor, and thus relieved his parents of the expense of his clerical education. He was ordained on September 23, 1600. 

After two years in captivity at Tunis, he escaped to Europe, visited Rome, and was later the bearer of a confidential message from the Pope to Henry IV of France. He lived in Paris for nearly twenty years, occupied either as a parish priest or a tutor. At the age of forty-nine, he founded the Priests of the Mission, a congregation bound by simple vows, which gave missions in rural districts. He also established Preparatory and 

Major Seminaries, and the Sisters of Charity for the service of the poor and sick. This latter congregation dates its foundation from March 25, 1634. It was from the activities of the 

Sisters of Charity that he received his purest glory and his greatest popularity. 

On October 23, 1620, he established an association of men for the relief of the able-bodied poor. Under his guidance, this men's association rendered valuable services in seeking out and aiding the bashful poor. 

He died on September 27, 1660, was beatified August 13, 1729 by Pope Benedict XIII, and solemnly canonized June 16, 1937 by Pope Clement XII. On June 22, 1883, Pope Leo XIII 

declared him the special patron with God of all charitable associations emanating from him in any manner whatsoever and established on French territory; and on May 12, 1885, special patron with God of all charitable associations existing 149

throughout the Catholic world and emanating from him in any manner whatsoever. St Vincent de Paul taught that active charity was not something merely superficial or accidental in the practical life of a Christian, but an integral organic part of Christian teaching, and the necessary expression of a lively faith which does not permit us to see others in spiritual or corporal need without manifesting our love towards them.

Charity, he held, must pass into action, and into actual and true expression. He transmitted to future generations the organization of charity which the Catholic Church may justly claim as one of her greatest glories in modern times. 

There is scarcely a single form of charitable activity in America today, that was not successfully undertaken by him in France three centuries ago. He reformed the treatment of prisoners, built free schools for working-class children, founded homes for deserted infants, arranged vocational training for boys and girls, established homes for the aged. He anticipated the demands of the most advanced philanthropists by providing that husband and wife should not be separated, as in most institutions, but that each couple should spend their remaining days together. He made such adequate provision for the regular relief of the destitute, that there was left no excuse for street begging. He recruited and trained what have been called his armies of charity, lay men and lay women as well as the consecrated Sisters of Charity, to visit and relieve the poor in their own homes; and he organized a vast work for relieving provinces devastated by war. 

Probably his most noted follower was Frederic Ozanam, the leading founder of the Society of St. Vincent de Paul.¹

Ozanam, of French descent, was born April 23, 1813, in Milan, then a French city. Upon his admission to the bar, in Paris, he became a member of the household of the great scientist, Ampere, who was his daily example and religious model. 

Ozanam and six or seven other Catholic young men may all be described as founders of the Society, but Ozanam was its soul. 

All were influenced solely by the desire of finding for themselves mutual support, strength, and consolation in the practice

of doing good, and in devoting themselves to some little works for the sake of the love of Jesus Christ. No member used the

Society for the advancement of any personal material interest. of The first meeting of the Conference of St. Vincent de Paul was held in Paris in May, 1833. It was by means of the Sisters of Charity of St. Vincent de Paul, that the Conference reached the poor. The Sisters gave invaluable advice, drew up lists poor families to be visited, and furnished bread and meat tickets until the Conference issued its own. Each Brother of the Conference looked after one family. It gave dual aid, moral and material, and thus established the scope of the future work of the Society.

At first, the Conference denied admission to others, but Ozanam soon definitely declared himself in favor of securing more members. Among the new recruits was Leon Le Provost, the founder of the Congregation of the Brothers of St. Vincent de Paul. Before the death of Ozanam on September 8, 1853, the little association of friends had become the nucleus of an immense brotherhood spread over Europe. In Paris they numbered two thousand brothers, who visited five thousand families—that is to say, twenty thousand persons or one fourth of the poor of that city. In France, there were five hundred Conferences, and there were others in England, Spain, Belgium, America, and in Jerusalem.

In the United States the first Conference of the Society of St. Vincent de Paul was established at St. Louis on November 

14, 1845, by twelve members of the Cathedral parish, under the approval of Bishop Peter Richard Kenrick. He had been moved by the enthusiasm of Father John Timon. The latter was born of Irish parents at Conevago, Pennsylvania, on February 12, 1797, ordained a priest in 1825, and after missionary work in the Republic of Texas, which had seceded from Mexico, had been transferred to the Diocese of Buffalo. In 1843 or early in 1844, he made a visit to the headquarters of the Lazarists' Community in Paris. He there became interested in the Society of St. Vincent de Paul, and visited its headquarters

frequently to make a study of its activities. Before returning to the United States, he went to Dublin, Ireland, where he obtained the rules and other necessary literature, printed in English. Upon his return to Buffalo, he thus had whatever might prove helpful to him in the object he had in view, the establishment in America of a Conference. Then, having occasion to visit his former headquarters in St. Louis, he called upon Bishop Kenrick, and explained to him enthusiastically the desirability of having the Society organized in the United States. The Bishop assigned the formation of the first Conference to Rev. Ambrose J. Heim. Its members soon numbered 

620, and they followed exactly the rules of the Society of Dublin. Father Timon was made Bishop of Buffalo on October 17, 1847, and immediately established Conferences in 

Buffalo and Lockport. He died April 16, 1867.2

By 1860, there were in the United States several Conferences, groups of men numbering from 80 to 130. New York had six 

Conferences, and Philadelphia eight. The conference in New

York had been instituted in 1857, and that in Brooklyn the same year. The first General Assembly of the Society at large was held in 1864 in New York City, after which the first printed report of the Superior Council of New York was published. In 1863, the New York Conference had organized a special work known as the House of Protection for the care of dependent and delinquent boys, located on Eighty-sixth Street of that city. Later the Society surrendered its activities to an organization which became the New York Catholic Protectory. 

In 1870, it organized another special work known as the St. 

Vincent de Paul Lodging House for Boys. It was located on 

Warren Street, and later, under the direction of Father Drumgoole, became the Mission of the Immaculate Virgin.³ 

A General Convention of the Society was held in New York City in September, 1895, and thereafter for twenty-one years, the St. Vincent de Paul Quarterly was published in the cause of charity. The files of the magazine are a record of Catholic charities in the United States. Its successor was the Catholic 

Charities Review, which was called into existence by the

approval of the National Conference of Catholic Charities, and by the national representatives of the Society of St. Vincent de 

Paul. Notable workers in New Jersey were Rev. Francis A. E. 

Foy of Nutley, and Mr. John Guilfoyle of St. Patrick's parish, Jersey City.¹

Of the history of the Society of St. Vincent de Paul in St. 

Mary's parish, there are apparently (and not inappropriately) no written records. So unostentatiously were its works performed that it is practically impossible to name its members. 

For, first of all, the work is one of faith and of preservation and strengthening of the faith; next, it is a work of apostleship by means of charity and example rather than by discourse; in origin and in ideal, it is a work of mutual personal satisfaction by pious exercises and practices; the members humbly conceal their names, do good in secret and impersonally; alms are begged and distributed; and among the members there is kept a prudent silence on the subjects which divide men.5 

The Conference of the Society of St. Vincent de Paul in St. 

Mary's Church in Jersey City, which had its origin in the very early days of the parish, was reorganized on January 29, 1926. 

Among its Presidents have been Terence McSweeney, Owen 

Sherry, and Thomas Copeland." Sodality of the Children of Mary The Sodality of the Childen of Mary Immaculate owes its origin to a manifestation of Sister Katherine Labouré, a novice in the mother-house of the Sisters of Charity in Paris in 1830. 

Associations, formed for experiment, were established in various houses of the Order. In June, 1847, the Superior General of the Missions was empowered to erect in each house of the Sisters of Charity a pious confraternity, dedicated to Mary Immaculate. Members were to be young girls attending various schools and workshops.8 

At an audience of His Holiness, Pius IX, on March 10, 1867, at the instance of H. Capitali, Secretary of the Sacred 

Congregation of the Propagation of the Faith, the Most Rev-

erend John McCloskey, Archbishop of New York, was authorized to establish the Sodality of the Blessed Virgin Mary in the girls' schools under the charge of the Sisters of Charity in his diocese for the benefit of the children attending these schools. In the late summer of 1867, the Sisters of Charity at 

Mount St. Vincent in New York began this work. By association, the members of this Sodality honor the Virgin, and share good works, prayers, and communions, and all acts of virtue practiced by the Society. They also profit by the good example which the members give one another, and by the advice and particular instruction which are given to them. 

The obligations of the Sodality are a matter of free acceptance by its members, and a patent of aggregation from the Sisters of Charity of St. Vincent de Paul and the approval of the 

Bishop of the Diocese are necessary. The chief Feast Day of the Society is that of the Immaculate Conception, December 8 of each year. The Mother Superior of the local convent is generally the Directress of the Sodality. Girls are asked to retain their membership in the Sodality after graduation, and as far as possible to assist at the monthly assembly. The activities of the Sodality are marked by monthly and annual retreats, and regular frequentation of the Sacraments." 

A former parishioner mentions among past workers in the 

Sodality: Margaret Cook, Bridget McGee, Mary Nolan, Marcella Smith, Elizabeth Cue, Mary McEntee, and Mary McQuade.

She observes that the members of the Children of Mary, almost invariably former pupils at the Institute, were at first members in the Infants' Society, next in the Angels' Society, and then, as they went into the higher classes, they became members of the Children of Mary. She recalls meetings, month after month (1885-1905), at which the attendance was steadily about 160, almost the full membership; and she treasures memories of corporate communions, preceded by processions of the young ladies, wearing white veils, from the Institute to the Church. There are instances of several members from one family: the Bayley family furnished seven members; the Cronley family, five; the Barry, Gough, and Henzey fami-

lies, each four; and each of the following families had three members in the Sodality: Brady, Cooney, Crosby, Dorgan, Fitzgerald, Kelly, Kenny, Lewis, McMahon, McQuade, Monaghan, Mullins (sisters of Rev. Thomas A. Mullins), Mullins (Anna, a noted business woman is of this group), O'Connor, Pynkham, Ryan, and Sullivan. Twenty-seven families had two members each.

The present president of the Sodality at St. Mary's is Helen 

Hagan. Among those who preceded her in office are: Clare 

Cannon, Kathryn Tyrer (now Mrs. Charles Ziegler), Mary 

Keane (now Mrs. Thomas McGrath), Agnes Farmer, Elizabeth McAghon, Helen Mackin, and Mary Perry." 

Confraternity of the Holy Rosary 10 The Confraternity of the Holy Rosary is more than four centuries old. Through the preaching of Alan DeRupe, associations were erected before 1475. Of them, that established in 

Cologne in Germany by Father James Sprenger is especially famous. These associations are developed under the supervision of the Dominican Fathers, and the direction of their organization is entirely in the hands of these Fathers. The object of the Society is to effect a union of prayer and devotional exercises. In numbers, it is the largest organization of its kind in the Catholic Church. On October 2, 1898, new interest was given to the formation of the Confraternity of the Holy Rosary by Pope Leo XIII, who by his important "Apostolic Constitution of the Rosary Confraternity" practically gave a new charter to Rosarians. It is through the Confraternity of the 

Holy Rosary, that there has been effected a uniform system in the recitation of the Rosary.¹ 11 Within the Rosary Society there is a grouping of perpetual Rosarians designed to assure the continuous recitation of the Rosary by day and night. These associates at stated times perform their allotted duty. This group is a development of the 

Confraternity of the Holy Rosary, and dates from the seventeenth century. It is not represented at St. Mary's. But another group, independent of the Confraternity itself, and

known as the "Living Rosary," was founded in 1826 in the city of Lyons, France, by Marie Pauline Jaricot. 

At St. Mary's, as in other parishes, the Sodality of the Living 

Rosary consists of a number of circles of fifteen members, each of whom agrees to recite a single decade (of beads) every day, and thus daily complete among themselves the recitation of the whole Rosary. This Society of the Living Rosary re ceived the approbation of Pope Gregory XVI (1831-1846) on January 27, 1832. It is canonically instituted. When the Dominicans were expelled from France, Gregory XVI committed the supreme direction of this society to secular priests who were called Directors General. Since 1878, the Dominican Order has had the supreme direction and care of all the Sodalities of the Living Rosary. These sodalities, however, are not confraternities and are not obliged to keep a general register, nor are any public exercises of devotion prescribed. Their great simple object is the devout recitation of the beads.12 

At St. Mary's, the present president is Margaret Henzey;

Jane McAghon preceded her in this office; and Nellie McCabe, noted for many years as a capable church debt collector, once occupied the position.13 

Society of the Holy Name of Jesus 

The beginning of the Holy Name Society dates from the recommendations of a general council convoked at Lyons, France, on September 20, 1274, by virtue of which Pope Gregory X addressed a letter to the Master General of the Order of Preachers, then a comparatively new institution. The Order was directed to teach the people to revere in a particular manner the name of Jesus. Among those who, through the ages, became prominent in the movement were St. Dominic, Ambrose of Siena, Henry Suso, a German friar, St. Bernardine of Siena, a Franciscan, and St. John of Capistran. Definite organization was given to Holy Name Societies in the sixteenth century by Didacus of Victoria, a Spanish Dominican, who wrote the rules and constitution. Pope Pius IV (1559-1565) raised the 

Society to the dignity of a confraternity, and designated the

Dominicans as moderators of the movement. As early as 1580, it existed in Peru. There is a well-established tradition (but no authentic record) that confraternities were founded in New Orleans and San Francisco in the colonizing periods of Spain and France. A little more than a century ago, a Holy Name 

Society was set up in Kentucky by the Rev. Charles Nerinckxy, a stalwart Belgian missionary, who labored faithfully to check profanity among the hardy pioneers then pushing over the Alleghany Mountains. His sphere of activity, however, was only local; and while his society was the first among the English-speaking Catholics of America, it is unlikely that it was the source of the present Holy Name movement in the United States. Strangely enough, however, the first Dominicans in the United States were missionaries in Kentucky. In 1868, they began their first parochial work in New York City, and while organizing there the parish of St. Vincent Ferrer, one of their first steps was to band the men into a Holy Name Society. In 

1871, a charter was obtained from Rome to make this a canonical society. During the next ten years, the movement, through the influence of Dominican missioners, spread slowly but effectively. By 1882, there were five Holy Name Societies in the City of New York. A diocesan union, which was then formed, gave great impetus to the movement. In 1911, the first 

National Congress of the Holy Name Society was held in Baltimore.14 

St. Mary's Holy Name Society, the oldest in Hudson County, was formed on October 12, 1886, during the pastorate of 

Father Senez.15 For years, it was probably the only canonical

Holy Name Society in the City; for, until 1896, an ancient decree forbade the existence of the Society in more than one church of a single city.16 In that year, this restrictive legislation was modified, and the erection of new branches of the Holy Name Society was placed under the jurisdiction of the American hierarchy." 17 Father Charles J. Kelly was first Chaplain of St. Mary's Holy 

Name Society. Its first President was Hugh Coyle, a native of County Monaghan, Ireland, who was an outstanding figure

in Catholic circles in Jersey City.18 In less than a year, the membership of the Society grew to more than five hundred. Of these, one hundred were received as members on June 12, 1887 by Bishop Wigger.19 On August 30, 1888, under the auspices of the Society, there was a parish excursion.20 On the occasion of its fifth anniversary on October 12, 1891, more than five hundred members received Holy Communion, and Father Kelly preached an appropriate sermon.21 During these five years, the Society held its monthly meetings in the Catholic Institute.22 

The formation of the Catholic Club caused a decline in the Holy Name Society at St. Mary's; and in 1901, it had very few active members.23 Then, through the particular efforts of 

Father Michael McGuinness, a young assistant of Father Ter- Woert, it took on a new life and soon became a flourishing body. Within a year its membership increased from twelve members to eight hundred members.24

In 1904, the Jersey City Holy Name Society came into existence, with Father McGuinness, now pastor of St. Mary's, Paterson, as its chairman. Important figures in its development were the following priests, then all young curates in 

Jersey City: Fathers Patrick A. Maher (deceased), a curate at St. Mary's; George Bennett, Secretary, of St. Joseph's (deceased); Mark J. Duffy of St. Michael's, now pastor of St. 

Aloysius, Jersey City; John J. Murphy of St. Bridget's, now pastor of the Sacred Heart Church at Vailsburg, Newark; 

Henry J. Watterson of St. Lucy's, now pastor of Holy Trinity, Westfield; John J. Preston of St. John's, late pastor of St. 

Cecelia's, Kearny; and Cornelius J. Kane, also of St. John's, now pastor of St. Mary's, Rahway.25 

On November 27, 1904, the first Sunday in Advent, the

Jersey City Society held its first Vesper service at St. Mary's. 

The preacher was the Rev. Clement M. Thuente, O.P. The following May, a second Vesper service was held at St. 

Michael's in Jersey City. There Bishop O'Connor preached the sermon.28 Two thousand attended. 

In May, 1905, the Diocesan Holy Name Union was organized. Father McGuinness had meanwhile been transferred from

St. Mary's to St. Paul of the Cross in Jersey City. When 

Bishop O'Connor did not favor a Diocesan rally at Seton Hall, Father McGuinness urged that the first rally of the diocese be held in Jersey City on the last Sunday of September;27 and it was there, on that day, that Societies from many parishes in 

Newark, Jersey City, and Hoboken marched to Hamilton Park.

Benediction was pronounced from an altar erected upon the steps of St. Francis Hospital near by. After 1905, the Newark societies held their rallies in Essex County. In 1906, the local rally centered at the Church of Our Lady of Grace in Hoboken, Bishop McFaul of Trenton preached. In 1908, the rally for the first time was held at West Side Park, which had been opened to the public in 1907.28 Since 1908, the closing exercises of the annual rally have been held in the same recreation center, which is now known as Lincoln Park. 

The development of the Diocesan Holy Name Union was marked, of course, by the growth of the parish societies. The membership of St. Mary's Holy Name Society increased greatly. 

By 1909, it numbered eleven hundred members. During Father 

TerWoert's pastorate, it was carefully nurtured. It now (1938) has an actual membership of 750. This is suffering a gradual decrease, of course, because the population of the parish has become smaller. There still remains, however, the fervor of more than half a century ago, when St. Mary's was the only 

Holy Name Society in Hudson County. And though none of its present members is of those who were its very first adherents, there are still alive elders of the parish who remember the untiring zeal of Fathers Kelly, Mooney, Brady, McGuinness, Maher, O'Malley, James J. Smith, Thomas J. Martin, and

Fitzpatrick. The spirit which animated these young priests in their duties as directors, has led the men of St. Mary's parish to make manifest a uniform devotion to the Holy Name of 

Jesus Christ, true God and true man.

APPENDIX I. CONVEYANCES TO ST. MARY'S CHURCH 

The conveyances to St. Mary's Church are numerous. On October 7, 1864, Rt. Rev. James R. Bayley, styled Doctor of Divinity and

Bishop of Newark, signed and acknowledged five deeds in Newark before Theodore Runyon, then a Master in Chancery, and later 

Chancellor of New Jersey. By these deeds, recorded in Hudson

County in Book 119 of Deeds, on February 22, 1865, Bishop Bayley conveyed their respective church properties to St.

Mary's in Jersey 

City, Our Lady of Grace in Hoboken, St. Mary's Star of the Sea in Bayonne, St. Paul's in Greenville, and St. Joseph's in Hudson (now Jersey) City. Previously, the legal titles of these properties had been vested in him. An examination of the deed which he executed in favor of St. Mary's Church aids to a correct understanding of the early history of the parish. Eight parcels of property are conveyed in the order below indicated. To further a correct understanding of the later history, the details concerning subsequent conveyances are stated at proper places. 

First tract: (Bishop Bayley to St. Mary's Church). This consists of four lots in Pavonia, in the Fourth Ward of Jersey City, known as lots 17, 18, 19, and 20, in Block 150, as indicated by the Map of 

Harsimus, made by Joseph F. Mangin, in 1804. These lots form a square of 100 feet at the corner of Erie and Second Streets. Bishop 

Bayley had acquired them from Edward Thebaud and wife, of 

Madison, N. J., by deed dated February 2, 1860, for the sum of $8500. Earlier, the Thebauds had acquired lots 17 and 18 by foreclosing a mortgage. The indications are that for these two lots, 17 and 18, which had a frontage of 50 feet on Erie Street and of 100 feet on Second Street, Thebaud paid the sum of $3940 at a sheriff's sale on October 22, 1853. Thebaud's deed to this parcel is recorded in Book 35, page 154. The other (and inside) lots, 19 and 20, of this plot of 100 feet square, had been purchased by Thebaud at a partition sale of the lands of Israel Champion on 

September 29, 1855, for $2905, as indicated by Thebaud's deed from James W. Palmer, William Blair, and Peter Bentley, Commissioners in Partition, recorded in Book 52 of Deeds, page 86. 160

APPENDIX 

161 Second tract: (Bishop Bayley to St. Mary's Church). This tract is also in Block 150 on Mangin's Map of Harsimus. It embraces lot 21, which is 25 feet wide and 100 feet deep, lying immediately to the north of the First Tract as described, and it also embraces parts of lots 22, 23 and 24. It covers an additional frontage of 42 feet on Erie Street to the north of the First Tract, and a frontage of 60 feet on Third Street. It is shaped like the letter "L." This 

Second Tract was purchased by Bishop Bayley from Andrew An derson of Orange for $5750, by deed dated April 18, 1860. Anderson had owned this tract, and other adjoining lands, for almost thirty years. This conveyance of Anderson to Bishop Bayley, and that by Thebaud for the First Tract, gave to the congregation of St. 

Mary's a frontage of 100 feet on Second Street, 142 feet on Erie

Street, and 60 feet on Third Street. The easterly line of both these tracts made a length of 200 feet running from a point on the north side of Second Street distant 100 feet from Erie Street to a point on the south side of Third Street, also distant 100 feet from Erie Street.

A plot at the corner of Erie and Third Streets, which had a frontage of 58 feet on Erie Street and a frontage of 40 feet on

Third Street, was not acquired by the congregation until 1901, when William Howeth, who had carried on a grocery and provision business there for many years, conveyed this corner plot to the church. This was immediately after the tornado of that year. 

The conveyance made by Mr. Howeth was dated October 1, 1901, and recorded in Book 787 of Deeds at page 449; the stated consideration was $12,000. Mr. Howeth had owned this property since 

April 30, 1868. His deed is in Book 161, page 691. 

Third and fourth tracts: (Bishop Bayley to St. Mary's Church).

These are two lots on the south side of Third Street 50 feet east of Monmouth Street. They form a plot 50 feet wide on First Street, with a depth of 125 feet. There seems to be no history of their use for parochial purposes.

Fifth tract: (Bishop Bayley to St. Mary's Church). This tract consists of three lots with a frontage of 75 feet on Jersey Avenue and a depth of 100 feet on Second Street, and is part of the site of St. Mary's Academy and the Orphan Asylum. These three lots were bought by Bishop Bayley in February, 1863, from Messrs.

Schugard and Wrighter and others, for the sum of $7500, as appears by deed recorded in Book 98, at page 453. A lot 25 feet by 100 feet on Jersey Avenue, immediately to the south of the lots just described, was conveyed by Patrick and Ellen Flood to the Trustees of St. Mary's Church by deed dated April 15, 1868, and recorded July 7, 1868 in Book 173 of Deeds, page 486.

The consideration paid Flood was apparently $6000, of which $3000 was in cash, and the balance of $3000 was secured by a mortgage held by the Fifth Ward Savings Bank, of Jersey City, which the Trustees agreed to pay. The congregation thus owned in 1868 a plot of land 100 feet square, at Second Street and Jersey Avenue, on which, of course, there were buildings. 

By deed dated December 28, 1882, but not recorded until June 16, 1886 in Book 442 of Deeds, page 110, the church corporation conveyed this plot to the Sisters of Charity of St. Vincent de Paul, a New York corporation. The consideration expressed is one dollar, and the conveyance was made subject to these conditions: (1) the Sisters were bound to take care of the orphan girls of the parish as far as the capacity of the buildings on the premises would permit; and to continue in the buildings an academy, and a workshop, the profits of which would be used to defray the expenses of the orphan asylum; (2) if the Sisters withdrew from the parish they would reconvey the premises. This deed (Book 

442, page 110) contains an agreement that the church corporation will pay off a mortgage upon the property due in July, 1884, and that "the rector will, by means of collections and otherwise, assist the Sisters who take charge of the free schools, orphan asylum, and academy, in the same manner as is now done." 

On October 6, 1887, by deed recorded in Book 446, page 402,

William and Margaret Ryan conveyed to the Mount Sisters a lot 25 by 100 feet. This adjoins the corner lot, to the west, on Second 

Street. The consideration stated is $4850, and the conveyance is made subject to the lien of a mortgage for $2500. 

By deed dated August 14, 1906, recorded December 24, 1906, in Book 971, page 294, the Mount Sisters reconveyed to the church corporation the corner plot 100 feet square, which had been conveyed to the Mount Sisters by the church corporation by the deed above mentioned, recorded in Book 442 at page 110. The deed executed by the Sisters in 1906 recites the conditions upon which the Mount Sisters had accepted the property in 1882, and it is

APPENDIX 

163 FR stated that the re-conveyance is made because the Mount Sisters are withdrawing from the parish. The expressed consideration for this deed of 1906, made by the Mount Sisters to the church corporation, is one dollar. 

By deed dated January 4, 1907, recorded February 18, 1907, in

Book 962 of Deeds at page 504, the Mount Sisters also conveyed to the church corporation the property which they had acquired from William and Margaret Ryan on October 6, 1887. The deed of 1907, made by the Mount Sisters to the church corporation, recites a consideration of one dollar, and is made "in order to enable the said St. Mary's Church of Jersey City to carry on said charitable, benevolent, and educational work in Jersey City." 

This, it is stated in the deed, was the purpose for which the Mount Sisters had acquired the Ryan property. 

There is also recorded, under date of February 18, 1907, in Book 

962 of Deeds, page 495, "A consent to convey." This instrument recites a meeting of the Sisters at Mount St. Vincent, New York, on January 2, 1907, and the adoption of a resolution by them to the effect that, as they had taken possession of the Ryan property in order to use the same in carrying out their charitable, benevolent, and educational work in Jersey City, the conveyance to be made by the Sisters to St. Mary's Church is for the same purposes. The present title to the corner of Jersey Avenue and Second Street, a tract with a frontage of 100 feet on Jersey Avenue, thus, as indicated, is the property of St. Mary's Church. 

Sixth tract: (Bishop Bayley to St. Mary's Church). This tract embraced ten lots; four of them formed the plot on which Father 

John Kelly built St. Mary's Chapel. All ten lots were subsequently conveyed to St. Michael's Church by appropriate deeds. 

Seventh tract: (Bishop Bayley to St. Mary's Church). This was a lot on Third Street, 25 feet wide and 100 feet deep. It was conveyed to Bishop Bayley about February 1, 1864, by Andrew

Anderson, for $1750, as appears by deed recorded in Book 107, at page 229. The plot was part of the site of the old Institute, and is now part of the site of the present Parish School. It adjoins the tract next described. 

Eighth tract: (Bishop Bayley to St. Mary's Church). This is a plot 50 feet wide on Third Street, with a depth of 100 feet. It

begins 100 feet east of Erie Street, and lies to the west of the Seventh Tract as described. This Eighth Tract was bought by 

Bishop Bayley from Robert C. Bacot about February 1, 1864 for $3500, by deed recorded in Book 107, page 226. With the preceding (Seventh) tract purchased from Anderson, and the other preceding (Second) tract, also purchased from Anderson, it gave a total frontage on Third Street of 135 feet, beginning 100 feet from the corner of Erie Street, and running easterly. The easterly line of these combined plots corresponds to the easterly line of St. Mary's Institute in 1868. No additional purchases of adjoining property on Third Street were made between 1864 and 1901. Then in 1901, as already set forth, there was acquired the Howeth property at the corner of Third and Erie Streets, which gave an unbroken frontage on Third Street of 175 feet. Later, when it became necessary for school purposes that additional frontage be secured further east on Third Street, Monsignor TerWoert acquired an additional frontage of about 82 feet of land. This was secured by three conveyances; in each the consideration was expressed as one dollar. The three deeds were from: (1) Horace Billington and wife, dated January 24, 1914, recorded in Book 1171 of Deeds, page 33, conveying a lot 25 by 100 feet, situated 175 feet west of Grove Street, and immediately adjoining the old Institute property on the east; (2) Mary A. Sprague, dated January 26, 1914, recorded in Book 1171 of Deeds at page 531, conveying a similar lot adjoining the Billington lot on the east; and (3) John Warren (then unmarried), dated May 5, 1914, recorded in Book 1183 of 

Deeds, at page 153, conveying a plot 31.9 feet deep by 100 feet, situated 143.3 feet west of Grove Street, and adjoining the Sprague property just described. In combination, all told, the conveyances to St. Mary's Church now give a present frontage on Third Street of approximately 257 feet.

It has been noted that by virtue of the description of the First Tract described in the deed from Bishop Bayley to the Church, the Church secured legal title to a plot at the corner of Second and Erie Streets, which was 100 feet square. In the spring of 1868, the Trustees of the Church purchased additional property facing Second Street, and situated to the east of the plot conveyed by Bishop Bayley. This property facing Second Street was 25 feet wide and 100 feet deep, and was known as lot 16, in Block 150, on

APPENDIX 

165 Mangin's Map of 1804. It was purchased by the Trustees of the church from Ellen and Thomas O'Callaghan, by deed dated April 

28, 1868, and recorded May 11, 1868 in Book 170 of Deeds, at page 5. The consideration was $4250, and additionally it was conveyed subject to a mortgage of $2500 held by the Fifth Ward Savings Bank of Jersey City, which the Trustees agreed to pay. 

This property, it is thought, was a dwelling house, which may have been, until 1900, the abode of the Christian Brothers. From 1868 to 1901, the frontage of the church property on Second Street was thus 125 feet.

Then, after the tornado, Monsignor TerWoert acquired another lot to the east of the property bought from the O'Callaghans. 

This other lot is described in the conveyance made by Catherine 

Brown, widow, dated October 2, 1901, recorded October 3, 1901 in Book 787 of Deeds, page 447. The stated consideration is $6500, and the land embraced is lot 15, in Block 150, on Mangin's Map of Harsimus, and begins 125 feet east of Erie Street. It had been in the Brown family since 1860. Their deed is recorded in Book 

851 at page 70. This property is now part of the site of the Convent. The effect of this deed, with earlier conveyances, was to give the church corporation a frontage on Second Street of 150 feet, now occupied by the church and the convent. 

It should be noted here that there appears of record a deed of release as to Lots 19 and 20 in Block 150 on Mangin's Map of

Harsimus, executed in favor of Bishop James R. Bayley by 

Josephine Hutton, widow of Robert Hutton, dated April 1, 1863, recorded April 2, 1863 in Book 98 at page 362, for a consideration of one dollar. Josephine Hutton was a sister of the mother of the 

Doctors Kopetschny, an old family of the parish, who resided for generations at Fourth Street and Jersey Avenue. This release affects, as indicated, the property conveyed by the Champion Commissioners to Thebaud. To ascertain its exact significance, an extended examination of ancient records would be necessary. There are indications, however, that the release was a gift to St. Mary's congregation.

The acquisition of the property for the old Catholic Club House on Jersey Ave. is indicated by a deed from the heirs of John 

Boyce dated September 10, 1891, recorded September 16, 1891 in

Book 537, page 363; the consideration expressed is $16,000. The

conveyance is made to St. Mary's Catholic Church, Jersey City, and embraced a plot 75 feet by 100 feet on the west side of Jersey 

Avenue, beginning 25 feet south of Third Street.

The church corporation also owns the premises 240 Second Street, Jersey City. This house and lot were acquired in 1902, when it was necessary for the Christian Brothers to move from 

219 Third Street, which had been the rectory of Father Senez from about 1870 until his death in 1900, and which had been renovated and moved about twenty feet to the east by Father TerWoert. 

The plans for the basement of the present church made it necessary that this renovated building be razed, and it was thus necessary to secure another abode for the Brothers. The dwelling at 

240 Second Street was then owned by James Sullivan, a widower, long a prosperous shoe merchant on Newark Avenue. His deed to the church was dated April 3, 1902, and was recorded in Book 806 of the Deeds at page 131. The expressed consideration was $5500, and the property was sold subject to the rights of the tenant, a police officer named Walter Cooper, the head of a family long parishioners of St. Mary's. The house had previously been owned by Joseph M. Lane, Sr., grandfather of Merritt Lane, later a Vice- 

Chancellor of New Jersey.

II. ASSISTANT PRIESTS AT ST. MARY'S 

1855-1938 On the registers of St. Mary's are the names of several assistants of whom it is regrettably difficult to obtain adequately reliable information, except by an apparently disproportionate effort of time and labor. Fathers Patrick O'Brien, George McMahon, and James

Daly officiated at St. Mary's Chapel, at Erie and Tenth Streets, in 

1856 and 1857. Their history, strictly, is part of that of St. Peter's 

Church, Jersey City, of which the chapel was a mission. There are indications that Father James Daly labored (1858-1869) at 

Gloucester, Woodbury, and Snow Hill, New Jersey. Fathers

James and Perrache, who officiated at St. Mary's in 1859, the year 

Dean Senez was made pastor, were probably, though not surely, visiting clergymen. There is a lack of information about Rev. 

Michael Angelo Raybaudi, an apparently efficient assistant who served at St. John's (now Sacred Heart) Church, Trenton, in

APPENDIX 

167 1 

1859-1860, and of another, Rev. James Heffernan (1878). Several assistants, notably Rev. Henry J. Connery and Rev. W. J. Fennessy, in the early 1900's came from New England dioceses, to which they returned.

From 1856 to 1938, seventy-seven priests labored at St. Mary's. 

Their names and approximate dates of service are given in the following pages in fair chronological order. Nearly all of them are sketched in alphabetical order (pp. seq.). For the most part, those living at the present time (1938) have supplied information concerning their own careers. For the deceased priests, reliance has been placed upon the writings of Archbishop Corrigan, Dean Flynn and Father Leahy; upon obituaries published in The Catholic News; upon the newspapers of Hoboken, Jersey City, Newark, Paterson, and other centers; and upon information furnished by relatives.
