Rev. Leo J. Martin-December 10, 1933-November 1, 1935 Rev. John A. Costello-December, 1937REV. GEORGE WILLIAM AHR, S.T.D., was born in St. Patrick's parish, Newark, June 23, 1904. His parents were George and Mary (Mueller) Ahr. He attended St. Patrick's and St. Ann's Schools, Newark (1910-1917); St. Benedict's Preparatory School, Newark (1917-1918); St. Vincent's College, Latrobe, Pennsylvania (19181923); Seton Hall College (1923-1925); and North American

College, Rome (1925-1929). He was ordained there at Leonine 

Seminary, July 29, 1929, by Most Rev. De Sanctis. In 1929 he received the degree of Doctor of Sacred Theology from the Uni versity of Propaganda at Rome. 

Dr. Ahr served as an assistant at St. Mary's, Jersey City (Aug. 7, 1929-Nov. 2, 1929), and at St. Venantius, Orange (Nov. 1929-Sept., 1930). He taught at Seton Hall High School (Sept., 1930-June, 1933), and has been a professor at the Seminary of the Immaculate 

Conception since June, 1933. 

RT. REV. HUBERT J. BEHR, S.T.D., was born September 5, 1866. 

His parents were John J. and Catherine (Classen) Behr. He attended St. Boniface School, Paterson, N. J. (1872-1883); St. 

Peter's College, Jersey City (1883-1887); Seton Hall College (1887-

1888); and North American College, Rome, Italy (1888-1893).

Doctor Behr was ordained at St. John Lateran on May 27, 1893, by His Eminence Cardinal Lucide Mary Parocchi. He received the degree of Doctor of Sacred Theology from the University of 

Propaganda, Rome, in 1893. 

Dr. Behr served as assistant at St. Mary's, Jersey City (18931897); was vice-rector of Newark Diocesan Seminary (1897-1901); and has been pastor of St. Michael's, Elizabeth, since 1901. He was made Domestic Prelate on St. Hubert's Day, November 3, 1929.

He helped prepare Monsignor Flynn's history, The Catholic Church in New Jersey. He was one of the examiners who promoted Monsignor TerWoert to the pastorate of St. Mary's Church. 

At the request of Bishop Walsh, he preached the eulogy at 

Monsignor TerWoert's funeral. 

REV. JOHN F. BOYLE served as assistant under Father Ter- 

Woert at St. Mary's from August 12, 1900, to December 25, 1904.

He was born in St. John's parish, Worcester, Massachusetts, and was the son of Daniel F. and Elizabeth A. Boyle. After completing his grammar school education in the public schools, he entered the local classical high school, and was a member of the class of 1891. 

In June, 1894, he finished the classical course in Holy Cross College, and a year later he entered the Grand Seminary of Montreal, where he was ordained by Archbishop Bruchesi of Montreal on 

December 23, 1899. On Christmas Day of that year, he celebrated his first public Mass in St. John's, Worcester. 

Father Boyle's first appointment was to St. Mary's Star of the 

Sea, in Bayonne, New Jersey, where he served as curate for six months under Rt. Rev. Isaac P. Whelan. He was then transferred to St. Mary's Church in Jersey City. At the end of a period of almost five years, he returned to the Springfield Diocese, and was immediately sent to St. John's parish in Worcester, as an assistant to the Rt. Rev. Thomas Smith. This was the parish in which 

Father Boyle was baptized, was confirmed, received his First Communion, and celebrated his first public Mass. His next appointment was at St. Bernard's Church in Fitchburg, Massachusetts.

Later he was pastor of the Immaculate Conception Church at Lancaster, Massachusetts, for a period of two years preceding his death from a paralytic stroke on November 22, 1918. He died at St. Vincent's Hospital, Worcester.

APPENDIX 

171 REV. DANIEL J. BRADY served as assistant at St. Mary's, from 

September 26, 1897, until the death of Dean Senez on February

II, 1900, then as administrator of the parish until June 1 of that year, and finally, as assistant to Father TerWoert until April 21, 1901. He was born at Cortober, County Cavan, Ireland, on October 24, 1868; and was the son of Daniel Brady, a farmer, and 

Kate (O'Reilly) Brady. In March, 1885, he came to Jersey City, and the next month entered St. Mary's Institute. After studying there for more than a year, he spent two years (1886-1888) at St. 

Francis' College, Brooklyn. In 1888, he entered Seton Hall College, where he attained the degree of Bachelor of Arts in 1891. He then entered the Seminary, and on June 23, 1895, was ordained by Bishop Wigger, in St. Patrick's Cathedral, Newark. His first assignment was at St. John's in Paterson, where Dean McNulty, a successor of Father Senez, was then pastor. 

When Father Brady came to St. Mary's, Rev. James F. Mooney was administrator, and was about to be transferred to St. Cecelia's in Kearny. In 1898-1899, Father Brady oversaw extensive renovations, including the installation of electric lights, at the Catholic Club. After leaving St. Mary's, he was curate at St. Joseph's in 

Paterson, under Rev. Charles P. Gillen. From 1903 to 1906, Father

Brady was pastor of the Church of the Immaculate Conception at Franklin Furnace, Sussex County. He then established the 

Church of the Holy Rosary at Edgewater, where he remained until 1910.

At this time, Father Brady obtained a leave of absence, to become affiliated with the Diocese of Dallas, Texas. There he busied himself with the collection of funds for an orphan asylum of that diocese. In 1914 he returned to the Newark Diocese, and was appointed to St. George's, Paterson. He was Chaplain, in 1917, at St. Elizabeth's Hospital, in Elizabeth. After serving there eleven years, he became pastor of the Church of the Sacred Heart at Dover, where he was planning the erection of a new church edifice, when on December 16, 1937, he was appointed pastor of All 

Saints, Jersey City.

RT. REV. HENRY ATHANASIUS BRANN, D.D., LL.D. (Colgate University, Seton Hall College, Manhattan College, St. Francis 

Xavier's College, and Fordham University, and Litt.D., St. Peter's 

College, Jersey City), was an assistant priest under Dean Senez

at St. Mary's Church from 1864 to 1865. He was born August 

15, 1837, at Parkstown, County Meath, Ireland, seven miles from historic Tara. His father was James Brann, and his mother, Margaret (McGrath) Brann. The site of the house in which he was born is now known as Brann's Crossroads. In 1843, his elder brother, James Montgomery Brann, and a sister, Anne, settled in Rahway, New Jersey, where James became principal of the public school. 

In 1849, Henry A. Brann came to America with his parents, and after a short stay at Rahway, settled in Jersey City. There the family became members of St. Peter's parish, which then embraced Hudson County, the eastern part of Bergen County, and several small islands in the Hudson River near Jersey City. 

The first stranger that Henry Brann met in Jersey City was Father John Kelly, then pastor of St. Peter's, who immediately went to 

Brann home to welcome them. Father Kelly learned of James

Brann at Rahway, and eventually persuaded him to become the principal of St. Peter's parochial school. 

Henry A. Brann attended St. Peter's parish school, and then entered the College of St. Francis Xavier, New York City, where in 1857, as leader of his class, he attained with distinction the Bachelor's degree. He spent the following two years at the Sulpician Seminary at Mount St. Mary's, Emmitsburg, Maryland.

He next went to Paris, and for two years studied at the Grand Seminary of St. Sulpice. Then, at the suggestion of Archbishop 

Hughes, he went to Rome, where he became the first graduate of North American College. His first prefect of studies there was 

Rev. Patrick Hennessy, later pastor of St. Patrick's Church, Jersey 

City. 

Henry A. Brann was ordained on the feast of the Blessed 

Trinity, June 14, 1862, by Cardinal Patrizzi, in the basilica of St.

John Lateran, Rome. In August, 1862, Dr. Brann entered upon the duties of vice-president of Seton Hall, where he succeeded Dr. 

De Concilio as Professor of Moral and Dogmatic Theology. He remained there until 1864, when he became an assistant at St. 

Mary's, Jersey City. He was an important figure in the erection. and development of St. Mary's Institute. 

On September 25, 1864, at the dedication of St. Michael's Monastery, he delivered an eloquent discourse. In 1865, at the request

APPENDIX 

173 of Father John Kelly and the latter's brother, Eugene, Bishop Bayley appointed Father Brann as assistant to Father Kelly, whose health was failing, and Father Brann thus became virtually the acting pastor of St. Peter's, Jersey City, until Father Kelly's death in 1866. In that year Father Brann was transferred to the 

Church of the Madonna at Fort Lee. While stationed there, he erected the St. Cecilia's Church in Englewood, and began to build the first brick Holy Trinity Church at Hackensack. 

In 1867, at the request of Father Isaac Hecker, the head of the Congregation of St. Paul the Apostle, Father Brann became a 

Paulist, and for one year, in their house at Fifty-ninth Street and Ninth Avenue, New York City, he taught philosophy and theology. 

In 1869, Cardinal McCloskey, at the request of Most Rev. Richard 

Vincent Whelan, Bishop of Wheeling, West Virginia, sent Father

Brann to act as Director of the Seminary there, while Bishop Whelan was in attendance at the Vatican Council in Rome. At 

Wheeling, during the illness of Father Kreusch, Father Brann acted as pastor of the German Church there, for he was the only priest in the vicinity besides Kreusch who could speak German. 

His desire was to stay at Wheeling, but in 1870, Cardinal McCloskey appointed him pastor of Kingsbridge and Fort Washington parish, on the upper west side of New York City.

The southern boundary of this parish was 167th Street, and its northern boundary, Yonkers. Here Father Brann built the little church of St. John at Kingsbridge, and the Church of St. 

Elizabeth at 137th Street and Broadway. The ground for the latter was donated by James Gordon Bennett, Sr., who also contributed $5000 toward the erection of the building. During this pastorate, Father Brann wrote many of his books, and was Defen sor Vinculi and Censor Librorum. At the Third Plenary Council of Baltimore (1884), he was theologian to Archbishop McCloskey. 

On January 26, 1890, Most Rev. Michael A. Corrigan, the third

Archbishop of New York, an old friend and classmate at Rome, designated Dr. Brann as pastor of St. Agnes' Church on East 

Forty-third Street, Manhattan, as successor to another classmate, Dr. Harry McDowell, who had died a month earlier. On January 

15, 1910, while on a visit to Rome, Father Brann was made a 

Domestic Prelate by Pope Pius X. On August 25, 1911, Monsignor 

Brann opened the little Church of St. Agnes of the Snows at 

Jefferson, New Hampshire, the ground for which he secured

at his own expense. The edifice was paid for by summer sojourners in the White Mountains.

About the year 1900, St. Agnes Church in New York City was destroyed by fire. Monsignor Brann rebuilt it. At St. Agnes, he built a parish school five stories high, acquired two houses as a convent for the Mount Sisters, and built a new rectory adjoining the church. He also amassed a large sum of money as a foundation for the support of the parish school, when neighborhood conditions should change. In his endeavors, he was aided financially by many non-Catholics, including James Stillman, an Episcopalian and President of the National City Bank of New York, and Thomas L. James, who was Postmaster General under President 

Arthur. While pastor of St. Agnes, Monsignor Brann introduced there the Sisters of the Franciscan Missionary of St. Mary, whose mother-house is at Grotta Ferrata, near Rome, and whose work has spread throughout the world. They established a day nursery for the children of working women at 211-215 East Forty-fifth Street. 

Monsignor Brann died in the rectory of St. Agnes Church on 

December 28, 1921. He was, at the time, the oldest living Catholic priest in New York City. He was buried beside his parents, in old St. Peter's Cemetery in Jersey City.

Among Monsignor Brann's writings are: The History of the 

North American College at Rome; Life of Archbishop John 

Hughes (republished by Dodd, Mead and Co. in its Makers of

America Series; an essay, "The Popes as Champions of Popular 

Liberty"; Truth and Error; The Age of Unreason; Curious Questions; numerous Essays on Dante; and Waifs and Strays (a work in three volumes published by the Franciscan Missionary of Mary). 

REV. JAMES JOSEPH BRENNAN was an assistant at St. Mary's from April 30 to May 31, 1882. He was born March 1, 1850, in

Ireland. He was reared in St. Patrick's parish, Newark. He pursued a classical course at St. Charles, Ellicott City, Maryland, and at Seton Hall, and was graduated in 1872. On January 10, 1876, he was ordained at the Seminary there by Bishop Corrigan. Others also ordained at that time were Fathers Sheppard, Whelan, Mulligan, John F. Brady, and Burke. All became noted figures in New Jersey.

For a short period, Father Brennan was chaplain at St. Elizabeth's Convent, the motherhouse of the Diocesan Sisters of Charity.

APPENDIX 

175 He was an assistant at the Church of the Immaculate Conception at Camden, under Very Rev. Peter J. Fitzsimmons; at St. Joseph's, Jersey City, under Rt. Rev. Robert Seton; and at St. John's, Paterson, under Very Rev. William A. McNulty. On June 14, 1882,

Father Brennan was appointed pastor at Whippany and Northfield, in Morris County. On July 20, 1882, Morris Plains was substituted for Northfield, and Father Brennan was designated as the first pastor of Morris Plains and Whippany. He built the first church of St. Virgilius, which is now located at the boundary of 

Morris Plains. It is presently the charge of Rev. Philip Henebry, formerly curate and administrator of St. Mary's, Jersey City. Dr.

Thomas F. Burke was pastor of St. Virgilius (1926-1933), before his appointment as pastor of St. Mary's. 

In February, 1892, Father Brennan became rector of the Sacred 

Heart Church, Newark. He died there March 20, 1897.

VERY REV. ROBERT EMMET BURKE was born in the parish of 

Kilmore, Ireland, June 11, 1849. As a child, he was brought to this country and lived in St. John's Parish, Paterson. He was prepared for the priesthood at St. Charles College, Ellicott City, Maryland, and Seton Hall, South Orange. He was ordained by 

Bishop Corrigan, June 10, 1876.

Father Burke served as assistant at St. Mary's under its second pastor, Rev. Patrick Corrigan, from July 2 to September 1, 1876.

Then he accompanied Father Corrigan to the Church of Our Lady of Grace in Hoboken. Later, from a curacy under Monsignor De

Concilio at St. Michael's Church in Jersey City, Father Burke went to the Church of the Sacred Heart at Mount Holly on September 1, 1880, and remained there until 1884. He was then transferred to Phillipsburg. There, in 1886, he resumed work on the present church of St. Philip and St. James, which had remained incompleted for several years. He finished this church, fitted it with modern improvements, and built an addition to the rectory. 

On December 1, 1889, it was formally dedicated by the Most Rev. 

Michael J. O'Farrell, first Bishop of Trenton. Rev. John A. Sheppard-then of St. Nicholas, Passaic, and later pastor of St. Michael's in Jersey City-officiated as sub-deacon at the Solemn Mass. 

On September 6, 1897, Father Burke was appointed pastor of St. Mary's Church at Bordentown. He remained there until January 1, 1898, when he was transferred to Fort Hancock, Sandy Hook, 4

where he officiated two years. As early as 1894, Father Burke, while at Phillipsburg, had taken charge of the mission at this military post. Incidentally, it was Father Patrick Killeen, then a young pastor at Red Bank, New Jersey, who in 1861 established the first mission at the fort, and held services monthly for the Catholic soldiers of the garrison.

In the spring of 1900, Father Burke succeeded Monsignor Thomas R. Moran, a well-beloved pastor, at St. Paul's, Princeton. 

There Father Burke made some needed improvements, and beautified the church. He was succeeded, May 9, 1904, by Father Walter 

M. Leahy, the author of The Diocese of Trenton. 

Father Burke was Dean of Warren County. He died at Englewood Cliffs, on July 2, 1922, while chaplain at St. Michael's Villa. 

He was buried from St. John's Church, Paterson, July 5, 1922.

VERY REV. PATRICK BYRNE was born in Templemore, in the dio cese of Lismore, Tipperary County, Ireland, January 6, 1837. After receiving a classical education in his native diocese, he emigrated to America, and settled in the Diocese of Buffalo. He pursued a course in philosophy, first at Georgetown under the Jesuits, where he was a pupil of the famous Rev. Carlos Maria Maldonado, S.J.; and afterwards at St. Mary's Seminary, Baltimore, Maryland. Here he was a fellow student of James Cardinal Gibbons. Father Byrne was ordained in the Diocese of Newark by Bishop Bayley on 

October 7, 1859. 

After very short assignments at Princeton, New Brunswick, and 

Phillipsburg, Father Byrne became curate at St. Mary's, Jersey City, under Father Senez, from February 1, 1861 to the early part of 1862. He next served under Very Reverend Patrick Moran, at St. 

John's, Newark.

Father Byrne was appointed, in June, 1863, pastor at Camden. 

There he built the present Church of the Immaculate Conception, and acquired the cemetery where the early Catholics of Camden and vicinity buried their dead. He also attended chapels at Snow 

Hill, Fellowship, and Waterford. By establishing a building and loan association, he enabled many of his parishioners to purchase their own homes. He opened the second church in Camden, originally the Church of the Sacred Heart. 

In September, 1873, after Father Byrne had prepared plans and laid the foundation of a parochial school building in Camden,

APPENDIX 

177 he took charge of St. John's (now Sacred Heart Church) in 

Trenton. This was at the request of Bishop Bayley. There he remained until 1878, when he relinquished this pastorate after having built a magnificent school in which the quality of instruction was notably good. He was appointed dean by Bishop Bayley, whom for many years he then aided in diocesan work. He appears to have officiated (1869-1870) at St. Mary's in Dover, which Father Senez had begun in 1846.

From 1895 to 1987, Father Byrne was pastor at St. Leo's Church, Irvington, and officiated, too, at St. Aloysius Church at Caldwell, of which, in 1897, he became pastor. On March 25, 1895, he directed the dedication of the Chapel of the Institute of Holy Angels at 

Fort Lee. For ten years, as pastor of St. Aloysius, Caldwell, he attended to the spiritual needs of the Sisters of Mount S. Dominic's 

Academy in that town.

Father Byrne wrote extensively in various fields of literature, and was regarded as an authority in controversial theology. His library contained rare and old books on this subject. He ranked among the foremost preachers of his day, and he was always and everywhere a champion of civil and religious rights. 

Father Byrne was among the greatest leaders of the temperance cause in the United States. His initial work was done at St. 

Mary's, Jersey City, in the first years of his priesthood. With letters of endorsement from Bishop Bayley, he went through the country establishing local units of the Temperance and Benevolent Society, and within seven years he saw branches thriving in nearly all dioceses. He was, for twenty years, the first president of the 

Temperance and Benevolent Union of America. At the end of his term, the Union had 225 societies. Archbishop Ireland, Cotter, and 

McGildrick, as priests, were associated with him in his temperance work. As president of the Union, in 1876, he participated in the 

Philadelphia Centennial, having a position in line near General 

Grant, and the Emperor and Empress of Brazil. On July 4, 1876, the Union presented to Philadelphia the beautiful fountain that stands in Fairmount Park. Father Byrne was the principal speaker on that occasion. The fountain is symbolic: there are depicted 

Moses striking the rock, Father Matthew, Commodore Barry, Bishop Carroll, and Charles Carroll. Following the "Know

Nothing" period, this statue, it is said, gained respect for the Catholic Church in the community.

When Father Byrne was assigned to Caldwell, it was ata his own request, for he wished for quiet, and for the delightful air of that place. Ten years later, on June 14, 1907, after a week of illness, he died at St. Michael's Hospital, Newark. He was buried from St. Aloysius, Caldwell; Rev. Patrick Cody delivered the eulogy. Father Byrne was admired and respected by everybody; and the ministers of the Baptist, Methodist, and Presbyterian churches of his community feelingly commemorated his passing. 

His final resting place was in Camden, New Jersey.

REV. JOHN P. CALLAGHAN was a curate at St. Mary's from Sep tember 3, 1878, to March, 1882, and again, from March 6 to May 30, 1886. He was born in Newark, February 7, 1855. He attended 

St. Charles College in Maryland, and Seton Hall, where he was graduated in 1874. He read theology in the American College, Louvain, Belgium, and was ordained in the Cathedral at Mechlin, December 22, 1877. Apparently his first assignment was at St. 

Mary's. He labored at Mendham and Plainfield, and was rector at Hibernia. In 1882, he was placed in charge of St. Mary's, Plainfield, after the pastorate of Father Morris, and shortly before Rev. 

Patrick E. Smythe was designated as pastor there. He was made administrator of Our Lady Help of Christians in East Orange in 1893, when its rector, Rev. Michael J. Kerwin, was stricken with paralysis. On the latter's death, May 10, 1894, Father Callaghan was regularly appointed pastor. He died there on Ash Wednesday, February 25, 1914.

REV. LAWRENCE C. M. CARROLL served as an assistant at St. 

Mary's from July 8, 1883, to February 11, 1889. He was born in 

St. Patrick's parish, Newark, on May 4, 1854, and educated in the parochial schools of that parish and of St. John's. He then attended 

St. Charles at Ellicott City, Maryland, where he was graduated with honors. He entered Seton Hall College, and in June, 1875, attained the degree of Bachelor of Arts. He was ordained June 7, 1879. 

Father Carroll's first assignment was at St. John's Church, Orange, where he remained until July 4, 1883. He then became first assistant rector of St. Mary's, in Jersey City. He remained there until February 19, 1889, when he left, as a member of the first American Catholic Pilgrimage to visit the shrines of both 

Rome and the Holy Land.

APPENDIX 

179 In August, 1889, Father Carroll was appointed rector of Our Lady of Sorrows at South Orange, then a new parish, which he organized and where he built the church, school, and rectory. 

On March 15, 1896, he became pastor of St. Patrick's, Jersey City, succeeding the Rev. Patrick Hennessy, who had erected that church, and who had died on February 27, 1896, at the age of sixty-two, after a pastorate there of twenty-six years. Father Carroll undertook the erection of the present parish school and hall. 

After a long recurrent illness, Father Carroll died at St. Vincent's Hospital in New York City on August 7, 1908. He was succeeded at St. Patrick's by Rt. Rev. John A. Stafford, who, too, had been a curate under Dean Senez, at St. Mary's. Father Carroll's grave is in Holy Name Cemetery, Jersey City.

REV. FRANCIS LEO CONNOR was an assistant priest at St. Mary's from July 13, 1924 to May 24, 1926. He was born at Kingston,

Pennsylvania, in 1900, and came to the Diocese of Newark to be educated at Seton Hall College and the seminary at South Orange. 

He was ordained at St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, in 1924. 

After his labors at St. Mary's, Jersey City, Father Connor was stationed at the Church of the Sacred Heart, Vailsburg; St. John's, Paterson; and the Church of the Sacred Heart, Bloomfield.

On October 1, 1932, after a leave of absence from the diocese on account of illness, Father Connor was assigned as curate to St. James the Less in Newark. There he was stricken with appendicitis. He died, after an operation, at St. James' Hospital, Newark, on November 11, 1932. Among his surviving next of kin was his brother, Rev. Joseph Pierre Connor, an assistant at Our Lady of 

Lourdes, West Orange. Very Rev. William F. Lawlor, pastor of 

St. Mary's Star of the Sea, Bayonne, who delivered the funeral sermon at St. James, Newark, on November 14, 1932, described the dead priest as of sterling character. 

REV. PATRICK M. CORR, who was a curate at St. Mary's from

June 2, 1882 to September 6, 1885, was born about 1854 in the parish of Ballintemple, County Kildare, Ireland. He entered Seton 

Hall in September, 1876, and was there ordained priest June 15, 1878. He first labored as curate at St. James the Less, Newark. 

¹The Cathedral at Syracuse, New York, was built from the same plans, slightly modified.

Father Corr went from St. Mary's to Hackensack, in the fall of 

1886, to become pastor of the Church of the Holy Trinity. There, among his predecessors had been Fathers Patrick Corrigan, and

Henry A. Brann, noted figures at St. Mary's. At Holy Trinity, Father Corr worked most zealously, renovating the church, reducing the debt, and building a residence for the Sisters of Charity, whom he invited to take charge of the parochial school. 

He remained pastor of Holy Trinity until January 7, 1890, when Bishop Wigger appointed him pastor of St. Bridget's, Jersey 

City, in succession to the Rev. James M. Hanly, who, in 1886, had erected the present church of that parish. The energy of Father 

Corr in this new field of his activities was marked. His principal monument was the first large and well-appointed school in the parish.

In the vain hope of recruiting his failing health, he went to 

Ireland, and there he died on July 24, 1893. 

REV. JOHN ALOYSIUS COSTELLO was born in St. Michael's parish, Jersey City, September 9, 1910. His parents were Daniel and Delia (O'Grady) Costello. He attended St. Michael's Grammar School (1915-1923); St. Peter's Preparatory School (1923-1927); Seton Hall 

College (1927-1931); and Immaculate Conception Seminary (19311935). He was ordained June 15, 1935 at St. Patrick's Procathedral,

Newark, by Bishop Walsh. He served as assistant at Our Lady of the Lake, Verona, New Jersey, until December, 1937, when he was assigned to St. Mary's, Jersey City. 

REV. JAMES COYLE, first assistant of Father John Kelly, at St.

Peter's, Jersey City, in the middle 1850's, was born in Ireland about 

1828. He was ordained at St. Joseph's Seminary, Fordham, on

March 13, 1852. He was once an assistant at New Rochelle. He appears to have officiated at St. Mary's Chapel in Jersey City between November 27, 1856 and March 30, 1859, when he severed his connection with the Diocese of Newark. 

In October, 1856, after the Five Corners section, later Hudson City, was detached from a mission church in West Hoboken, Father Coyle was deputed by Father John Kelly to build a small church near the Erie Railroad tunnel, then in course of construction through Bergen Hill, a project which had brought many Catholics to that neighborhood. This church was a small frame

APPENDIX 

181 structure under the patronage of St. Bridget, and was located on what was then Clinton (now Hopkins) Avenue, near St. Paul's and Palisade Avenues. Before the completion of this church, which was the fourth Catholic church built in Hudson County, Rev. Aloysius Vanuta was made its pastor; and as the congregation grew rapidly, Father Vanuta, seeking a location for a new church, fixed upon the present site of St. Joseph's, at Baldwin and Pavonia Avenues, Jersey City. 

It is indicated that Father Coyle left the Newark Diocese because Bishop Bayley appointed a Frenchman and an Italian pastors respectively of St. Mary's and St. Joseph's. In the New 

York Diocese Father Coyle built three churches: Our Lady of the Rosary at Fishkill, dedicated October 16, 1864; St. Ann's, Jockey 

Hill, now Sawkill, blessed in June, 1869; and St. John's at Stony

Hollow, dedicated November 28, 1869. In July, 1867, he was made pastor of St. Mary's, Roundout. He died there, July 1, 1872, as the result of exposure to the excessive heat. He was buried under the church, on the Gospel side of the altar. 

REV. THOMAS FRANCIS CURRY was born in St. Anthony's parish, 

New York City, May 14, 1897. His parents were Thomas and Mary (Bornemann) Curry. He attended Grove Street Public School, New York City (1902-1909); St. Peter's Preparatory School, Jersey

City (1909-1913); St. Peter's College (1913-1915); Seton Hall College (1915-1917); and the Seminary of the Immaculate Conception, South Orange (1917-1921). He was ordained at St. Patrick's 

Procathedral, Newark, on May 21, 1921, by Most Rev. John J.

O'Connor. 

Father Curry served as assistant at the Church of the Immaculate 

Conception, Montclair (1921-1926); at St. John's, Orange (three months); at St. Mary's, Jersey City (1926-1928); at St. Michael's, Newark (1928-1936); and again at St. Mary's, Jersey City (1936).

Father Curry was an assistant at St. Mary's, when the new church was solemnly opened by Bishop O'Connor on September 26, 1926. 

RT. REV. JANUARIUS DE CONCILIO, D.D., was an assistant of 

Father Senez at St. Mary's from 1864 to 1867. He was born in 

Naples, Italy, on July 6, 1835, and educated under the celebrated philosopher San Severino, and at the College of Brignole-Sale,

Genoa. Several of his classmates became bishops and cardinals. 

In May, 1859, he was ordained at Genoa.

Coming to America soon after ordination, Dr. De Concilio was called to the chair of moral and dogmatic theology at the seminary at Seton Hall. He added luster to the fame of that institution, where he served in 1860 and 1861. Ill health caused his resignation, but later he occasionally gave instruction there. In 1862 and 

1863 he was an assistant of Rev. Anthony Cauvin at Our Lady of 

Grace, Hoboken. He was at St. Mary's, when Bishop Bayley erected 

St. Michael's parish, Jersey City, and in November, 1867, appointed him pastor of that new parish. There Dr. De Concilio first had only the brick building which had been St. Mary's Chapel, and for a short period he used it as both church and school. He soon secured an adjoining house as a convent for the Sisters in charge of the school. Then, at 185 Erie Street, he erected a four-story brick building. The basement he occupied as a rectory, and the upper part was used as a church. He next rebuilt the old chapel for use as a school only.

In 1872 Dr. De Concilio began the erection of the present St. 

Michael's Church on Ninth Street. It was dedicated by Bishop 

Corrigan on October 8, 1876, and was completed in 1878, having cost $130,000.

In 1876, Mr. Harold Henwood, a wealthy convert to the 

Catholic faith, purchased the Children's Home on Pavonia Avenue at Erie Street for $30,000, and presented it to St. Michael's parish. 

To fit it for orphan boys, Dr. De Concilio expended $10,000. Later, it was entirely rebuilt, and eventually it was used for the Sacred 

Heart Academy, a private school.

Dr. De Concilio went to Italy in 1882, and at Rome was the guest of Pope Leo XIII. He visited many places of interest on the 

Continent and in the Holy Land. At about this time, St. Michael's was the most populous Catholic parish in New Jersey. 

In 1884, the third Plenary Council of Baltimore honored Dr. De 

Concilio by adopting his revision of the catechism, which, known as the Baltimore Catechism, was in general use afterwards in Catholic Schools. His pre-eminence as a theologian, which particularly fitted him for this labor, was universally acknowledged. 

In May, 1887, Dr. De Concilio was made a Domestic Prelate. 

In the early 1890's, he erected at 240 Ninth Street, to the east of St. Michael's Church, the four-story red-brick residence, which was the parish rectory for about thirty-five years. During the pastorate of Monsignor Sheppard, his successor, it was renovated and re-

APPENDIX 

183 modelled for use as a convent. It was also during Monsignor 

Sheppard's pastorate that the Board of Education of Jersey City, by condemnation proceedings, acquired title to the property that was the orphan asylum and academy. Public School No. 37 now occupies the site. 

After April 15, 1896, the building at 185 Erie Street was for several years used as a parish club house. About 1910-11, with the old building (the original St. Mary's Chapel) at Erie and Tenth 

Streets, it was made, under the direction of Mr. John T. Rowland, into the present parish school.

In the fall of 1896, for reasons of health, Dr. De Concilio went abroad again. When he returned, on September 8, 1897, he was warmly welcomed by his parishioners. His health, however, had failed. He died of Bright's disease on March 22, 1898, in the rectory at 240 Ninth Street, Jersey City. His funeral Mass was celebrated by Father TerWoert, and his eulogy was pronounced by Rev. Charles H. Mackel, sometime administrator at St. Michael's. 

Father Mackel once said of him: "For a third of a century Monsignor De Concilio devoted the best attainments of learning and ability to the advancement of the people of St. Michael's. He saw a new generation grow up around him, and was justified in being proud of the nobility of the young men and women whom he baptized, instructed, and brought to the fullness of manhood and womanhood in the knowledge and practice of the truths of 

Catholic faith."

Among the books which Dr. De Concilio wrote were The 

Knowledge of Mary and The Elements of Intellectual Philosophy. 

RT. REV. JAMES THOMAS DELEHANTY was born in St. John's parish, Worcester, Massachusetts, on July 10, 1873. His parents were Michael and Catherine (Dugan) Delehanty. He attended the public school (1878-1887), the High School (1887-1891), and Holy Cross College (1891-1894), all in Worcester; and the Grand 

Seminary, Montreal, Canada (1894-1898). Father Delehanty was ordained at St. James Cathedral, Montreal, Canada, on December 

17, 1898 by Most Rev. Paul Bruchier D.D.

He served as assistant at St. Mary's, Jersey City (Feb.-Dec., 1899); at St. John's, Jersey City (Dec., 1899-June, 1903); and at St.

Mary's, Plainfield (June, 1903-May, 1908). He was pastor at St. 

Andrew's, Westwood (May, 1908-Aug., 1912); and at Our Lady

of Mt. Carmel, Boonton (1912-1925). St. John's Cathedral in 

Paterson, New Jersey, has been his charge since February, 1925.

He was made a Domestic Prelate, and is now the Vicar-General of his diocese.

REV. JOSEPH LEO DONNELLY was born in St. Antoninus parish, 

Newark, March 26, 1906. His parents were James Joseph and Anna

Elizabeth (Doran) Donnelly. He attended the Holy Name School, East Orange (until 1920); Seton Hall Preparatory School, South Orange (1920-1924); Seton Hall College, South Orange (19251929); and Immaculate Conception Seminary, Darlington, New

Jersey (1929-1933). Father Donnelly was ordained at St. Patrick's 

Procathedral, Newark, June 10, 1933, by Bishop Walsh. He served as assistant at St. Mary's Church, Jersey City, from June, 1933 to 

September, 1937, and since then at St. Michael's Church, Cranford, New Jersey. 

VERY REV. MICHAEL J. DONNELLY was an assistant at St. Mary's, under both Dean Senez, in 1898, and Monsignor TerWoert in the early 1900's. He was born March 21, 1871, in the Cathedral (St. Patrick's) parish in Newark. His parents were Michael and Mary (Bruen) Donnelly. He was educated at St. Mary's Academy, St. 

Vincent's Academy, and St. Benedict's, in Newark; and at Seton

Hall College and Immaculate Conception Seminary, in South 

Orange. He was ordained in St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, June 4, 1898, by Bishop Wigger.

Father Donnelly's first assignment was to the Church of St. 

John the Baptist, Jersey City, under Monsignor TerWoert. After he had labored at St. Mary's, he assisted Rev. John F. Boylan at 

Our Lady of the Valley, Orange, and Very Rev. Patrick Cody at 

St. James, Newark. Since December 3, 1909, Father Donnelly has been pastor of the historic rural church, St. Joseph's, Newton, New Jersey, a vicinity where, in the middle 1840's, Dean Senez was a zealous laborer. 

Father Donnelly is Dean of Sussex County and Consultor to the Bishop of Paterson.

REV. EUGENE A. FARRELL served at St. Mary's from February 

24, 1889, to June 22, 1892. In May, 1885, he was assistant to Rt. 

Rev. Joseph M. Flynn, rector of the Church of the Assumption at

APPENDIX 

185 Morristown. In the middle 1880's he was among the several priests of Morris County who attended to the spiritual needs of the parishioners of St. Cecilia's Church at Rockaway. He was a curate at 

St. Patrick's, Jersey City, in the later 1880's. From 1892 to 1895, he was pastor at St. Joseph's Church at Mendham, where he worked very earnestly and successfully. His popularity in the parishes in which he had first labored, aided him greatly in his efforts to reduce the debt of St. Joseph's. He was pastor of St. Peter's Church, Belleville, from June 12, 1895 to September 14, 1898, when death called him. His remains were buried in the churchyard cemetery. 

REV. JOHN JOSEPH FINNERTY officiated as curate at St. Mary's 

Church from May 31, 1916 to June 27, 1918, and from December 31, 1919 to September 12, 1920. He was born in Hoboken, November 17, 1888. His parents were Thomas and Mary (Burke) Finnerty. He attended St. Lawrence's parish school, Weehawken (1895-1904); St. Charles College, Ellicott City, Maryland (19051907); Seton Hall Preparatory School and College (1907-1912). 

After he completed his theological studies at the Immaculate Conception Seminary (1912-1916), he was ordained there by Bishop 

O'Connor on May 31, 1916.

Father Finnerty was an Army Chaplain in 1918-1919. His other assignments have been at St. Theresa's, Summit (1919); at St. 

Rose of Lima, Newark (1920-1928); at St. Patrick's, Elizabeth (1928-1935); and at St. Catherine's, Hillside, New Jersey, where he has been pastor since 1935. The Church of Christ the King at Hillside is also under his care. 

REV. ROBERT GEORGE FITZPATRICK was a curate at St. Mary's 

Church from March 14, 1915 until November 30, 1932. He thus enjoys the distinction of having served at St. Mary's longer than any other assistant. He was born August 1, 1888, in St. Joseph's parish, Jersey City. His father, Patrick, was an employee of the old Jersey City and Bergen Horse Railway Company, later (1894) the Consolidated Traction Company. His mother, Mary Rafter, emigrated from the neighborhood of Maryborough, Queen County, Ireland. Father Fitzpatrick was named after Most Rev. Robert

Seton (1839-1927), pastor of St. Joseph's, and afterwards Bishop of 

Heliopolis. Father Fitzpatrick was baptized by the Rt. Rev. Paul 

T. Carew, then an assistant at St. Joseph's, and now (1938) pastor

of St. John's, Orange, where Father Senez, prior to 1854, secured the site for the first frame church there, then called St. Ignatius. 

After five years at St. Joseph's School, Jersey City, Father Fitzpatrick, in 1900, entered St. Peter's High School. Among his teachers was Hon. Mark A. Sullivan, then recently graduated from St. 

Peter's College, and later a Judge of the Courts of Errors and 

Appeals of New Jersey and of the Common Pleas of Hudson County. Father Fitzpatrick attended Seton Hall High School, and matriculated in 1905 at Seton Hall College. He received the degree of Bachelor of Arts in 1908. He was ordained March 2, 1912, by Bishop O'Connor, and celebrated his first Mass at St. 

Joseph's Church in Jersey City, where he had been baptized and confirmed. He was assistant for exactly three years at the 

Church of the Blessed Sacrament, Newark. 

On March 9, 1915, Father Fitzpatrick was named assistant, under Father TerWoert, at St. Mary's. During his curacy, he witnessed the demolition of the old Catholic Institute, the erection on the enlarged site of the present parish school, the erection of the present rectory, and of the superstructure of the present church, and the enlargement and renovation of the former rectory building at 246 Second Street, now used as a convent by the Sisters of Charity. It was his task, in the early fall of 1918, to turn the key for the last time in the door of the Holy Name Club House on Jersey Avenue, in the presence of John Moriarity, now deceased, a grandson of Henry Carroll, one of the first trustees of the parish.

After the death of Monsignor TerWoert on November 8, 1932, Father Fitzpatrick was administrator of St. Mary's until December 10 of that year, when he was appointed pastor of his present charge, Corpus Christi Church at Hasbrouck Heights. On June 

21, 1936, there was sent to him as an assistant there, Rev. Francis 

M. Mulquin, who had grown up in St. Mary's parish and who had been ordained a few days earlier. 

REV. PHILIP J. HENEBRY was born in the parish of Portlaw, 

Killown, County Waterford, Ireland, on October 25, 1888. His parents were Michael and Johanna (Walsh) Henebry. He attended, in Ireland (1905-1913), Mount Molleray College, Waterford; St. John's College, Waterford; Maynooth College, County

APPENDIX 

187 3 Kildare; and the Royal University of Ireland.

Coming to America, he entered Seton Hall Seminary, South Orange (1913-1914). 

Father Henebry was ordained at Seton Hall Chapel on March 19, 1914, by Bishop O'Connor. He served as curate at St. Mary's 

Church, Paterson (March, 1914, to July, 1918), and at St. Mary's, Jersey City (July, 1919 to June, 1933). Since then he has been pastor of St. Virgilius Church, Morris Plains, where he succeeded 

Rev. Thomas F. Burke, Ph.D., S.T.D., the present pastor of 

St. Mary's, Jersey City.

RT. REV. CHARLES J. KELLY was an assistant at St. Mary's from 

September 13, 1885 to January 8, 1894. He was born in Plainfield, New Jersey, February 2, 1857, and studied at St. Charles' College, Ellicott City, Maryland, and at Seton Hall College, South Orange,

New Jersey. Upon completing his theological studies at Immaculate Conception Seminary, South Orange, he was ordained on 

June 7, 1881, in St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark. Father Kelly's first mission was at St. Aloysius, Newark, under Rev. Walter Fleming, an intensely zealous ascetic, whence he was transferred to St. 

Mary's, Jersey City. There, as already detailed, he labored with great fruit among the young men.

On January 9, 1894, he was appointed rector of Our Lady of 

Grace Church, Hoboken, in succession to Father Patrick Corrigan, who had been pastor of St. Mary's in 1876. In Hoboken, Father 

Kelly re-opened the shool, established a home for the orphans, and decorated the church. On Sunday, November 8, 1903, he directed the celebration of the Silver Jubilee of the dedication of Our 

Lady of Grace. 

Father Kelly was a prominent figure in the celebration, in 1903, of the Golden Jubilee of the Diocese of Newark. He was named 

Domestic Prelate in March, 1903. The honorary degree of Doctor of Laws and Letters was conferred upon him by Seton Hall College. 

He died November 11, 1911.

REV. THOMAS M. KILLEEN was born in New York City, Novem ber 3, 1834, and enjoyed the distinction of being the first student who was graduated from St. Francis Xavier's College in that city. 

He studied theology at the College of Propaganda in Rome, and was ordained by Bishop Bayley in Newark December 6, 1860. He was an assistant at St. James the Less, Newark, under Rev. James

Callan; at St. Mary's, Jersey City, under Father Senez; and at St. 

John's, Paterson, again under Father Callan.

Father Killeen became first resident pastor of St. James Church, at Red Bank, on April 5, 1863, a church that was previously a mission of South Amboy. In 1867 he was transferred to St. John's, Newark, where he remained pastor until August, 1876. He then became rector of St. Mary's Star of the Sea, Bayonne. In 1880 he erected the present church on Fourteenth Street and Avenue C (since enlarged to double its original size). He also built a convent on Fourteenth Street, and the first frame school of the parish (demolished in 1898).

After a pastorate of twenty years at St. Mary's Star of the Sea, Father Killeen retired from the active duties of the ministry in July, 1896. Among his assistants in Bayonne in the late seventies was 

Monsignor TerWoert. After his retirement from the active duties of the ministry, Father Killeen was a familiar figure in Jersey City until his death, on July 30, 1907.

REV. CHRISTOPHER A. LAWLESS was attached to St. Mary's Church, under Father TerWoert, from April 5, 1914 to June 20, 1915. He was born in Morristown, New Jersey, March 19, 1887. He attended the Bayley School there, and Seton Hall College, from which he was graduated in 1910. He then attended the seminary at South 

Orange, and was ordained on March 19, 1914. St. Mary's was his first assignment.

When war was declared against Germany, Father Lawless joined the army as a chaplain. He directed religious work at Camp 

Beauregard, Indiana; Camp Taylor, Kentucky; and Camp Lee,

Virginia. He was discharged from service, June 4, 1919. He was then assigned to St. Aloysius, Caldwell. After a short service there, he became an assistant of Rev. Ferdinand E. Bogner, pastor of Sts. 

Peter and Paul, Hoboken. After a year there, he assumed duties at 

Our Lady of Grace, Hoboken, under Rt. Rev. Eugene P. Carroll. 

He was chaplain of the Hoboken Fire Department and the Ho boken Post of the American Legion. 

His death, on August 22, 1922, was due to Bright's disease, after a breakdown of his health in March of that year. He was buried from the home of his parents at Elm and South Streets, Morristown; the Mass was at the Church of the Assumption. The Hoboken

APPENDIX 

189 Post attended in uniform, and there was present a delegation from the Hoboken Fire Department. 

REV. HENRY BERNARD LYNCH was a curate at St. Mary's, Jersey 

City, from July 2, 1912 to October 24, 1915. He was born on August 

14, 1888, in the parish of St. Mary's Star of the Sea, Bayonne. His parents were William and Mary (Drudy) Lynch, once residents at Lambertville, New Jersey, and he was named after Father Ter- Woert, who was always interested in his progress as student and priest. Father Lynch attended the parochial school in Bayonne, and the Bayonne Public High School. He was graduated in 1908 at 

Seton Hall College. Bishop O'Connor ordained him on June 1, 

1912, and assigned him to the Jersey City parish. He died May 29, 1919, after a short illness from pneumonia which had followed an attack of influenza. He was buried in Holy Name Cemetery, Jersey City. Rev. Robert G. Fitzpatrick, then a curate at St. Mary's,

Jersey City, assisted at his obsequies. 

Among Father Lynch's classmates at Seton Hall were Dr. Burke, present pastor of St. Mary's, Jersey City, and Fathers Fitzpatrick, Landers and Mullins, (the two latter, boys of St. Mary's, Jersey City) and Monsignor Lawlor, present rector of St. Mary's, Bayonne. 

REV. JAMES P. MCCORMICK was an assistant at St. Mary's from 

January 22, 1894 to June 30, 1896. He was educated at Niagara

University, and was ordained by Bishop Wigger at the Franciscan Monastery, Paterson. He was first affiliated with the diocese of 

Mobile, and the Archdiocese of New Orleans. After leaving St. 

Mary's, he was stationed at St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, and subsequently became pastor of St. Patrick's, Chatham. His poor health compelled him to apply for a leave of absence. He died of heart disease, at the age of forty-three, on May 29, 1903, in the home of relatives in New York City, and was buried from Our 

Lady of Grace, Hoboken on June 1, 1903.

REV. EDWARD MCCOSKER, an assistant at St. Mary's in 1860, was born in 1828, in the parish of Drumragh, in the diocese of Derry, Londonderry County, Ireland. He attended St. Mary's College, Wilmington, Delaware, and then entered St. Mary's Seminary at 

Baltimore, where, on June 18, 1859, he was ordained by the Most 

Reverend Francis Patrick Kenrick (1797-1863), Archbishop of Balti-

more, and founder of the Seminary of St. Charles Borromeo at Philadelphia.

Father McCosker was assistant at St. Peter's in New Brunswick under Rev. John Rogers, a noted pioneer priest; at St. Mary's in 

Jersey City under Father Senez; and then at St. John's, Newark under Very Rev. Patrick Moran, first Vicar-General of the Diocese of Newark. On August 12, 1861, Father McCosker was made pastor of St. Joseph's at Newton, seat of the ancient Sussex Court House. 

There he labored for nearly a score of years. He built a beautiful brick church and rectory at Newton, a frame mission church at Hackettstown, and a brick church at Franklin Furnace. He was thus active in the center of a district which Father Senez (18451848) used to visit from Madison. In 1881 Father McCosker was appointed pastor at St. Mary's, Rahway, founded in 1845, and built up (1854-1873) by Rev. Thomas Quinn, who had been the predecessor of Father Senez at St. John's, Paterson. 

On July 10, 1896, Father McCosker retired as Rector Emeritus of the Rahway church. During his decline, he lived at St. Joseph's 

Hospital, Paterson. He died on April 15, 1906. To his labors at

Newton there is a charming tribute written by Rev. Michael A. 

McManus (1849-1897), his successor at Newton. It is also an excellent sketch of Catholicity in northwestern New Jersey in the middle nineteenth century.

REV. PATRICK A. McGAHAN officiated at St. Mary's from February 

25 to June 8, 1883. He was born in Cully Hannah, County Armagh, Ireland, June 29, 1850. He attended Mount Melleray, Waterford, the American College at Louvain, and St. Nicholas Seminary, Belgium. He was ordained at Louvain May 22, 1875.

Father McGahan labored at St. Bridget's, Jersey City, in its early days; at St. John's, Trenton; and St. John's, Paterson. From August 

20, 1880 to December 1, 1883, he was pastor at St. Bernard's, Mount 

Hope, then a flourishing iron-mining section of Morris County. 

As early as 1781, Catholic families settled there. Father McGahan, in 1882, established a school which was conducted by the Sisters of Charity, but later, as the population dwindled, it was abandoned (1892). He was acting pastor of the Poles in Jersey City. He died in St. Vincent's Hospital, New York, July 22, 1894.

REV. JAMES MCKERNAN was a curate at St. Mary's from March 8, 

1874 to June, 1876. He was born in Ireland, September 21, 1834,

APPENDIX 

191 1TS 5 and ordained May 22, 1869. Originally he was a priest of the 

Oblates of Mary Immaculate (Oblate Fathers). In 1873, he was incardinated into the Diocese of Newark. During that year he was an assistant to Rev. James J. McGahan, the first resident pastor of the 

Church of the Holy Cross (then St. Pius) in Harrison, New Jersey. 

On the latter's death, on January 7, 1874, Father McKernan was made administrator of the parish, a post he filled until March 3, 1874, when Rev. Thaddeus Hogan, later an important figure in the 

Diocese of Trenton, was appointed rector. After his labors at St. 

Mary's, Father McKernan was pastor (1876-1879) at St. Mary's, Salem. 

In November, 1879, ill health compelled this zealous priest to resign his pastorate, to the intense regret of a devoted flock. From 

1880 to 1886, he was the first resident pastor of Our Lady of Good 

Counsel, Moorestown, where Rev. Patrick Byrne had been an early worker. From 1887 to 1891, Father McKernan was pastor of St. 

Joseph's, Beverly; and on March 1, 1891, he became pastor of St. 

Joseph's, North Plainfield, where he served until 1893. He then became pastor of St. Joseph's at Sea Isle City. Soon afterwards, he suffered a stroke of paralysis, which incapacitated him. For several years, he was a patient at St. Francis Hospital, Trenton, where he died March 26, 1914, in his eightieth year. 

REV. WILLIAM T. MCLAUGHLIN, a curate at St. Mary's from September 5, 1897 to September 11, 1897, was born in St. John's parish, Paterson, February 15, 1861. He received his early education at St.

John's parochial school, and at St. Francis College, Milwaukee, Wis. He entered Seton Hall College in 1885, and was ordained on 

May 23, 1891. His first curacy was at St. Lucy's in Jersey City, for three years. For a short period, he was then a curate at St. Patrick's 

Procathedral in Newark, whence he came to St. Mary's in Jersey 

City. He served again at St. Lucy's as an assistant to Rev. John J. 

Boylan, then rector there, and was next transferred to St. Patrick's at 

Chatham, where he spent nearly two years as pastor. From 1899 to 1911, he was in charge of St. Augustine's in Union Hill. Then, as pastor, he began his third and final period of service at St. Lucy's, Jersey City. This covered a period of thirteen years. After an illness of a year, during which he subjected himself to two operations for an affection of the throat, he found it necessary to be re-

lieved of his duties as pastor. He died in St. Francis Hospital, Jersey City, on April 30, 1924.

It is recorded that Father McLaughlin was an exemplary priest, a devoted worker in the cause of religion, and silent in his benefactions. An outstanding figure in Irish-American Societies, he was a member of The All-American Society; The Clan-na-Gael; an organizer of the Friends of Irish Freedom in city, county, and state; and chaplain of the local division of the Ancient Order of Hibernians. 

He was buried May 5, 1924, in the Cemetery of the Holy Sepulchre, Paterson, New Jersey.

REV. WILLIAM AUGUSTUS MAGUIRE was born in Hornell, New 

York, December 21, 1890. His parents were John F. and Ann (Ryan) Maguire. He attended Seton Hall College (1905-1910); 

Catholic University of America, Washington (1910-1911); and the 

American College, Louvain, Belgium (1911-1915). Father Maguire was ordained at Brussels, Belgium, July 4, 1915, by Most Rev. Tacci

Porcelli. He served as assistant at St. Mary's Church, Jersey City (August, 1915-May, 1916); and at the Church of the Immaculate 

Conception, Montclair (May, 1916-June, 1917). Since June, 1917, he has been serving in the United States Navy. Present rank is Commander; his post of duty, the Naval Training Station at San Diego, California, as Senior Chaplain.

REV. PATRICK AMBROSE MAHER was an assistant at St. Mary's in Jersey City from July 14, 1901 to February 18, 1906. He was the son of Patrick and Elizabeth (Mitchell) Maher, and was born at Mount Hope, Morris County, New Jersey, January 2, 1873. His father was a miner. Three days after his birth, he was taken by sleigh to St. Cecilia's Church, Rockaway, where he was baptized by Dr. Wigger, then pastor at St. Vincent's, Madison. 

For two years the boy attended the public school, held in the basement of the Methodist Church, at Mount Hope. In 1883 he entered St. Bernard's School, established there by Rev. Charles Gillen. He was confirmed by Bishop Wigger. From 1887 to 1891, Father Maher followed various pursuits, while under private instruction from Rev. James Hall, then pastor of St. Bernard's, Mount

Hope, and later (1912-1918) second pastor of St. Aloysius Church in Jersey City. After two years (1892-94) at St. Charles, Ellicott 

City, Maryland, the young man entered Seton Hall, where he was

APPENDIX 

193 graduated in 1897. After studying theology at the Seminary there, he was ordained June 29, 1901, by Most Rev. James A. McFaul,

Bishop of Trenton, who officiated because Bishop-elect O'Connor was awaiting consecration as the fourth Bishop of Newark, in succession to Bishop Wigger, who had died the previous January. 

In July, 1906, Father Maher was made pastor of St. Patrick's in 

Chatham, which Bishop Wigger had established, in 1871, while pastor at Madison. On April 1, 1914, he was transferred to St. Mary's at Rahway. From September 1, 1917 to November 1, 1920, he was pastor of St. Mary's at Dover, whence he went to St. Mary's at 

Plainfield. There he was pastor until his death, October 6, 1936. 

He was chaplain of the Catholic Daughters of America, and the 

Knights of Columbus, at Plainfield. 

REV. LEO J. MARTIN, a curate at St. Mary's, Jersey City, from 

December 10, 1933 to November 1, 1935, was born in the parish of 

St. Mary Star of the Sea, Bayonne, August, 17, 1903. His parents were Thomas and Helen (Gallagher) Martin. He attended St.

Peter's College, Jersey City (1918-1922), Seton Hall College (19231927), and Immaculate Conception Seminary, South Orange (19271931). He was ordained at St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, May 

30, 1931, by Bishop Walsh. For two years (1931-1933), he was stationed at the Aquinas House of Studies, South Orange, New Jersey.

Since December, 1936, he has been Chaplain at Holy Name Hospital, Teaneck, New Jersey.

REV. THOMAS JOSEPH MARTIN, a curate at St. Mary's from Sep tember 14, 1908 to June 12, 1912, was born in St. Joseph's parish, Newark, New Jersey, October 16 1882. He is the son of Stephen and Mary (McDonnell) Martin. He attended the parish school (1891-1895), the Cathedral (St. Patrick's) school (1896-1897); Seton 

Hall High School and College (1897-1904); and Immaculate Conception Seminary (1904-1907). He was ordained in the chapel there, by Bishop O'Connor, December 21, 1907. Until his appointment to St. Mary's, he was vice-president of Seton Hall College. 

From St. Mary's, Father Martin went to the church of St. Rose of Lima, Newark, where he was an assistant from June 12, 1912 to June 22, 1923. He was then appointed pastor of Our Lady of 

All Souls, East Orange, where his predecessors had been Monsignor 

John J. Murphy, and Rev. Thomas Edmond O'Shea, both graduates

of St. Mary's Institute, Jersey City. Father Martin, after a priestly career of thirty years, observes that he has not met better practical Catholics than those of his days at St. Mary's. 

Rt. Rev. James FRANCIS MOONEY was an assistant at St. Mary's from December 5, 1891 to April 4, 1898. He was born in St. 

Stephen's parish, Brooklyn, New York, on September 9, 1864, and was the eldest of the three sons of Bernard and Anna Mooney. His brothers were John J. Mooney M.D. (1866-1924), and George, who died in his early twenties. After preliminary studies at St. Francis 

Xavier College, Brooklyn (1871-1877), and St. Francis Xavier College, New York (1877-1880), and a short experience as a law clerk, James F. Mooney entered Seton Hall. There he was graduated in 1884. For scholastic endeavor, he won many medals at grammar and high school and at college, three being awarded when he was graduated from Seton Hall. He was sent to Genoa to study at 

Brignole-Sale, the institution at which saintly Bishop Wigger had completed his studies. He was ordained there on September 21, 1889. 

Father Mooney celebrated his first Mass in the United States at St. Stephen's Church, Brooklyn, where he had been baptized. In 

Italy, he acquired command of the native language, and upon his return to America he worked with great zeal in the Italian settlements in Newark and Jersey City. As assistant at St. Mary's, he was practically the administrator for Dean Senez. Through the good offices of Monsignor TerWoert, he was appointed pastor of St. 

Cecilia's, Kearny, about April 4, 1898. He served there until August 

1, 1901. During this period, he added four classrooms and a hall to the parish school.

As he was devoted to close study, he gave up his pastorate to become a professor at Seton Hall, where he filled the chairs of English and Latin in the college, and those of Moral Theology and Canon Law in the seminary. Within a short time, he became 

Director of the Seminary, and, in 1907, president of Seton Hall. 

To fulfill worthily his position as teacher of Theology, he privately prepared himself for the difficult examinations required of candidates for the Doctorate at the College of Brignole-Sale. As far as is known, he is one of few who there attained that degree, and, uniquely, he attained this distinction after serving in the ministry. 

Gifted and discreet, Father Mooney held many diocesan offices

APPENDIX 

195 which demanded skill and circumspection. As an educator, he devoted himself to his work; at the same time, by exact financing and care, he succeeded in freeing Seton Hall College from debt. He had, however, hardly achieved this result, on January 6, 1909, when, on Passion Sunday of that year, he saw the college building become a total loss through fire. With quick discernment and fearless resolution, he turned Alumni Hall into dormitories, recreation hall, and classrooms, and reopened the college on the Wednesday after Easter. 

He built the present high school building, and Bayley Hall, and laid aside sufficient moneys to meet the burse fund of the Immaculate 

Conception Seminary. 

For his erudition and accomplishments Father Mooney was honored with the degree of Doctor of Laws, in 1908, by Mt. St. Mary's 

College, Emmitsburg. On May 27, 1911, he was made a Domestic Prelate.

For many years, Father Mooney suffered from ill health, yet his was the longest administration that Seton Hall College had known. 

He resigned on August 2, 1922. After his resignation he remained 

Censor Librorum, as well as Judge of the Newark Diocese. He became irremovable pastor of St. Joseph's Church in Jersey City March 

19, 1923. He passed away quietly in the Lord on February 22, 1928. His successor at St. Joseph's was Most Rev. John A. Duffy, now Bishop of Buffalo. Monsignor Mooney was buried in the family plot in Holy Cross Cemetery, in Brooklyn. 

REV. JOHN P. MORRIS was born in St. John's parish, Paterson,

New Jersey, July 14, 1841. He attended St. John's School in Paterson, and other private schools. He pursued a college course at 

Seton Hall, where he was a classmate of Bishop Wigger; and made his theological studies at the American College in Rome, where he was ordained June 15, 1867.

Father Morris' first assignment was at St. Peter's Church, Jersey 

City. He was then sent to St. Mary's, where he officiated in 18681869, going from there to St. Patrick's, Elizabeth. In 1870, he became assistant of the Rev. John J. Connolly, then pastor at St. 

Mary's, Plainfield. When Father Connolly died a year later, Father

Morris became pastor. He built St. Mary's Church, Plainfield, the corner stone of which was laid in 1875. He also erected the Church of St. John the Evangelist, in Dunellen, which was dedicated October 24, 1880. Father Morris in 1882 was assigned to the Church of

Our Lady of Grace (St. Mary's), at Nutley (then known as Avondale). There he remained for twenty-two years, until his death on March 21, 1904. 

Father Morris was an accomplished musician, and an engaging character, making many friends among the non-Catholic of his community and furthering the cause of Catholicity. His inherited wealth, he gave freely to the poor.

At his funeral, Dean William A. McNulty was celebrant of the 

Mass. In the sermon, Monsignor Charles J. Kelly, a native of Plainfield, said: "Father Morris was the good shepherd, who carefully watched over his sheep, and cared tenderly for his flock. His name will not be forgotten in this parish; he will long be remembered." 

Father Morris was buried in Holy Sepulchre Cemetery, Paterson, beside his father and mother.

REV. JOHN M. NUBERG was an assistant at St. Mary's parish from 

1921 to 1923. His parents were John and Mary Nuberg, and he was born, July 27, 1894, in St. James parish, Newark. He was educated there in the parish school (1901-1909), at St. Benedict's Col lege, Newark (1909-1913), and at Seton Hall College and Immacu late Conception Seminary, South Orange. After his ordination, he also attended Fordham University. He was ordained, May 21, 1921, at St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, by Bishop O'Connor. 

Father Nuberg was a curate at St. Ann's, Jersey City, and at 

St. Michael's, Cranford, New Jersey. In November, 1937, he was assigned as assistant at St. Henry's, Bayonne, under Rev. Michael 

J. Mulligan, D.D., a nephew of the late Very Rev. Bernard J. 

Mulligan, Dean of Camden County. 

REV. EDMUND Joseph O'Keefe, a curate at St. Mary's from May 

14, 1916 to February 17, 1918, was the son of John and Nora (Roache) O'Keefe, of Ballynattin parish, Ballynoe, County Cork, Ireland, and a nephew of the late Jerome O'Keefe, long a leading dry-goods merchant of lower Jersey City. Father O'Keefe was born on January 10, 1891. He attended St. Coleman's, Fermoy, near his birthplace, until 1909, when he came to America, and entered Seton Hall College, where he was graduated in 1910. He was ordained by Bishop O'Connor, at South Orange, March 19, 1914. 

Father O'Keefe served first under Dean McNulty at St. John's, Paterson; then at St. Mary's under Monsignor TerWoert; at St.

APPENDIX 

197 

James the Less, Newark, under Dean Cody; and at Franklin Furnace under Father Shaw. His last curacy was at Our Lady Help of Christians, East Orange, in 1921.

He died in Ireland, at the home of his parents, on October 19, 1923, after suffering from tuberculosis for nearly seven years. A requiem Mass, well attended by the clergy of the Diocese of Newark, was celebrated at Orange on one of the last days of October, 1923. Father O'Keefe's body was buried in Ireland.

REV. EDWARD M. O'MALLEY was a curate at St. Mary's Church in Jersey City from June 26, 1904 to August 23, 1908. He was born in Clinton, Massachusetts, in 1873, and was educated in the public schools of that town, at Boston College, and at the University of Ottawa, and the Seminary of Quebec. He was ordained at Quebec, on December 17, 1898.

Father O'Malley's first assignment was to Holy Cross Church, Harrison, where he served two years. He was then transferred to 

St. Joseph's Church, Newark, and remained there about three years. 

He next served as assistant at St. Joseph's parish, Paterson, whence he came to St. Mary's, Jersey City. After his labors here, he became pastor of Our Lady of Mount Carmel Church, Ridgewood, in 1908. 

During his pastorate of twelve years in Ridgewood, Father O'Malley bought a plot of ground and erected a new church, which was dedicated on July 4, 1915. He was preparing to erect a new school, in 

1920, when, following the death of Rev. James J. McKeever, he was named pastor of St. Rose of Lima Church, Newark. He served there until his death, on February 14, 1938. He found St. Rose's parish flourishing and without debt; he replaced the old frame edifice with the present handsome church, which was dedicated in 1927. 

Two Masses were offered for the repose of his soul, on February 17, in St. Rose of Lima Church, Newark; the first was attended by the children of the parish, and the second, a Solemn Mass, was attended by the clergy and the adult parishioners. On the following day, a Mass of Requiem was offered in St. John's Church, Clinton, Massachusetts, and the remains of Father O'Malley were buried there in St. John's Cemetery.

The esteem in which Father O'Malley was held in the field of his last labors was evidenced, at his obsequies in Newark, by the tolling of the bell of the neighboring Methodist church.

REV. CHARLES F. SHAW was an assistant at St. Mary's, under 

Father TerWoert, from January 14, 1912 to March 25, 1914. He was born in St. Joseph's parish, Paterson, New Jersey, January 19, 1878. His parents were Thomas Shaw and Ellen (Gubbins) Shaw.

His mother was born, in 1841, on Coles Street near Second, when St. Peter's was the only parish within the present confines of Hudson County; and she and his father were married, in 1865, in old 

St. Mary's.

Father Shaw attended Public School No. 13, Paterson (18841890); St. Joseph's School in that city (1890-1892); Wyckoff Country

School (1892-1896); St. Peter's College, Jersey City (1896-1901); and Seton Hall College, South Orange (1901-1902). He obtained the degree of A.M. there in June, 1904. In June, 1906, he finished his theological studies at Immaculate Conception Seminary, and was ordained on the sixth day of that month by Bishop O'Connor. 

Father Shaw was first assigned as curate at Our Lady of the Valley, Orange, New Jersey. After four years, he was transferred to St. Aloysius, Jersey City. In 1912, he came to St. Mary's. He next served at St. Cecilia's, Kearny, until April 10, 1917, when he became assistant at St. Michael's, Jersey City. On December 28, 1918, he was appointed pastor of Immaculate Conception Church, Franklin Furnace, and on January 12, 1928, pastor of St. Aloysius, Newark, his present charge.

REV. CHARLES A. SMITH was a curate at St. Mary's Church, Jersey 

City, from July 12, 1896 to October 8, 1905. He was born in the 

Georgetown section of Washington, in the District of Columbia, and in his boyhood came to Jersey City with his parents. He was educated at St. Peter's College, and Seton Hall College. He completed his theological studies at the American College in Rome, and was ordained there at St. John Lateran Church, May 30, 1896. 

Father Smith served as assistant pastor of St. Mary's Church, Jersey City, under both Dean Senez and Monsignor TerWoert. In 

1905 he became pastor of Holy Trinity Church at Westfield, New Jersey, said to have been an early mission of Father Senez. From 

1905 to 1915, he served as Chancellor of the Diocese of Newark under Bishop O'Connor. In 1915, Father Smith was appointed irremovable pastor of St. Joseph's Church, Newark. After a six months' illness of heart disease, he died at the rectory, 221 West 

Market Street, on July 12, 1932.

APPENDIX 

199 Father Smith was noted for his work in the field of charity, and his interest in the parochial school system. He was survived by three sisters, Miss Mary Smith of Bayonne, Sister Maria De Sales, then of Mt. Carmel Convent, Tenafly, and Sister Agnes Teresa,

Superior of St. Patrick's Cathedral School. He also left a brother, William, who resides in Bayonne. His brother, Rev. James P.

Smith, pastor of St. Peter's, Belleville, predeceased him. 

REV. JAMES P. SMITH was appointed a curate at St. Mary's on 

August 13, 1873. Father Smith was born in 1841 near Fethard in

Tipperary County, Ireland, and was baptized in the parish of Cluaneen. At an early age, he evinced a vocation for the sacred ministry. He received his preliminary training at a classical school in his native Cashel. He also attended Mount Melleray, a noted institution in Waterford County; and All Hallows College, Dublin.

Having concluded to adopt the American mission as his future field of labor, he came, about 1868, to the United States. He was ordained in the Diocese of Newark by Bishop Bayley, February 20, 1869. 

Father Smith's first curacy was at St. Mary's, Jersey City. There he served for about three years, and endeared himself to all by his gentle manner and kindly disposition. In 1876, when the Passionist Fathers gave up several churches in which they had officiated, Father Smith was assigned by Bishop Corrigan to St. Paul of the 

Cross, a parish which then included that part of Jersey City Heights lying between St. Joseph's, and St. Michael's Monastery in Union

City. It is recorded that Father Smith continued at St. Paul's to labor with unflagging zeal, winning golden opinions, until, in the prime of life, he was overcome by malarial fever. He died at St. 

Francis Hospital, on December 13, 1887. He is buried in the old 

Hudson County Catholic Cemetery. Tonnele Avenue, Jersey City. 

REV. WILLIAM PETER SMITH was an assistant at St. Mary's at the time of the death of Dean Senez. He was born in Jersey City in 1873, and was the youngest son of John G. Smith (1829-1899), a building contractor, of County West Meath, Ireland. As a boy, Father Smith attended private schools, then St. Peter's College, Jersey City, and St. Francis Xavier College, New York. He was admitted to Seton Hall College, and was raised there to the priesthood on May 27, 1899. Bishop Wigger ordained him.

Father Smith was first assigned to St. Michael's, Jersey City, where he remained a year; he then became a curate at St. Mary's, where he had been baptized. After a year, he was transferred to St. 

John's, Paterson, where he served two years. He then served as curate at Summit, and also in St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, under Monsignor Doane. At the latter's death, Father Smith was transferred to St. James the Less, Newark, as assistant rector. Later he officiated in that capacity in Elizabeth, and at St. Mary's, Plainfield.

He was pastor of St. Michael's, Cranford, when he died on February 22, 1917, at the family residence, 94 Bentley Avenue, Jersey

City. He had cleared his last parish of debt, and put it on a sound financial footing. He was survived by two sisters and three brothers: Angela, Margaret, John F., Joseph A., and Rev. James J.

Smith (1856-1919), pastor of Holy Rosary Church, Elizabeth. 

In 1938, the last survivor of the family is Margaret, who with 

John F. Smith, K.S.G. (1858-1933) was honored by Pope Pius XI 

for benefactions to the church. Their chief contribution was the establishment of the Church of Christ the King, intended for the use of Negroes in Jersey City.

Both priests and their deceased brothers and sisters, are buried in the family plot in Holy Name Cemetery, Jersey City.

RT. REV. JOHN A. STAFFORD served at St. Mary's from September 

12, 1892 to August 4, 1893. He was born in St. John's parish, Paterson, New Jersey, March 13, 1857, when Father Senez was pastor there. He received his early training in that parish. His classical studies, which he began at St. Vincent's, Latrobe, Pennsylvania, where Monsignor TerWoert was an earlier student, were completed at Seton Hall. He pursued his theological studies in the 

American College at Rome, where he was ordained, April 8, 1888 

by His Eminence Cardinal Lucide Mary Parocchi. 

Monsignor Stafford's first curacy was at St. Mary's, Plainfield, whence he came to St. Mary's, Jersey City. In September, 1893, he was chosen vice-president of Seton Hall. On June 26, 1897, he became pastor of St. Augustine's, Union Hill. On May 10, 1899, he was elected president of Seton Hall. For a short period, he was administrator at Our Lady of Sorrows, South Orange. In March, 1903, he was named Domestic Prelate by Pope Leo XIII. Upon the death of Father Lawrence C. M. Carroll, Monsignor Stafford,

APPENDIX 

201 in August, 1908 became pastor of St. Patrick's, Jersey City, a position he held until his death on January 21, 1913. He completed the present parochial school and hall at St. Patrick's, which were begun by Father Carroll. 

REV. JOHN ANTONY VASSALO was a curate at St. Mary's as early as 

February 15, 1869. He was born at Murialdo in the Diocese of Mondovi, Italy, October 8, 1843. He was an alumnus of the College of 

Brignole-Sale, and was ordained at Genoa, June 15, 1867, and came to America soon afterwards. For a brief period, he was stationed at St. John's, Orange, and Orange Valley; New Brunswick, and

Morristown were other fields in which he labored before his assignment, on January 5, 1786, as pastor of St. Theresa's, Summit, of which he was rector for nearly thirty years. His predecessor there was Rev. Winand M. Wigger, later Bishop of Newark.

Father Vassalo found at Summit a rectory, a church which was little more than a chapel, a school which had but one room, and the foundations of a future schoolhouse which had been laid by Dr. 

Wigger. The completion of this building was the first of his labors. He brought to St. Theresa's the Sisters of Charity to replace lay teachers, who, for some years, had been employed in the education of the youth. Father Vassalo erected a convent for the Sisters, and enlarged the church to three times its original capacity. In 1902, for additional school facilities, he acquired and remodelled a large dwelling-house. During his administration, he saw his congregation increase from five hundred to two thousand parishioners.

After a long illness, Father Vassalo's health gave way in April, 1905, and, suffering from asthma and rheumatism, he was taken to St. Michael's Hospital, Newark, where he died, June 3, 1905.

REV. SAMUEL J. WALSH was an assistant at St. Mary's first in 1873, and later from June 17, 1877 to January 4, 1883. He was born in 

England about 1835, and educated for the priesthood at St. Sulpice, Paris. After ordination, about 1860, he returned to England, and for ten or twelve years was in charge of a mission in Liverpool. 

Then he came to America. At St. Mary's, Jersey City, he was assistant pastor. Early in 1883, he was appointed second resident rector at St. Mary's at Totawa (now Paterson). There he died, after an illness of several months, on February 25, 1885. His funeral was held two days later. Very Rev. William A. McNulty celebrated the Solemn Requiem Mass and delivered a short discourse; Bishop

Wigger gave the absolution. The body was then taken to the Cemetery of the Holy Sepulchre, Totawa, where it was interred. 

It is recorded that Father Walsh was a terse, forcible, and eloquent preacher, and, as he had travelled widely, he was full of that experience which enables a speaker to make many interesting com parisons and illustrations. 

REV. JOHN A. WESTMAN was a curate at St. Mary's from October 

30, 1910 to December 17 1911. He was born in Paterson, September 

29, 1872, and educated at Seton Hall, from which he was graduated in 1893. He was there an outstanding athlete. On June 12, 1897, he was ordained by Bishop Wigger in the Cathedral at Newark. He was an instructor and vice-president of Seton Hall. On March 5, 1902, he was appointed administrator of St. Michael's, Cranford, and later, its pastor. Subsequently, he was pastor of St. Stephen's, Arlington, and established Our Lady Queen of Peace, North Ar lington. In 1925, he became resident chaplain of Holy Name Hospital, Teaneck, where he died April 4, 1936.
