REV. WILLIAM JOSEPH CAFFREY was born January 9, 1886 in St. 

Cecilia's parish, Brooklyn, New York. His parents were Charles and Elizabeth (Landers) Caffrey. He attended St. Mary's Institute (1892-1900); St. Charles College, Ellicott City, Maryland (19061907); Seton Hall College (1907-1910); and Immaculate Conception 

Seminary (1910-1914). Bishop O'Connor ordained him there, March 19, 1914. 

Father Caffrey was assistant at St. John's, Paterson, New Jersey, from April 1, 1914 to May 1, 1926, a period which covered the last years of the pastorate of Very Rev. William McNulty, who died there June 18, 1922, after a service in that parish of fifty-nine years as a successor of Father Senez. Since May 1, 1926, Father Caffrey has officiated as director at the Catholic Protectory, Arlington, New 

Jersey. He is a first cousin of Rev. William Landers; they were pupils together at the old Institute. 

REV. WALTER AUGUSTINE COAR, priest of the Order of St. Augus tine, was born in Jersey City, January 5, 1868. His father was 

Robert B. Coar, for many years Water Purveyor of that city. Father 

Coar attended the Catholic Institute, and the Jersey City High 

School. On September 2, 1888, at Villanova, Pennsylvania, he entered the novitiate of the Augustinian Order. He was ordained in the Cathedral at Philadelphia, May 27, 1895, by Most Rev. Patrick

John Ryan (1831-1911). Father Coar taught at Villanova College, and there filled the offices of vice-president and prefect of discipline. On January 25, 1899, he was killed by a freight train at the 

Villanova station. He had gone there to take a train to Jersey City for a visit to relatives. He was buried at Villanova. 

REV. JOSEPH F. DOLAN was born in St. Bridget's parish, Jersey

City, on October 29, 1876. His parents were Peter and Margaret (Meany) Dolan. He attended St. Bridget's School (1881-1887); 

St. Mary's Institute (1887-1890); St. Peter's College, Jersey City (1890-1897); and College of Brignole-Sale, Genoa, Italy. He was ordained at Genoa, July 13, 1902, by Archbishop Iminotti.

Father Dolan celebrated his first Mass at St. Mary's, Jersey City, September 5, 1902. The sermon was preached by Rev. John J. 

Coppens, S.J., once stationed at St. Peter's, Jersey City, and then of 

Worcester, Mass. Father Dolan served as assistant at St. Michael's, Cranford (September 29, 1902 to 1903). He was administrator of St. Vincent's, Bayonne (1903); administrator at Irvington, New

Jersey (1904); assistant and administrator at Morristown, New

Jersey (1904-1910); assistant in Belleville (1910-1913). 

In 1913, Father Dolan became pastor of St. Mary's, Hackensack, and in 1919 pastor of St. Vincent's, Bayonne, which is still his charge. At sixty-one years of age, it is his opinion that St. Mary's school saved the Faith in Jersey City; that the Christian Brothers gave to old St. Mary's a name and fame in Hudson County; that no brighter page in the history of a parish was ever written than by the Christian Brothers, the disciples of St. John de La Salle; that the education given by them enabled their pupils to fill important positions; and that words connot describe what the Brothers did for poor boys.

REV. JOHN F. DONNELLAN was born in Jersey City on May 18, 

1872. He was the son of John F. and Margaret Donnellan, of that city. He received his early education at St. Mary's Academy, and at 

St. Mary's Institute. He was then a student for three years at the 

De La Salle Institute, New York City, where he was graduated with high honors. Upon the completion of his course at Seton 

Hall College, South Orange, New Jersey, in 1892, he received the degree of A.B. His theological studies were begun at Seton Hall 

Seminary, and completed at St. Bonaventure's Seminary, Alle gheny, Cataraugus County, New York. Here he was ordained on June 24, 1897 by the Most Reverend James E. Quigley, D.D., later Archbishop of Chicago. 

Father Donnellan celebrated his first Solemn Mass at St. Mary's 

Church, Jersey City, on Saturday, June 27, 1897. On July 9 of that year, he became an assistant at St. Peter's Cathedral, Erie, Pennsylvania. Eight months later, he was made its rector, and also became Chancellor of the Diocese of Erie.

In 1914 Father Donnellan's poor health compelled him to give up his work. He returned to Jersey City, where he lived with his sisters, the Misses Margaret and Frances Donnellan, at 642 Jersey

Avenue. Although it was hoped that he would recover his health, complications set in. His end came peacefully on Saturday afternoon, August 14, 1915, about five o'clock. On the following Tuesday afternoon and evening, his body reposed in St. Mary's Church.

An honor guard, composed of forty-five members of the Holy Name Club, in which the dead priest had always displayed great interest, kept constant watch over his remains.

On Tuesday evening, August 17, 1915, the men of the parish marched from the Club House to St. Mary's Church, to attend a service in honor of Father Donnellan. 

A Pontifical Mass was celebrated on Wednesday, August 18, 1915, at ten o'clock, by the Most Reverend John E. Fitzmaurice, Bishop of Erie, Pennsylvania. The entire membership of St. Mary's

Holy Name Society, more than a thousand men, were in attendance. 

The mass was attended by more than one hundred and fifty former parishioners of Father Donnellan, who came to Jersey City from Erie. In the visiting party were fourteen priests, and the full choir of St. Peter's Cathedral of Erie. Music was also rendered by singers from St. Patrick's Cathedral in New York City.

In accordance with the wishes of Father Donnellan's relatives, his body was buried in the family plot in the Holy Name Cemetery, Jersey City.

REV. JOSEPH A. DOOLING was born in St. Mary's parish, Jersey 

Clty, March 1, 1905. His parents were Edward J. and Mary T. (O'Mara) Dooling. He attended the parish school (1909-1916); St. 

Michael's School, Jersey City (1916-1917); St. Peter's High School,

Jersey City (1917-1921); Seton Hall College (1921-1925); and Im maculate Conception Seminary, South Orange (1925-1929). He was ordained by Bishop Walsh at St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, May 25, 1929. His first assignment (June, 1929 to December, 1930) was at Our Lady of Mount Carmel, Newark. Then he was administrator (until June, 1931) at Our Lady of Mount Virgin, Garfield, when he was named pastor of that church. 

REV. MARK JOSEPH DUFFY was born in the Procathedral parish of St. James, Brooklyn, New York, on October 21, 1878. His parents were Patrick and Annie (Smith) Duffy. The family later moved to Jersey City. Father Duffy attended St. Dominic's Academy on First Street, Jersey City (1883-1887); St. Mary's Institute (1887-1890); St. Peter's College (1890-1891); St. Charles College, Ellicott City, Maryland (1891-1893); Seton Hall (1893-1898); and the Seminary of the Immaculate Conception, South Orange (18981902). He was ordained there on May 24, 1902, by Bishop O'Connor.

Father Duffy was assigned at once to St. Michael's, Jersey City, where he was an assistant of Rt. Rev. John A. Sheppard until 1912. 

He was pastor of St. Vincent's, Bayonne (1912-1919), and then became third pastor, in succession to Rev. James A. Hall, at St. 

Aloysius, in Jersey City. Father Duffy is a nephew of the late Rt. 

Rev. Patrick W. Smith, and the only brother of Most Rev. John

A. Duffy, D.D., seventh Bishop of Buffalo. 

REV. JOHN A. EGAN, S.J., was born in Jersey City in 1882, and educated at St. Mary's Institute and St. Peter's College. He entered the Society of Jesus in 1904, and was ordained in 1921. For six years (1922-1928), he was in charge of work among the deaf mutes of the metropolitan New York district. His health failed in 1928. 

He recovered partially, and went to Baltimore where he also worked among deaf mutes. He died at Worcester, Massachusetts, August 

31, 1930, and was buried at St. Andrew's on the Hudson. He was a brother of Rev. Peter Francis Egan, the sketch of whose life follows.

REV. PETER FRANCIS EGAN was born in St. Mary's parish, Jersey 

City, June 7, 1886, the son of Peter F. and Margaret (McMahon)

Egan. He was educated at St. Mary's Institute, Seton Hall College 

(A. B. 1907) and, after study at the Immaculate Conception Semi nary, South Orange, was ordained by Bishop O'Connor at St. 

Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, June 10, 1911.

Father Egan was assistant at St. Leo's, Irvington, from 1911 to 

1913. He was then transferred to St. Bridget's, Jersey City. He served as assistant there until the World War, when he went overseas as Chaplain. From 1921 to 1926, he was a curate at St. Joseph's, Roselle. From 1926 until his death, May 10, 1937, he was pastor of 

St. Francis de Sales Church, Lodi, which Father Senez in the 

1850's had established as a mission. Father Egan was buried in 

Holy Name Cemetery, Jersey City. 

REV. JAMES FRANCIS FLANAGAN was born in St. Mary's parish, 

Jersey City, June 15, 1891. His parents were Thomas F. and Julia

A. (Looney) Flanagan. He attended St. Mary's Institute (18971905); Seton Hall High School (1905-1908); Seton Hall College 

(1908-1912); and the Seminary of the Immaculate Conception, at 

South Orange (1912-1916). He was ordained there on May 3, 1916, by Bishop O'Connor. 

On May 7, 1916, at St. Mary's, Father Flanagan celebrated his first

Solemn Mass. He was assigned (1916) as assistant at St. Joseph's 

Church, Newark, where he became pastor on June 3, 1933, succeeding the Reverend Charles A. Smith, who was a former assistant at St. Mary's.

REV. JOSEPH JAMES GATELY attended St. Mary's Institute in the 

1880's. He was graduated from Seton Hall College in 1890, and was ordained at the seminary there, December 23, 1893. He was curate at St. Henry's, Bayonne, and at St. Patrick's, Newark, and was the first resident pastor of St. Vincent's, Bayonne. For reasons of health, he went to New Mexico, and was stationed there for several years in the vicinity of Albuquerque. He returned to New 

Jersey, and after laboring at Convent for a short period, was appointed pastor of Immaculate Conception Church, Montclair, where he died October 24, 1926. Bishop McLaughlin, then a professor at Seton Hall, preached his eulogy.

VERY REVEREND EDWARD AUGUSTINE GILGAN, priest of the Society of St. Sulpice, was born May 24, 1873, at Fishkill, New York. His parents were John Joseph Gilgan, a carpenter, and Agnes Letitia (Mahon) Gilgan. The family settled in St. Mary's parish in May, 1877. Dr. Gilgan attended the parochial school (1879-1888) and was then employed (1888-1895) as clerk in the construction aid maintenance department of the Pennsylvania Railroad. He was privately taught (1893-1895) by Rt. Rev. James F. Mooney. He attended (1895-1900) St. Charles College, Ellicott City, Maryland;

St. Mary's Seminary, Baltimore (1900-1903); whence he went to the 

Seminary of St. Sulpice, Paris (1903-1905). He was there ordained, July 1, 1905. At Issy, France, he spent a year (1905-1906) in study and travel.

Dr. Gilgan celebrated his first Mass in America, in the basement of the present St. Mary's Church, in July, 1906. He then pursued post-graduate courses at the Catholic University, Washington (19061908). In September, 1908, he became professor of dogmatic theology at St. Patrick's Seminary, Menlo Park, California, a position he held until June 1914. That fall, he began to teach philosophy at 

St. Mary's Seminary, Baltimore. He was also charged with the special work of collecting funds for the Sulpician Seminary, Catholic 

University. 

For seven years (1917-1924) Dr. Gilgan was professor of dogmatic theology at St. Mary's Seminary, as well as vice-president of 

Divinity College. In September, 1924, he became president of the latter institution, but because of a prolonged attack of arthritis, he retired from that office in January, 1934. He took up residence for treatments at Providence Hospital, Washington. In January, 

1937, he entered Jenkins Memorial Hospital, Baltimore, where he still suffers patiently. 

He attained the following degrees: A.B., June 12, 1901, St. 

Mary's, Baltimore; A.M., June 14, 1902, same institution; S.T.L., June 10, 1908, Catholic University, Washington, D. C.; and S.T.D., June, 1918 at St. Mary's Baltimore. He and his sister, Mrs. Letitia

Nolan, who is a parishioner at St. Mary's, are the last of a family of several children reared in the parish. 

REV. JOHN EDWARD KIERNAN was born in St. Mary's parish 

August 17, 1881. His parents were Bartholomew and Catherine (Cogan) Kiernan. He attended St. Mary's Institute (1888-1893);

APPENDIX 

209 St. Peter's High School and College (1894-1901), and Immaculate 

Conception Seminary (1902-1906). He was ordained at St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, June 9, 1906, by Bishop O'Connor.

There he served as an assistant until May 7, 1920, when he was made pastor of the Church of St. John the Baptist of Cliffside, New 

Jersey. He has recollections of having served Dean Senez as an altar boy.

REV. WILLIAM JOSEPH LANDERS was born in St. Gabriel's parish, 

New York City, June 8, 1888. His parents were Garrett and Kate (Brosnan) Landers. He attended St. Mary's Catholic Institute, Jersey City (1894-1902); St. Charles College, Ellicott City, Maryland (1902-1905); Seton Hall College, South Orange (1905-1908); and

Immaculate Conception Seminary, South Orange (1908-1912). He was ordained at St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, on June 1, 1912, by Bishop O'Connor. He celebrated his first Solemn Mass, in St. 

Mary's, on June 2, 1912.

Father Landers served as assistant at the Church of Our Lady 

Help of Christians, East Orange (1912); at Sacred Heart Church, Newark (1914-1922); and at St. Aloysius, Jersey City (1922-1928).

He was pastor of the Church of the Holy Rosary, Edgewater (19281936). He is the present pastor of St. Cassian's, Upper Montclair. 

On September 26, 1926, Father Landers was celebrant at the Vesper Services at the opening of the new St. Mary's Church. 

REV. MICHAEL F. MCGUINNESS was an assistant at St. Mary's 

Church, under Father TerWoert, from May 12, 1901 until November 5, 1902. He was born in the parish of Our Lady of Mercy, Brooklyn, New York, on November 20, 1868, the eldest son of 

Michael McGuinness and Ann McLaughlin, natives of Mullingar,

West Meath County, Ireland. His father, who was a journeyman baker in Brooklyn, settled in St. Bridget's parish, Jersey City, in the 

1870's, and for many years conducted a grocery and butcher business.

Father McGuinness attended St. Bridget's parochial school under the Sisters of Charity, and the Catholic Institute under the Christian Brothers. He also attended Public School No. 1 for two years or more, where the principal was George H. Lindsley, a noted pedagogue, and a teacher was Miss Eliza Tennant, a sister of Hon. 

George G. Tennant, one time Judge of the Court of Common Pleas

of Hudson County. Father McGuinness attended the Jersey City

High School for three years (1881-1884). 

After five years in various pursuits, Father McGuinness entered Seton Hall College. [His thoughts for entering the priesthood had been first stirred by a sermon of the Redemptorist Father Frawley, at a mission at St. Bridget's, in 1880.] He was graduated in 1894. 

He studied theology at the Seminary there, and was ordained, June 

4, 1898, by Bishop Wigger.

His first assignement was to the Church of St. James the Less, in Newark, under Rev. Patrick Cody. He was next assigned to St.

Mary's. By his good-natured zeal, he helped quiet a deep unrest among some of the Irish parishioners, who had manifested not over kindly respect for Father TerWoert in the early days of his pastorate. When he came to St. Mary's, he found a Holy Name Society consisting of twelve members. Within a year he had increased its number to eight hundred. His activities attracted the attention of other priests in lower Jersey City, who joined with him in the development of the Holy Name Society in northern New Jersey. 

In November, 1902, Father McGuinness was transferred to St.

Paul's of the Cross in the Hudson City section of Jersey City, where he remained until January, 1906. Then he returned, for a period of six months, to the Church of St. James the Less in Newark. In 

June, 1906, he was made pastor of the Church of St. Mary of the 

Immaculate Conception at Franklin Furnace in Sussex County; 

and on December 30, 1912, he became pastor of St. Mary Help of 

Christians, in Paterson, his present charge. He is apparently the only boy who attended the Institute and afterwards became a curate at St. Mary's.

Father McGuinness is a member of the Board of Consultors of the newly-erected Diocese of Paterson. 

REV. BEDE JOSEPH MCINTYRE, O.F.M., was born in St. Patrick's parish, Jersey City, December 21, 1894. His parents were James and 

Bridget (Hurst) McIntyre. He attended St. Mary's Institute, Jer sey City (1905-1908); Blackrock College, Dublin, Ireland (19091913); Duquesne University, Pittsburgh (1914-1918); and Saint

Bonaventure's Seminary, Alleghany, N. Y. (1918-1922). 

Father McIntyre was ordained at the church of St. Francis of Assisi, New York City, on August 31, 1924, by Most Reverend 

Amandus Bahlmann, O.F.M. He served as professor at St. Joseph's

APPENDIX 

211 Seminary, Callicoon, New York (1922-1924); then at St. Bonaventure's College, Alleghany, New York (1924-1928). He was an assistant at Holy Name Church, Garfield, New Jersey (1928-1933), and at the Retreat House for Men, St. Francis Friary, Brookline, Massachusetts (1933-1937). He is now professor at St. Bonaventure's 

College, Alleghany, New York. 

REV. HENRY FRANCIS MACKIN was born in St. Mary's parish, July 

13, 1900, and is the son of the late Peter J. Mackin and Helen V. (Kearney) Mackin. He attended the Catholic Institute (19071916); St. Peter's High School (1916-1920); Seton Hall College (1922-1926); and Immaculate Conception Seminary (1926-1930). 

Bishop Walsh ordained him in St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, June 8, 1930. His assignments have been: St. Cecilia's, Rockaway, New Jersey (June 1930-June 1932); St. Aloysius, Caldwell (June 

1932-June 1933); St. Vincent de Paul's, Bayonne (June 1933-December 1936); and since then, he has been Chaplain at St. Francis 

Hospital, Jersey City. 

REV. THOMAS A. MULLINS was born in St. Mary's parish, Jersey 

City, in 1889. He was the son of James and Margaret Mullins, long members of the congregation. He was graduated from the Cath olic Institute, Seton Hall College, and Immaculate Conception 

Seminary. He was ordained there by Bishop O'Connor March 2, 1912. He celebrated his first Mass, in the basement of the present 

St. Mary's Church, the following day.

Father Mullins was first assigned to St. Paul's, Jersey City. In 

1913, he was transferred to the Church of the Assumption at Morristown, where after a curacy of seven years, he succumbed to pneumonia. He died February 4, 1920, at All Soul's Hospital, Mor ristown. On February 9, 1920, after a Solemn Mass of Requiem celebrated by Father William A. Landers in St. Mary's, Jersey 

City, he was buried in Holy Name Cemetery, Jersey City.

REV. FRANCIS MICHAEL JOSEPH MULQUINN was born in St. Mary's parish, Jersey City, on October 15, 1909. His parents were Michael and Mary (Fogarty) Mulquinn. He attended the Catholic Institute, Jersey City (1916-1924); St. Peter's College (1924-1928); Seton 

Hall College (1928-1932); and the Immaculate Conception Semi nary, Darlington (1932-1936).

He was ordained at the Procathedral

in Newark on June 6, 1936, by Bishop Walsh. Since then, Father 

Mulquinn has served as assistant at Corpus Christi Church, Hasbrouck Heights. 

RT. REV. JOHN J. MURPHY was born in St. Michael's parish, Jersey

City, December 7, 1876. His parents were Daniel and Mary (Fitzgerald) Murphy, who resided in that city the greater part of their lives. He attended St. Mary's Institute (1882-1892), and was confirmed in St. Mary's Church. He also attended De La Salle Insti tute, New York City (1892-1893), and St. Peter's High School and 

College (1893-1895). In 1895, he entered the sophomore class at 

Seton Hall College, from which he was graduated in 1898 with the degree of A.B. He was among the last priests of the diocese to receive minor orders at the hands of Bishop Wigger; and was the first priest ordained (September 21, 1901) by Bishop O'Connor. 

Monsignor Murphy was head-master at Bayley Hall, in South

Orange, for a year. In July, 1902, he was assigned as curate at St. 

Bridget's, Jersey City, where he labored for twelve years, On June 12, 1914, he was made pastor of the new parish of Our Lady of All 

Souls, East Orange. In August, 1920, he succeeded the late Rt. Rev.

Patrick Cody as rector of St. James the Less, Newark, a parish for which Father Senez had bought the first church site in the early 

1850's. In January, 1932, Monsignor Murphy, in succession to Rev.

Henry G. Coyne, became pastor of the Church of the Sacred Heart, Vailsburg (Newark), where Father Senez, in 1850, laid the foundations of St. Mary's Orphan Asylum. Vailsburg was then within St. 

Patrick's parish, Newark, of which Father Senez was pastor.

REV. MAURICE PETER O'CONNOR was born March 29, 1850 in Scot land, of parents who were among exiles driven from Ireland by famine. At eleven years of age, he came with them to America.

The family settled in Jersey City, where the lad came under the instruction of Father Senez. He attended St. Mary's parish school, then probably held in the basement of the first church at Erie and Tenth Streets. He was sent to St. Charles College, Ellicott City, and later to Seton Hall, where he was graduated in 1873. Bishops 

McFaul and O'Connor were his classmates. Father O'Connor was ordained there, in the Seminary Chapel, on May 6, 1877, by Archbishop Corrigan, and was regarded as the first boy of St. Mary's parish to be called to the holy priesthood.

APPENDIX 

213 Father O'Connor celebrated his first Mass, at St. Mary's Church, on or about May 28, 1877. His first assignments were to St. John's, Trenton, and St. Columba's, Newark. He was assistant (18791882) at St. Joseph's, Newark. In the spring of 1882, he became the first rector of Our Lady Help of Christians, East Orange. On 

December 6, 1884, he assumed charge at the Church of St. Pius, Harrison. There, on August 15, 1886, a new edifice was begun, since known as the Church of the Holy Cross. 

Father O'Connor's labors at Harrison, where he died December 12, 1913, are recorded in large measure in Monsignor Flynn's History. Rev. Bernard F. Bogan, eulogist of Father O'Connor, said: "He labored faithfully for the honor and glory of God, the preservation of the Faith, and the honor of the community." The Newark 

Evening News stated: "He was outspoken in his criticism of municipal ills, from the pulpit and as a citizen. His activity made him well known throughout the state." Resolutions, adopted and published by the Mayor and Common Council of Harrison, recited Father O'Connor's efforts in the interest of the town and its citizens and noted that "by his integrity, ability, and wisdom, he commanded and obtained the highest respect." He was eminently open-hearted. 

REV. THOMAS EDMOND O'SHEA was born in St. Mary's parish, 

Jersey City on December 30, 1867. As a young man, he attended 

St. Mary's Institute. In 1881, he entered the novitiate of the Christian Brothers at Ammendale. In 1891, he entered Fordham College, where he remained until 1897. In September of that year, he entered St. Mary's Seminary at Baltimore, where he remained for one year. He was ordained by James Cardinal Gibbons on December 22, 1900. 

Prior to 1903, Father O'Shea was an assistant at St. Nicholas, Passaic; and in 1911, at St. Joseph's, Jersey City. From 1912 to 

1919, he was pastor of St. Mary Magdalen Church, Newark. In 

1920, he became pastor of Our Lady of All Souls, in the Ampere section of East Orange, New Jersey. 

Father O'Shea died suddenly, after making three sick calls, on 

Friday, September 17, 1923, at East Orange. He had been engaged on plans for a new church, rectory, school, and a convent. The funeral plans were arranged by Rev. Thomas J. Martin, who formerly had been an assistant at St. Mary's, Jersey City. The services for Father O'Shea were held on September 20, 1923, at the Church

of Our Lady Help of Christians in East Orange. Rt. Rev. Francis 

P. McHugh, of St. Patrick's Procathedral, in his eulogy said: "In a simple way Father O'Shea went about fishing for souls, and by his exemplary life was always doing good. He was gentle, kindly, and sacrificing, with ideals that were Godly. Ever hating sin, he always preached truth and virtue.” 

Father O'Shea was buried in the family plot in Holy Name 

Cemetery, Jersey City.

REV. JAMES JOSEPH QUINN was born in Montreal, Canada, about 

1862; and in the 1870's was a pupil at the Institute. He was educated at St. Charles College, Ellicott City, Maryland, and at Seton 

Hall (A.B. 1884). He was ordained May 26, 1888. He was a curate at St. Michael's, Newark (1888-1890); and at St. John's, Paterson (1890-1894). He died of a lung infection at St. Joseph's Hospital, in the latter city, on October 15, 1894. There he had been an inmate for a year. He was an untiring worker and a patient sufferer. 

Rev. Joseph F. Dolan was celebrant, and Rev. Patrick W. Smith, master of ceremonies, at the funeral Mass in St. John's, Paterson, October 19, 1894. The eulogy was delivered by Rev. Charles Henry

Mackel. Father Quinn was buried in the cemetery of the Holy 

Sepulchre, Paterson. 

REV. RICHARD RUSH RANKIN, priest of the Society of Jesus, was born in St. Mary's parish, January 26, 1881. His parents were 

Richard Rush and Alice (O'Beirne) Rankin. Father Rankin in his early days attended School No. 5, on Bay Street, where his mother then taught. He attended St. Mary's Academy (1890-1892); St. 

Peter's High School and College, Jersey City (1892-1899); and the 

Jesuit Novitiate, Frederick, Maryland (1899-1903). He was ordained at Woodstock, that state, in June, 1914, by Cardinal Gibbons, and celebrated his first Mass there. He is at present stationed at St. Peter's, Jersey City. 

RT. REV. PATRICK W. SMITH was born in St. Mary's parish, Jersey City, on March 12, 1864. He attended St. Mary's Institute (18711878); St. Peter's College in Jersey City (1878-1881); Seton Hall

College (1881-1885); and the Seminary of the Immaculate Conception, South Orange, New Jersey (1885-1888). He was ordained by 

Bishop Wigger in St. Patrick's Procathedral, Newark, May 26, 1888.

APPENDIX 

215 

Father Smith was first assigned as curate to St. Joseph's, Jersey

City (1888-1897); he next served as pastor of St. Mary Magdalen in 

Newark until June 1900, when he succeeded Father TerWoert as pastor of St. John the Baptist Church in Jersey City. This charge he held until November 20, 1934, when he died suddenly in Syracuse, New York, at the residence of his nephew, Most Rev. John 

A. Duffy, then Bishop of Syracuse.

Monsignor Smith was a member of the Newark Diocese School Board after 1920, and was elevated to the rank of Domestic Prelate by Pope Pius XI on August 12, 1929. In 1933, he participated joyfully in the ceremonies of the consecration and installation of his nephew John as the fourth Bishop of Syracuse. Another nephew,

Rev. Mark Joseph Duffy, is the pastor of St. Aloysius in Jersey 

City; and a third nephew, Rev. Garnet W. Keen, is an assistant at the Church of the Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary, Morristown.

The Solemn Pontifical Mass of Requiem for the soul of Monsignor Smith was sung by Bishop Duffy. Father Mark Duffy was deacon, and Father Keen, sub-deacon. The eulogy was delivered by 

Most Rev. Thomas H. McLaughlin, then Vicar-General of the

Newark Diocese. He was buried in the family plot in Holy Name 

Cemetery, Jersey City.

REV. MARTIN WALTER STANTON was born in St. Mary's parish, 

Jersey City, on April 17, 1897. His parents were Martin and Ellen (Joyce) Stanton. He attended the Catholic Institute, Jersey City (1902-1911); St. Peter's Preparatory School and College (1911-1919) where he attained the degree of Bachelor of Arts; Seton Hall Col lege (1919-1920); Immaculate Conception Seminary (1920-1924); 

and Fordham University (1932-1934). He attained the degree of 

A.M. at Seton Hall, and that of Ph.D. at Fordham. He was ordained at Newark, New Jersey, June 14, 1924, by Most Reverend

John Dunn, Auxiliary Bishop of New York, as Bishop O'Connor was then ill.

Father Stanton served as assistant at St. Theresa's Church, Summit (1924-1931); at St. Charles Borromeo's Church, Newark (19311932); and at St. John's Church, Newark (1932-1937). While serving at St. John's, he was Director of the Family Division of the 

Associated Catholic Charities of the Diocese of Newark (1935-

1936); and since December 30, 1936, has been Assistant Division 

Director of the Pontifical Society for the Propagation of the Faith. 

In 1938, he was appointed its Director, succeeding Bishop Griffin.

IV. LAY TRUSTEES OF ST. MARY'S CHURCH 

1864-1938 The Certificate of Incorporation of St. Mary's Church is dated 

August 17, 1864. The incorporators were: Bishop Bayley; Vicar- 

General Moran; Father Senez; and two lay trustees, Patrick Reilly, a marketman, who served until 1886, and Charles N. O'Neill, a lumber dealer, who served until 1876. In that year, Mr. O'Neill withdrew from all duties of a public nature. He was succeeded by 

Henry Carroll, a prosperous master baker, who served until 1888. 

Mr. Patrick Reilly, who died in 1886, was succeeded by his son, John Reilly, who served until 1889. 

Henry Carroll, who resigned, was succeeded by Charles Healy, who served until 1890. The latter was succeeded by James Rafter, a master carpenter, who served until 1918. John Reilly was succeeded, in 1889, by William Ryan, who served until 1895; then Mr. Ryan left Jersey City. He was succeeded by Edward O'Callaghan, M.D., who served until his death in 1899. The latter was succeeded by 

Charles E. Cassidy, a produce merchant, who resigned in 1903, upon moving to another parish. 

Mr. Cassidy was succeeded by Frank McNally, in January, 1903. 

He was a yardmaster in the Pennsylvania Railroad's Harsimus yard. 

He served until his death in 1904. His successor was John Mullins, furniture dealer, who served from 1905 to 1913, when he died. He was succeeded by James J. Ferris, a builder of piers and bridges and other railroad constructions. He died in 1914. 

Mr. Ferris was succeeded by Thomas J. McLaughlin, M.D., whose successor was John J. Corcoran, a master plumber, and now a member of the Board of Elections of Hudson County. William F. Sullivan, now Register of Deeds for Hudson County, succeeded Mr. Rafter, who died in 1918. Mr. Corcoran and Mr. Sullivan are the present lay trustees.

55. Memories of Tony Pastor's famed theatre, Fourteenth Street, New York, were still lively; and the first subway, with its outstanding kiosks on that street had just come into use. St. Mary's new rectory at 246 Second Street, to many eyes was regal, inside and out. A wit started this riddle on its rounds: 

Q. "Why is St. Mary's like Fourteenth Street, New York?"

A. "Both have a subway and a tony pastor." 

Monsignor Ter Woert relished the joke, if he heard it.

CHAPTER X 

1. The information for this sketch comes from Dr. Burke. 

2. The amount would have been larger, had there not been long litigation to which the parties, complainant and defendant, were his trustee, The First Naional Bank of Jersey City, his sister, Mrs. Henry Smith, her representatives, and several beneficiaries (lay and clerical and religious institutions) named under two deeds of trust executed by Monsignor TerWoert, in his declining years. His last will was involved in the proceedings.

6. Jersey Journal, Feb. 15, 1934; parish register. 

7. The fresco is the handiwork of Miss Joan Cunningham, a young artist of 

Syracuse, New York. 

8. The stained glass was furnished by Calvert, Herrick and Riedinger. They have forwarded this information: The stained glass is of American manufacture. It is, however, wholly in the tradition of the antique school which developed with church architecture. In type it is associated with the tradition period of the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries, when the Romanesque school reached its highest development and the Gothic had its firm beginning. Technically the glass is what is known as "pot metal" glass, which, after the details of design have been painted upon it is kiln-treated, to secure a thorough and permanent fusion of paint and glass. 

The pieces of glass (in some of these windows there are three thousand) are set in

NOTES 

245 lead, then framed and put into place. With time, a bloom gathers on the surface of the glass and the windows become more beautiful. 

9. The decorations are the work of Conippo Raggi & Sons, of East Orange, New 

Jersey. The resetting of the main altar and the new freestanding tabernacle were executed by the Rambusch Decorating Company, New York. The Censullo-Burke 

Construction Company, of Union City, New Jersey, was the contractor upon several items of construction. Robert J. Reiley, architect, supervised the improvements.

Mr. Coyle, who was her father, died July 5, 1910, in his sixty-third year.

As a young man he had conducted, after 1874, the business of Michael Brady, an early undertaker in Jersey City. In 1889 he engaged in business upon his own account.

After 1901, the affiliation of Mr. Coyle's family was with St. Peter's, where his boys attended college.
